- [verified] **Intel 4004** — Stack-address mux: effective address counter and refresh counter multiplexed to the address-register decoder. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §III.
- [verified] **Intel 4004** — Address buffer demultiplexed onto the internal bus in three 4-bit slices (A1, A2, A3). Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §III.
- [verified] **Intel 4004** — ADB register supplies one adder operand from the internal bus. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §III.
- [verified] **Intel 4004** — Accumulator shifter implements rotate right and rotate left. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §III.
- [verified] **Intel 4004** — Special ROMs perform code conversion for DAA and KBP instructions. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §III.
- [verified] **Intel 4004** — Condition logic senses ADD = 0, ACC = 0, carry FF and TEST to implement JCN and ISZ. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §III.
- [verified] **Intel 4004** — Instruction register (OPR + OPA) loaded from the internal bus through a multiplexer at M1/M2. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §III.
- [verified] **Intel 4004** — Double-cycle flip-flop set by any of the 5 double-length instructions. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §III.
- [verified] **Intel 4004** — Data-bus output buffers are tri-state ("1", "0", floating); only one buffer drives a line at a time. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973), §II, Figure 2.
- [unverifiable] **Intel 4004** — Primitive-level parameters not published in the manual: refresh/effective-address counter widths, special-ROM dimensions, instruction-decoder structure, cell/transistor counts. Source: Intel MCS-4 User's Manual (1973).

### MOS Technology 6502

#### Down to the primitives: The decode PLA and the timing sequencer — the whole control unit in one array

- **ROM array (the decode PLA)** — The control unit's memory is a mask-programmed ROM, not a RAM: a 130-row by 21-column array in which each row is one opcode-pattern-plus-timing-state combination. A row fires when the instruction-register bits, the cooked group input G and the current timing state T all match its pattern, and raises one of 131 PLA outputs (pla0–pla130) (verified — Visual6502wiki/6507 Decode ROM: "The Decode ROM is a 130x21 bits ROM in the 6502 that is used to decode the instruction and to control various units of the CPU."). The PLA is the only place the chip "knows" what an instruction is: there is no opcode table in software and no microcode store, just pattern rows.
- **Decoder (the G group signals)** — The opcode's low two bits are cooked into three group signals before they reach the ROM: G1 = IR0, G2 = IR1, G3 = !IR0 & !IR1 (verified — Visual6502wiki/6507 Decode ROM). An opcode ending in 11 turns on both G1 and G2, so every G=1 and G=2 row for its pattern fires at once — the mechanism behind the undocumented opcodes: LDA ($AD, G=1) and LDX ($AE, G=2) merge into LAX ($AF) because both rows fire (verified — same source). The decoder is three gates' worth of combinational logic, and it is the whole reason the 6502 has illegal opcodes at all.
- **Register (instruction register + predecode register)** — The opcode byte is latched into the 8-bit instruction register (IR), with inverted IR outputs available to the ROM, and a predecode register holds partially decoded signals. Predecode is the only decoding that sees an instruction during the two cycles before its T0/T1 states arrive (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 State Machine: "None of the decoding (except the predecode) sees the instruction during the two cycles that come before this"). The IR is the PLA's address; the predecode register is what lets the first two fetch cycles overlap the previous instruction's execution.
- **Shift register (the T-state sequencer)** — Timing is a shift register stepping a one-hot state through T0–T5; the PLA takes those states as inputs (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 State Machine: "The PLA has T0,T1,T2,T3,T4,T5 as inputs."). A separate T1F signal — "a 'real' T1" — is output on the SYNC pin. The sequencer also carries the out-of-band states: BRK gets VEC0/VEC1 instead of a T6, and read-modify-write instructions get two extra cycles, SD1 and SD2. The shift register is the chip's clock: nothing happens except in a named T-state.
- **Random logic (the additional-decoding layer)** — Between the PLA and the datapath sits a layer of combinational logic that is neither ROM nor register: the middle third of the die is, in Ken Shirriff's words, "a bunch of logic, mostly to do additional decoding of instructions" (verified — K. Shirriff, "The 6502 CPU's overflow flag explained at the silicon level", righto.com, January 2013). It combines PLA outputs with timing states and internal state (interrupts, RDY, the decimal-carry signals) to produce the roughly 44 datapath control signals (dpc0–dpc43) that actually drive the chip. The PLA decides *which* instruction is running; the random logic decides *when* each of its control actions fires.
- **Latch (control-signal latching)** — Every datapath control signal is latched during phi2, and is set mostly from opcode and timing data but also from some internal state (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath: "All datapath control signals are latched during phi2; they are set mostly from opcode and timing data, but also some internal state."). The latch is what makes the two-phase clock work as a pipeline: phi2 freezes the control for the cycle, phi1 executes it.
- **Adder (the PC incrementer)** — The program counter's incrementer is a 16-bit adder chain with no internal latches of its own: when it increments, the new value is put directly on ADL and ADH (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath: "When incrementing, the new value is put on ADL,ADH because there are no internal latches in the PC incrementer."). The I/PC signal gates whether it increments at all — it peeks at the predecoded next instruction and skips the increment for one-byte opcodes — and the PCLC/PCHC carry signals carry the increment across the low/high byte boundary. The incrementer is a pure combinational adder, not a counter: it recomputes PC+1 from the PC registers each cycle it is asked.
- **Register (address and data latches)** — The address leaves the chip through two 8-bit latches, ABL and ABH, loaded from ADL and ADH during phi1 and driven onto the address pins in that same phi1; the data latch DL is latched during phi2 and put on ADL, ADH or DB on the next phi1; the data output register DOR is latched from DB during phi1 and driven onto the data pins in phi2 on a write (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath). These three registers are the chip's only contact with the outside world: everything else on the die talks to them through the internal buses.
- **Pass transistors / mux (bus interconnect and precharge)** — The four internal buses (SB, DB, ADL, ADH) are joined by pass transistors that connect two buses together when a control signal fires — SB/DB and SB/ADH are bidirectional pass-connects, and DL/ADL, DL/ADH and DL/DB pass-connect the data latch onto the buses (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath, dpc table). And every internal bus is driven high during phi2 as a sort of precharge (verified — same source: "All internal busses (SB, DB, ADL, ADH) are driven high during phi2, as a sort of precharge."). Precharge is why the NMOS 6502 can use small pass transistors instead of full drivers: the bus is already high, and a transistor only has to pull it low.

**Parameter honesty.** Parameter honesty: the sources publish the ROM geometry (130 × 21), the 131 PLA outputs, the ~44 datapath control signals, the T0–T5 states plus T1F, VEC0/VEC1 and SD1/SD2, and the 8-bit instruction register; they publish no PLA transistor count per row, no shift-register latch count, no gate count for the random-logic layer, and no predecode-register width, and this decomposition invents none of them.

#### Down to the primitives: The ALU — two nibble adders with a decimal adjust

- **Mux (ALU input selection)** — The ALU's two operand inputs are selected by muxes, not wired: one side takes two options (SB/ADD or 0/ADD) and the other takes three (nDB/ADD, DB/ADD or ADL/ADD) (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath: "SB/ADD, 0/ADD, nDB/ADD, DB/ADD, ADL/ADD Two options for one side, three for the other."). The nDB input — the inverted data bus — is what makes subtraction possible without a separate subtractor: SBC adds the complement. The muxes are the ALU's only operand steering, and they are why the same adder serves arithmetic, logic and address computation.
- **Adder (two 4-bit nibble adders)** — The sum is computed as a pair of 4-bit additions, not one 8-bit addition: the two operands and the carry-in are added as a pair of nibbles, and the carry-out from bit 3 is the hand-off to the high nibble (verified — Visual6502wiki/6502DecimalMode: "The two operands, and the carry in, are added as a pair of nibbles. The carry-out from bit3 is adjusted in decimal mode, but only for ADC."). Each nibble's carry ripples bit to bit inside the nibble, so the sum settles in two short serial chains rather than one eight-bit chain — and the boundary at bit 3 is exactly where the decimal adjust sits.
- **Logic network (AND / OR / EOR / shift)** — The same operand network that adds also does logic: the operation-select signals ANDS, EORS, ORS, 1/ADDC, SRS, SUMS, DAA and DSA choose the ALU operation (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath: "ANDS, EORS, ORS, 1/ADDC, SRS, SUMS, DAA, DSA Select the ALU operation."). SRS is a logical right shift — there is no separate barrel shifter; shifts are one-bit operations through the ALU, and the rotate-right top bit is handled by not driving it (the ALU always computes a zero there). The NAND and NOR of the two operands' top bits are already available in the ALU for other purposes (verified — K. Shirriff, righto.com, January 2013) — the logic network is shared infrastructure, not a separate logic unit.
- **Comparator (the decimal adjust)** — The decimal adjust is not an adder at all: it is a handful of pulldown transistors gated by the DAA (ADC) and DSA (SBC) control signals. The 2A03 proves the count: removing five transistors from the polysilicon mask disables decimal adjustment entirely — three for ADC (t2750, t2202, t2556) and two for SBC (t1329, t3212) (verified — Visual6502wiki/6502DecimalMode: "With visual6502 and images from Quietust's investigation of the 2A03 we can see that a small number of changes, only to the polysilicon mask, disable the decimal adjustment by removing 5 transistors."). Five transistors is the whole decimal mode: the feature that sold the chip over the 6800 is five pulldowns.
- **Register (the ADD output register)** — The ALU's result is written into the 8-bit ADD register during phi2 and can be used the next cycle (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath: "The ALU output register (ADD) is written during phi2."). Its outputs split: ADD/SB7 is separate from ADD/SB(0-6) because the top bit is handled separately for rotate-right, and ADD/ADL feeds address calculations. Before the result reaches the accumulator, lines 1–3 and 5–7 pass through the decimal adjust, to finish the proper BCD add/subtract result if necessary (verified — same source). ADD is the ALU's only output register, and the decimal adjust sits between it and the accumulator, not inside the adder.
- **Comparator (flag generation)** — The carry-out (ACR) and overflow (AVR) leave the ALU back to the control logic, latched during phi2 and used in phi1 (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath: "The overflow and carry out signals AVR and ACR are output from the ALU back to the control logic, latched during phi2, used in phi1."). Overflow is computed from the top bits of the two operands and the carry out of bit 6 into bit 7 — V = not(((A7 NOR B7) and C6) NOR ((A7 NAND B7) NOR C6)) (verified — K. Shirriff, righto.com, January 2013) — reusing the NAND/NOR the ALU already computes. The decimal carries are picked up at phi2 as well, so the flag logic and the datapath agree on the same cycle boundary.
- **Register (the status register P)** — The processor status lives in its own 8-bit register: P/DB writes the flag values onto the data bus, and the data bus can be read to set the flags — read during phi2, latched into the flag register on the next phi1 (verified — Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath). The D flag that selects decimal mode lives here, which is why the NES CPU could keep the flag settable while the adjust transistors were gone.
- **FSM / controller** — The operation-select signals are raised by the PLA and the timing sequencer at the right phase, and the decimal carries are picked up at phi2 — the ALU itself has no sequencer; it is pure combinational logic plus the ADD register, driven by the control unit's phase discipline. The ALU's "state" is exactly one register (ADD) plus the flags.

**Parameter honesty.** Parameter honesty: the sources publish the input mux widths (2 options on one side, 3 on the other), the two 4-bit nibble adders, the eight operation-select signals, the five decimal-adjust transistors by name, and the AVR/ACR flag signals; they publish no gate-level schematic of the adder, no transistor count for the ALU as a whole, and no width for the predecode register, and this decomposition invents none of them. The gate-level form of the nibble adders is Level-4 work, labelled standard construction there.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — Decode ROM row format: each row matches IR bits plus the cooked group input G plus the timing state T; G1 = IR0, G2 = IR1, G3 = !IR0 & !IR1. Source: Visual6502wiki/6507 Decode ROM (NESdev wiki).
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — Opcode low bits 11 turn on both G1 and G2, so G=1 and G=2 rows fire together — the mechanism behind undocumented opcodes (LAX = LDA + LDX). Source: Visual6502wiki/6507 Decode ROM (NESdev wiki).
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — The PLA takes T0–T5 as timing inputs; a separate T1F signal drives the SYNC pin; BRK uses VEC0/VEC1, RMW instructions add SD1/SD2. Source: Visual6502wiki, 6502 State Machine.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — Predecode is the only decoding that sees an instruction during the two cycles before its T0/T1 states. Source: Visual6502wiki, 6502 State Machine.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — The middle third of the die is random logic doing additional decoding between the PLA and the datapath. Source: K. Shirriff, righto.com, January 2013.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — All datapath control signals are latched during phi2. Source: Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — The PC incrementer has no internal latches; the incremented value is put directly on ADL/ADH. Source: Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — All internal buses (SB, DB, ADL, ADH) are driven high during phi2 as a precharge. Source: Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — ALU input selection: two options on one side (SB/ADD, 0/ADD), three on the other (nDB/ADD, DB/ADD, ADL/ADD). Source: Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — ALU operation selects: ANDS, EORS, ORS, 1/ADDC, SRS, SUMS, DAA, DSA. Source: Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — The ALU output register ADD is written during phi2; its lines pass through the decimal adjust before reaching the accumulator. Source: Visual6502wiki, 6502 datapath.
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — Decimal adjustment is five pulldown transistors: three for ADC (t2750, t2202, t2556), two for SBC (t1329, t3212); removing them disables decimal mode. Source: Visual6502wiki/6502DecimalMode (NESdev wiki).
- [verified] **MOS Technology 6502** — Overflow reuses the ALU's NAND/NOR of the operands' top bits; V = not(((A7 NOR B7) and C6) NOR ((A7 NAND B7) NOR C6)). Source: K. Shirriff, righto.com, January 2013.
- [unverifiable] **MOS Technology 6502** — Primitive-level parameters not published: PLA transistor count per row, T-state shift-register latch count, random-logic gate count, ALU gate-level adder schematic and transistor count, predecode-register width. Sources: visual6502 project pages, righto.com.

### AMD Zen 4

#### Down to the primitives: The op cache and branch predictor — the front end that feeds the machine

- **CAM (the L1 and L2 BTB tag match)** — The branch target buffers are associative lookups: the fetch address is matched against stored tags, and a hit returns the target in a single cycle from the L1 BTB. Zen 4's L1 BTB keeps Zen 3's 1-cycle latency while tracking up to 3,072 branch targets, depending on branch density (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Zen 4's L1 BTB keeps the same 1 cycle latency, but improves capacity. Depending on branch density, Zen 4's L1 BTB can track up to 3072 branch targets, though in practice it'll probably be able to track 1024 to 2048 targets."). The 1.5K entries each hold up to two targets — the second in the same aligned 64-byte line as the first — which is how 1.5K entries cover up to 3,072 targets (per the AMD Software Optimization Guide: "Each BTB entry can hold up to two branches"). The L2 BTB is bigger and faster than Zen 3's: measured at 8,192 entries, up from 6,656, with the lookup penalty cut from three cycles to one (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Zen 4's L2 BTB sees a slightly size increase as well, with capacity going from 6656 entries on Zen 3 to 8192 on Zen 4. More significantly, Zen 4's L2 BTB is faster: Zen 3 suffered a three cycle penalty when it needed a branch target from its L2 BTB. Zen 4 brings that penalty down to just one cycle."). Note the capacity is disputed: AMD's own figures put the L2 BTB at 7K entries, so the wiki keeps 7K and records the 8,192-entry measurement as disputed.
- **CAM (the indirect target array)** — Indirect branches, which can go to many targets, get their own array separate from the main BTBs (verified — Chips and Cheese: "AMD's CPUs have a separate indirect target array for handling branches that go to multiple targets."). Measured tracking holds up to 3,072 targets without significant penalty (verified — same source: "In our testing, Zen 4 seems to be able to track up to 3072 branch targets without significant penalty."). It works as an overriding predictor: if the branch goes where it went last time, the fast main BTBs supply the target and the array stays out of the way. The array's entry count and associativity are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the op cache and the L1 instruction cache)** — The op cache is an associative SRAM array: 64 sets by 12 ways, each set-way entry holding up to 9 macro-ops, for a maximum of 6.75K ops, physically tagged (verified — AMD Software Optimization Guide 57647: "The Op Cache is organized as an associative cache with 64 sets and 12 ways. At each set-way intersection is an entry containing up to 9 macro ops. The maximum capacity of the Op Cache is 6.75 K ops."). It is the front end's working memory: a hit delivers up to 9 macro-ops per cycle with no decode and no L1I access. The miss path's store is the 32 KB, 8-way L1 instruction cache, which refills the op cache through the 4-wide decoder. The op cache's banking and port counts, and the L1I's banking, are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the micro-op queue)** — Between the op cache and rename sits the micro-op queue, which absorbs the op cache's 9-per-cycle bursts and meters ops out at 6 µops per clock (verified — Chips and Cheese: "AMD says the micro-op cache delivers 9 ops per cycle, but throughput is limited by the 6-wide renamer downstream."). The queue's depth is not published, so this decomposition names the primitive and its rate only.
- **FIFO / queue (the reorder buffer)** — The ROB is a 320-entry circular buffer — a FIFO in structure, with in-order retirement at its head. Its capacity is confirmed by independent measurement (verified — Chips and Cheese: "We used a custom test with up to 128 integer adds, 128 floating point adds, 40 stores, and 55 branches (in that order) to confirm AMD's published 320 entry ROB capacity."). Each entry can hold up to 4 NOPs — fused NOP pairs are fused again at rename — which is why a NOP-only test measured a reordering capacity of 1,265 NOPs (verified — same source: "Zen 4's reorder buffer is also special, because each entry can hold up to 4 NOPs."). The ROB's associative lookup structure is not published.
- **Register file (the physical register files and the register alias table)** — Rename draws on three files: 224 integer registers, 192 FP/vector registers widened to 512 bits, and a 68-entry mask file. The register alias table is itself a small multi-ported register file mapping architectural registers to physical ones, and it carries the renamer's zero trick: a MOV or zeroing idiom is eliminated by pointing several architectural registers at one physical register, and a known-zero value is remembered by a bit in the RAT instead of allocating a real integer register (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Eliminated MOVs can be handled by pointing multiple architectural registers to the same physical one, meaning that no extra physical registers have to be allocated for such MOVs. If the renamer knows a register's value has to be zero, it can remember that by setting a bit in the register alias table instead of allocating a real integer register."). Measured, 202 of the 224 integer registers are available to hold speculative results (verified — same source: "We measured 202 integer registers (out of 224) available to hold speculative results, which covers 63% of ROB capacity."). The files' read/write port counts are not published.
- **Counter / stack (the return stack)** — Returns are predicted by a 32-entry return-address stack: a call pushes the return address, a return pops it (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Like prior Zen generations, Zen 4 has a return stack with 32 entries. However, AMD seems to have made improvements so that a single thread can use all 32 entries."). It is a tiny LIFO — a stack-pointer counter plus a 32-deep register array — and it is why call/return pairs predict almost perfectly until the stack overflows.
- **SRAM bank + counter (the direction predictor's pattern-history tables)** — The conditional predictor's two overriding levels are pattern-history tables: SRAM banks indexed by branch history, each entry a saturating counter — the standard construction of a direction predictor (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Like other CPUs in the Zen series, Zen 4's direction predictor has two overriding levels."). Zen 4's L2 level is much larger than Zen 3's and recognises extremely long patterns (verified — same source: "Zen 4 and Zen 3 seem to have similarly capable L1 predictors, but AMD has dramatically beefed up Zen 4's L2 predictor. ... It can recognize extremely long patterns and has enough storage to perform very well, even with a lot of branches in play."). AMD publishes no table sizes, history lengths or algorithm for either level, so this decomposition names the primitive and its role only.
- **Priority encoder / switch arbiter (the schedulers' wakeup and select)** — The back end's schedulers are the select logic: each cycle they wake every queued op whose operands are ready and pick which ones issue, with the FP side split into two triple-port schedulers sharing a large non-scheduling queue (verified — Chips and Cheese: "On the floating point and vector execution side, Zen 3 and Zen 4 retain the large non-scheduling queue found in previous Zen generations, but move from a unified quad-port scheduler to two triple port schedulers."). The integer side shares its scheduling queues between ALU and AGU ports, and the whole layout is unchanged from Zen 3 (verified — same source: "Scheduler and execution pipe layout were not changed from Zen 3."). A scheduling queue has to check every entry every cycle and send an instruction for execution on the same cycle it gets marked ready (verified — same source: "A scheduling queue potentially has to check every entry every cycle, to see if it's ready to execute. To make things even harder, it has to be able to send an instruction for execution on the same cycle it gets marked ready."). Scheduler queue depths and the exact select policy are not published.
- **Mux (the supply-path select)** — The front end's last primitive is a mux, not a store: each cycle the fetch stream comes either from the op cache (hit) or from the L1I-plus-decoder refill path (miss), and the BTB's two-target entries let the predictor cover two taken branches per cycle (per the AMD Software Optimization Guide: "Predicting with BTB pairs allows two fetches to be predicted in one prediction cycle."). The mux radix and the op cache's banking are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** Parameter honesty: the sources publish the op-cache geometry (64 sets × 12 ways, up to 9 macro-ops per entry, 6.75K ops maximum), the L1 BTB's 1-cycle latency and up-to-3,072 targets, the L2 BTB's measured 8,192 entries (disputed against AMD's 7K), the indirect target array's 3,072-target tracking, the 32-entry return stack, the micro-op queue's 6 µops-per-clock rate, the 320-entry ROB (4 NOPs per entry), the 224/192/68 register files, and the two triple-port FP schedulers; they publish no BTB associativity or entry layout, no direction-predictor table sizes, history lengths or algorithm, no op-cache or L1I banking or port counts, no micro-op queue depth, no scheduler queue depths or select policy, and no ROB lookup structure, and this decomposition invents none of them.

#### Down to the primitives: The double-pumped AVX-512 datapath — 512-bit instructions on 256-bit pipes

- **Multiplier array (the FMA multiply stage)** — Each 256-bit FMA pipe's first stage is a multiplier array: four double-precision lanes per pipe, each multiplying 53-bit mantissas (the IEEE 754 double format) into partial products that a reduction tree sums. The array is what makes the pipe a multiply-accumulate unit rather than a plain adder. AMD publishes no multiplier geometry — no Booth encoding, no compressor-tree structure, no partial-product count — so this decomposition names the primitive and its role only; the gate-level form is Level-4 standard construction.
- **Adder tree (the FMA accumulate stage and the FP add pipes)** — The FMA's second stage adds the product into the accumulator, and the two ADD/MUL pipes are adder trees in their own right: FADD/FMUL latency is 3 cycles and FMA latency 4 cycles in the 256-bit forms. The adder tree is also what the double pump reuses: the low half's accumulate and the high half's accumulate run through the same tree on consecutive cycles. The tree's depth and carry structure are not published.
- **Register file (the 192 × 512-bit FP file and the 68-entry mask file)** — Operands live in a 192-entry FP/vector physical register file widened to 512 bits, with a separate 68-entry mask register file holding the per-lane predicates. The widening is the most expensive part of the AVX-512 implementation (per Chips and Cheese: "The most expensive change probably had to do with extending the vector register file to make each register 512-bits wide."). Measured, the mask file supports around 52 renames — fewer than its 68-entry capacity (verified — Chips and Cheese: "With around 52 renames for mask registers, this register file is much smaller than what we see on Skylake-X or Ice Lake."). The files' read/write port counts are not published.
- **Mux (the low/high half-select of the double pump)** — A 512-bit op is split into its low and high 256-bit halves and occupies a pipe for two consecutive cycles; a half-select mux steers the low half in cycle 1 and the high half in cycle 2. The split happens as late as possible — the instruction stays one micro-op through most of the pipeline and is only broken into two 256-bit halves after it enters the execution pipe (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Zen 4 partially breaks this tradition, by keeping instructions that work on 512-bit vectors as one micro-op throughout most of the pipeline."). The mux's exact implementation is not published.
- **Barrel shifter / crossbar (the full-width 512-bit shuffle unit)** — The one exception to double-pumping: a dedicated full-width shuffle unit executes 512-bit shuffles in a single cycle. A shuffle is a cross-lane permutation — a crossbar (or barrel-shifter network) that routes any of the 16 32-bit lanes to any output lane in one pass. Its radix and lane-routing structure are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the 88-entry load queue and 64-entry store queue)** — Feeding the pipes is the load/store unit, behind an 88-entry load queue and a 64-entry store queue. The queues are the ordering structures: loads wait here until their addresses are known and their data returns; stores wait here until retirement, because only known-good data can be committed to cache (per Chips and Cheese: "stores have to stay there until retirement because only known-good data can be committed to cache."). 512-bit stores are the exception to the one-micro-op rule: they are decoded into two micro-ops and consume two store-queue entries (verified — Chips and Cheese: "512-bit stores are an exception to the rule. They're still decoded into two micro-ops, which means they consume two valuable store queue entries."). The queues' associative lookup structure (how a load finds a matching store) is not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 data cache and the L2)** — The load/store unit draws on the 32 KB, 8-way L1 data cache, behind which sits the 1 MB, 8-way L2 at 14 cycles. L1D bandwidth is two 256-bit loads and one 256-bit store per cycle, unchanged from Zen 2 (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Zen 4's L1D can handle two 256-bit loads and one 256-bit store per cycle, which means that vector load/store bandwidth remains unchanged from Zen 2."). A 512-bit vector load therefore takes two cycles on the 256-bit load ports. The caches' banking and port counts are not published.
- **FSM / controller (the FP schedulers and pipe reservation)** — The FP scheduler issues AVX-512 macro-ops when their operands are ready, and reserves the chosen pipe for two consecutive cycles — a small controller FSM whose state is, per pipe, free or busy-for-the-second-half. The FP side uses two triple-port schedulers sharing a large non-scheduling queue. The scheduler's exact state encoding and select policy are not published.
- **Comparator (the per-lane mask evaluation)** — The mask register file's per-lane predicate bits are evaluated against the operation: masked lanes neither compute nor write. The comparator is one AND per lane between the mask bit and the operation enable — the primitive that turns a 512-bit op into a partially-masked one. The mask file's port count and the exact gating structure are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** Parameter honesty: the sources publish the four 256-bit FP pipes (2 FMA/MAC + 2 ADD/MUL) plus the dedicated 512-bit shuffle unit, the 192 × 512-bit FP register file and 68-entry mask file (around 52 mask renames measured), the 88-entry load queue and 64-entry store queue, the 32 KB 8-way L1D and 1 MB 8-way L2 at 14 cycles, the 2× 256-bit loads + 1× 256-bit store per cycle, and the FADD/FMUL 3-cycle and FMA 4-cycle latencies; they publish no multiplier-array geometry, no adder-tree depth or carry structure, no FP register-file port counts, no shuffle-unit crossbar radix, no load/store queue lookup structure, and no scheduler state encoding, and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Op cache organization: 64 sets × 12 ways, up to 9 macro-ops per entry, maximum capacity 6.75K ops, physically tagged. Source: AMD Software Optimization Guide for the AMD Zen4 Microarchitecture, publication 57647, rev 1.01, April 2023.
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — BTB is a two-level structure; each entry holds up to two branches, and BTB pairs allow two fetches to be predicted in one prediction cycle. Source: AMD Software Optimization Guide 57647, §2.8.1.2.
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — L1 BTB keeps 1-cycle latency, tracks up to 3,072 branch targets depending on branch density (in practice 1,024–2,048). Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1: Frontend and Execution Engine".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — L2 BTB measured at 8,192 entries (up from 6,656 on Zen 3); lookup penalty cut from 3 cycles to 1 cycle. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [disputed] **AMD Zen 4** — L2 BTB capacity: AMD/Wikipedia put it at 7K entries; Chips and Cheese measured 8,192 entries. Wiki keeps 7K; no change made.
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Indirect branches get a separate indirect target array; measured tracking holds up to 3,072 targets without significant penalty. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Return stack has 32 entries; on Zen 4 a single thread can use all 32. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Op cache delivers 9 ops per cycle, but throughput is limited by the 6-wide renamer downstream. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Renamer eliminates MOVs and zeroing idioms by pointing multiple architectural registers at one physical register and by setting a zero bit in the register alias table instead of allocating a real integer register; 202 of 224 integer registers measured available for speculative results. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — ROB capacity of 320 entries confirmed by a custom test (128 integer adds, 128 FP adds, 40 stores, 55 branches); each ROB entry can hold up to 4 NOPs. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Scheduler and execution pipe layout unchanged from Zen 3; FP/vector side uses two triple-port schedulers sharing a large non-scheduling queue; a scheduling queue must check every entry every cycle and issue on the same cycle an op is marked ready. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Direction predictor has two overriding levels; Zen 4's L2 predictor is much larger than Zen 3's and recognises extremely long patterns. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — 512-bit instructions are kept as one micro-op through most of the pipeline, split into two 256-bit halves as late as possible. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — 512-bit stores are decoded into two micro-ops and consume two store-queue entries. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Two 256-bit FMA units and four 256-bit ALUs, unchanged since Zen 2; L1D handles two 256-bit loads and one 256-bit store per cycle, unchanged from Zen 2. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [verified] **AMD Zen 4** — Mask register file supports around 52 renames measured, fewer than its 68-entry capacity. Source: Chips and Cheese, "AMD's Zen 4 Part 1".
- [unverifiable] **AMD Zen 4** — Primitive-level parameters not published: BTB associativity and entry layout, direction-predictor table sizes/history lengths/algorithm, op-cache and L1I banking/port counts, micro-op queue depth, scheduler queue depths and select policy, ROB lookup structure, FMA multiplier-array geometry, adder-tree depth/carry structure, FP register-file port counts, shuffle-unit crossbar radix, load/store queue lookup structure, scheduler state encoding. Sources: AMD Hot Chips 35, AMD SOG 57647, Chips and Cheese, mersenneforum teardown.

### DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)

#### Down to the primitives: The tournament branch predictor

- **SRAM bank (the local history table)** — The predictor's per-branch memory: 1,024 entries of 10 bits, indexed by the branch instruction address, each entry holding that branch's last ten outcomes (verified — Kessler, McLellan & Webb, ICCD 1998: "The local history table holds 10 bits of branch history for up to 1024 branches, indexed by the instruction address."). It is the local path's addressable store: a fetch presents a branch PC, the table returns that branch's own 10-bit pattern, and nothing else in the predictor knows per-branch history. Its banking and port counts are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the local prediction table)** — The local path's decision store: 1,024 entries of 3-bit saturating counters, indexed by the 10-bit local history just read out — the history pattern is the address into this table, not the PC (verified — ICCD 1998: "The 21264 uses the 10-bit local history to pick from one of 1024 prediction counters. The local prediction is the most-significant bit of the prediction counter."). The prediction is a single bit read off the counter's top bit, so this bank's output is one bit wide per lookup. Its banking, port counts and read latency are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the global prediction table)** — The global path's decision store: 4,096 entries of 2-bit saturating counters, indexed by the 12-bit global (path) history of the last twelve branches executed anywhere in the program (verified — ICCD 1998: "The global predictor is a 4096 entry table of two-bit saturating counters that is indexed by the global, or path, history of the last twelve branches. The prediction is the most-significant bit of the indexed prediction counter."). Where the local table learns a branch's own rhythm, this table learns correlations between branches. Its banking, port counts and read latency are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the choice prediction table)** — The referee's store: a second 4,096-entry table of 2-bit saturating counters, also indexed by the global history, that learns which of the two predictors to believe in each global context (verified — ICCD 1998: "The chooser array is 4096 two-bit saturating counters."; Kessler, IEEE Micro 1999: "The choice prediction, or chooser, is also a 4,096-entry table of 2-bit prediction counters indexed by the path history."). Its output bit is the select signal for the final prediction mux. Its banking, port counts and read latency are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the path-history silo)** — The speculative global history lives in a silo of branch predictions: it is updated speculatively before a branch retires and backed up on a mispredict, so the machine can rewind the history it guessed wrong (verified — Kessler, IEEE Micro 1999: "The processor maintains path history with a silo of 12 branch predictions. This silo is speculatively updated before a branch retires and is backed up on a mispredict."). Depth is disputed: the earlier ICCD 1998 paper says thirteen ("The 21264 maintains global history with a silo of thirteen branch predictions and the 4096 prediction counters. The silo is backed up and corrected on a mispredict."); the wiki keeps 12, the later journal version's figure, and records the disagreement. The silo's cell implementation and backup mechanism are not published.
- **Counter (the saturating counters)** — The predictor's learning elements: one 3-bit saturating counter per local-history pattern and one 2-bit saturating counter per global-history and choice entry, all trained at branch retirement by saturating addition — increment on taken, decrement on not-taken, clamped at the rails so a counter never wraps (verified — ICCD 1998: "After branches issue and retire the 21264 inserts the true branch direction in the local history table and updates the referenced counter (using saturating addition) to train the correct prediction."). The chooser is the only counter with a training gate: it moves only when the local and global predictions disagreed (verified — ICCD 1998: "If the predictions of the local and global predictor differ, the 21264 updates the selected choice prediction entry to support the correct predictor."). The counter circuits themselves are standard construction, not published per-counter detail.
- **Shift register (the local history update)** — Each local-history entry is a 10-bit shift register: at retirement the branch's true direction shifts in and the oldest outcome shifts out, so a branch that alternates taken/not-taken settles into a repeating 10-bit pattern the local table can then recognise (verified — ICCD 1998: "The local history of the branch will eventually resolve to either 1010101010 or 0101010101 (the alternating pattern of zeroes and ones indicates the success/failure of the branch on alternate invocations). As the branch executes multiple times, it will saturate the prediction counters corresponding to these local history values and make the prediction correct. Any repeating pattern of 10 branch invocations can be trained this way."). The shift register is the per-entry storage form of the local history table's 10-bit rows.
- **Mux (the final prediction select)** — The predictor's last step is a 2:1 mux, not a table: the choice counter's most-significant bit selects the local prediction or the global prediction as the fetch direction (the scheme "dynamically chooses between local and global history to predict the direction of a given branch" — ICCD 1998). The mux itself is standard construction — the paper publishes the choice behavior, not the select circuit — and follows the paper's own convention that every prediction in this design is the most-significant bit of its counter.
- **Comparator (the MSB test and the disagreement gate)** — Two one-bit comparisons run the predictor's logic. First, each prediction is the most-significant bit of its counter — a 1-bit read of the counter's top bit, the primitive that turns a 3-bit or 2-bit confidence value into a taken/not-taken decision (verified — ICCD 1998: "The local prediction is the most-significant bit of the prediction counter."). Second, the chooser's training gate is a disagreement test: the local and global prediction bits are XORed, and the chooser counter moves only when they differ — the standard-circuit form of the paper's "if the predictions of the local and global predictor differ" rule, labelled as construction, not as a published circuit.
- **Pipeline register (the speculative prediction latch)** — The prediction and the speculative history have to survive from the fetch cycle until the branch retires, while the machine speculates through up to 20 branches (verified — ICCD 1998: "The 21264 can speculate through up to 20 branches."). The silo is the storage that carries that speculative state, and its backup-on-mispredict is the restore mechanism; the latch and restore circuitry around it is standard construction, not published.
- **Row decoder (the table address decode)** — Four tables need four decoders: branch-PC bits select 1 of 1,024 local-history rows, the 10-bit local history selects 1 of 1,024 local-counter rows, and the 12-bit global history selects 1 of 4,096 rows in each of the global and choice tables. The decoders are standard SRAM-array construction; Kessler publishes no decoder structure, table banking, or port counts for any of the four tables, and this decomposition invents none of them.
- **Adder (the saturating incrementer)** — Every counter update is a saturating add: a small incrementer/decrementer with clamp logic at the top and bottom rails (verified behavior — ICCD 1998: "updates the referenced counter (using saturating addition)"). There are three widths in play — 3-bit for the local table, 2-bit for the global and choice tables — and the incrementer's circuit form is standard construction, not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The sources publish the four table geometries (1,024 × 10-bit local history, 1,024 × 3-bit local counters, 4,096 × 2-bit global counters, 4,096 × 2-bit choice counters), the 12-branch global history, the path-history silo (12 per IEEE Micro 1999, thirteen per ICCD 1998 — disputed), the saturating-addition training rule, the most-significant-bit prediction convention, the chooser-update-only-on-disagreement rule, the 29-Kbit total predictor storage, the 90–100% prediction accuracy, the SPECfp95/SPECint95 misprediction rates (under 1 and about 11.5 per 1,000 completed instructions), and the up-to-20-branches speculation depth; they publish no table banking, port counts, row-decoder structure, read latency, or mux/comparator transistor implementation, and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Local history table: 1,024 entries × 10 bits, indexed by the branch instruction address. Source: Kessler, McLellan & Webb, "The Alpha 21264 Microprocessor Architecture", ICCD 1998, §2.2.
- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Local prediction table: 1,024 entries of 3-bit saturating counters, indexed by the 10-bit local history; the prediction is the counter's most-significant bit. Source: Kessler et al., ICCD 1998, §2.2.
- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Global prediction table: 4,096 entries of 2-bit saturating counters, indexed by the global/path history of the last twelve branches; the prediction is the most-significant bit of the indexed counter. Source: Kessler et al., ICCD 1998, §2.2.
- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Chooser: 4,096 two-bit saturating counters, indexed by path history; updated only when the local and global predictions differ. Source: Kessler et al., ICCD 1998, §2.2.
- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Training: at branch retirement the true direction is inserted into the local history table and the referenced counter is updated with saturating addition. Source: Kessler et al., ICCD 1998, §2.2.
- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Accuracy: 90–100% prediction accuracy on most simulated applications/benchmarks, better than larger tables of either individual method. Source: Kessler et al., ICCD 1998, §2.2.
- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Predictor storage totals 29 Kbits; fewer than one misprediction per 1,000 completed instructions on SPECfp95, about 11.5 per 1,000 on SPECint95. Source: Hennessy & Patterson, Computer Architecture: A Quantitative Approach, 3rd ed. (secondary).
- [disputed] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Path-history silo depth: ICCD 1998 says thirteen branch predictions; IEEE Micro 1999 says 12. Wiki keeps 12 (the later journal version).
- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — The path-history silo is speculatively updated before a branch retires and backed up on a mispredict. Source: Kessler, "The Alpha 21264 Microprocessor", IEEE Micro, Mar–Apr 1999.
- [verified] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — The 21264 can speculate through up to 20 branches. Source: Kessler et al., ICCD 1998, §2.2.
- [unverifiable] **DEC Alpha 21264 (EV6)** — Primitive-level parameters not published: table banking and port counts, row-decoder structure, read latency, mux/comparator transistor implementation, per-table transistor counts, silo cell implementation and backup mechanism. Sources: Kessler et al., ICCD 1998; Kessler, IEEE Micro 1999.

### Intel Pentium Pro (P6)

#### Down to the primitives: Reorder buffer + reservation station — the pool of pending µops

- **Register file (the retirement register file, RRF)** — The architectural register file, the machine's committed state: when a µop retires, the ROB writes that µop's result into the appropriate RRF entry and notifies the RAT so subsequent renaming can proceed (verified — Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997: "When a uop is retired, the ROB writes that uop's result into the appropriate RRF entry and notifies the RAT of that retirement so that subsequent register renaming can be activated."). The RRF is the file the ROB's speculative state is committed into, and the file a flush reverts to on a fault or mispredict. Its entry count mirrors the architectural state (eight integer registers plus FP and flags state); its port counts and exact width are not published.
- **Register file (the ROB as the rename register file)** — The 40-entry reorder buffer is itself the physical register file: up to 3 µops per cycle are renamed "to a set of 40 physical registers by the Register Alias Table (RAT)" (verified — Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997), and those 40 physical registers are the 40 ROB entries. Each ROB entry is the physical destination of one in-flight µop: it holds the µop, its destination, its result value once produced, and its ready/status bits, so a consumer that finds its operand "present in a ROB entry" reads the value straight out of the ROB. Its banking and port counts are not published.
- **Register file (the register alias table, RAT)** — The rename map: one entry per architectural register, holding the ROB entry index of the most recent writer (plus a valid bit saying whether the value lives in the ROB at all). At rename, each source operand is looked up in the RAT — a hit means the operand's value is (or will be) in that ROB entry, a miss means it still lives in the architectural register file — and each destination is pointed at a fresh ROB entry, eliminating register re-use artifacts (verified — Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997: the RAT "eliminates register re-use artifacts"). On retirement the RAT is notified, so later renames see the committed mapping. The RAT's exact entry count (integer vs FP vs flags) and port counts are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the ROB as a circular queue)** — The ROB is a 40-entry circular queue: µops are allocated at the tail in program order (up to 3 per cycle) and retired from the head in program order (up to 3 per cycle), with head and tail pointers tracking the window (the queue form is standard — University of Washington CSEP548 course slides: "circular queue with head & tail pointers"; "holds 40 'executing' instructions in program order (dispatched but not yet committed)"). Retirement order is reimposed by the hardware itself: "Retirement hardware in the ROB uses uop timestamps to reimpose the original program order on the uops as their results are committed to permanent architectural machine state" (verified — Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997). The queue's head/tail pointer widths and the timestamp field width are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the reservation station as a waiting queue)** — The 20-entry RS is the waiting room: "The renamed uops are queued in the RS where they wait for their source data" (verified — Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997), and they leave only when their operands are data-ready and a functional unit is free (verified — Papworth, IEEE Micro Apr 1996: "the micro-ops wait in a 20-entry reservation station (RS) until all of their operands are data ready and a functional unit is available"). It is a queue with out-of-order exit: any entry can leave next, not just the head, which is what makes the pool a dataflow scheduler rather than a pipeline stage. Its allocation order and free-entry accounting are not published.
- **CAM / associative match (the RS tag comparators)** — The wakeup mechanism. Each RS entry stores operand tags — the ROB entry numbers of the producers of operands not yet available — instead of values, and every cycle each entry compares its tags against the tags broadcast with completing results; a match captures the result into the entry and marks that operand ready (the tag mechanism is the standard Tomasulo form: University of Washington CSEP548 slides — "operand fields in reservation stations point to reorder buffer location"; "results tagged with reorder buffer entry number"; RS entries "snoop on common data bus for missing operands" — secondary). The behaviour it implements is verified in the primary sources: execution units "send the result data back to dependent micro-ops in their reservation stations" (Papworth, IEEE Micro Apr 1996), and a result "is forwarded to subsequent uops that need it" (Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997). Twenty entries × two operand tags means up to forty tag comparisons per broadcast; the comparators' circuit form, the matchline organisation and the number of result buses are not published.
- **Comparator (the retire-head ready check)** — Retirement is a comparison at the ROB head: each entry carries a ready/completed bit, and the retire controller retires the head entries only when they are complete — up to three per cycle — and "examines each completed micro-op for the presence of faults or branch mispredictions, and halts further retirement upon detecting such a discontinuity" (verified — Papworth, IEEE Micro Apr 1996). The halt is what reimposes the sequential fault model: a faulting µop stops the head, the machine flushes the speculative state behind it, and the fault is taken in program order. The comparator circuit itself is standard construction, not published.
- **Mux / demux (operand select and result broadcast)** — Every RS entry's operand path is a mux across the four places source data can come from — "immediates, data bypassed from just-executed uops, data present in a ROB entry, and data residing in architectural registers (such as EAX)" (verified — Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997) — selected by the RAT lookup and the tag-match state. On the writeback side, each completing result is demuxed to its ROB entry and broadcast to all twenty RS entries at once. The mux/demux circuits are standard construction; the number of result buses and their arbitration are not published.
- **Switch arbiter (the RS dispatch arbiter)** — The gate between the waiting pool and the five execution ports: "As many as 5 micro-ops per clock can pass from the reservation station to the various execution units" (verified — Papworth, IEEE Micro Apr 1996). Each cycle the arbiter resolves which ready µops take which of the five ports (two integer, FP, load, store classes) — a ready µop with no free port of its class waits another cycle. The arbitration policy (oldest-ready first, per-port priority, or round-robin) is not published, and this decomposition invents none of it.
- **Priority encoder (oldest-ready selection)** — The arbiter's selection logic, in primitive form: among the ready µops competing for one port, an encoder picks the highest-priority one — in Tomasulo-style schedulers the oldest ready µop, so the oldest waiting work is never starved by younger µops that became ready later. The priority rule is the standard construction of a dispatch arbiter; P6's actual selection policy is not published, and the encoder's width and placement are not claimed.
- **Counter (the ROB head/tail pointers)** — The pointers that make the circular queue work: the tail advances by 0–3 per cycle as µops are allocated, the head advances by 0–3 as they retire, each wrapping modulo 40. By arithmetic from the published 40-entry geometry the pointers are 6 bits wide (2^6 = 64 ≥ 40); that width is arithmetic, not a published figure. The counter circuits are standard construction.
- **Credit counter (the free-entry accounting)** — Allocation is gated on free slots: the front end can rename at most 3 µops per cycle, and only while both the ROB and the RS have free entries; when the RS fills with µops queued behind one miss, the wide front end stalls (the Level-2 bottleneck-out). The accounting is a credit counter — decremented on allocation, incremented on retirement (ROB) or dispatch (RS) — the standard flow-control primitive for a bounded queue. Its exact implementation and stall-signalling path are not published.
- **FSM / controller (the retirement controller)** — The state machine that walks the ROB head: it checks each head µop's completion and fault/mispredict status, commits up to 3 results per cycle into the RRF, notifies the RAT, and on a discontinuity halts retirement and flushes the speculative state — "flush all or part of its state on detection of a mispredicted branch" (verified — Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997). The controller is the block that turns the ROB from a buffer into the machine's precise-interrupt mechanism. Its state encoding and cycle-by-cycle sequencing are standard construction, not published.
- **Pipeline register (the RS entry latches and the ROB writeback latch)** — A µop's opcode, operand values and tags, and control bits sit latched in its RS entry from allocation until dispatch, and its result is latched into its ROB entry on writeback; the rename/allocate, dispatch, execute and retire stages hand the µop along through these registers. The latch count and the pipeline-register placement between the stages are standard construction, not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The sources publish the governing geometries — 40-entry ROB, 20-entry RS, 40 physical registers via the RAT, up to 3 µops renamed and retired per cycle, up to 5 µops dispatched per cycle, the four operand sources (immediates, bypassed results, ROB entries, architectural registers), the result-forwarding and in-order-retirement behaviour, the 14-stage pipeline split (8 front-end / 3 out-of-order core / 3 retirement), and the timestamp-based retirement ordering; they publish no RS per-entry comparator or cell implementation, no dispatch arbitration policy, no RAT entry count or port counts, no ROB storage-cell form, no result-bus count, no pointer widths, and no latch counts, and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — Rename: the sources and destinations of up to 3 uops are renamed every cycle to a set of 40 physical registers by the RAT, which eliminates register re-use artifacts, and are forwarded to the 20-entry RS and the 40-entry ROB. Source: Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997, §2.
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — RS wait: renamed uops are queued in the RS where they wait for source data — immediates, data bypassed from just-executed uops, data present in a ROB entry, or data in architectural registers. Source: Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997, §2.
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — Dispatch and writeback: µops wait in the 20-entry RS until operands are data-ready and a functional unit is available; up to 5 µops per clock pass from the RS to the execution units, which send result data back to dependent µops in the RS and store the result in the ROB. Source: Papworth, IEEE Micro, Apr 1996.
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — ROB retire: the ROB stores µops in original program order and retires up to three per clock into the RRF; it examines each completed µop for faults or branch mispredictions and halts further retirement on a discontinuity. Source: Papworth, IEEE Micro, Apr 1996.
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — Retirement mechanics: retirement hardware uses uop timestamps to reimpose program order; when a uop retires the ROB writes its result into the appropriate RRF entry and notifies the RAT; up to 3 uops retire per clock; on a mispredict the machine flushes all or part of its state. Source: Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997, §2.
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — Result forwarding: once a uop has executed and its destination data has been produced, that result data is forwarded to subsequent uops that need it, and the uop becomes a candidate for retirement. Source: Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997, §2.
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — Pipeline: 14-stage pipeline decoding 3 instructions per clock; in-order front end 8 stages, out-of-order core 3 stages, in-order retirement logic 3 stages. Source: Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997, §2.
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — RS tags are ROB entry numbers: operand fields in reservation stations point to a reorder buffer location and results are tagged with the reorder buffer entry number. Source: University of Washington CSEP548 course slides (secondary).
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — ROB is a circular queue with head and tail pointers, holding 40 executing instructions in program order (dispatched but not yet committed). Source: University of Washington CSEP548 course slides (secondary).
- [verified] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — RS entries snoop on the common data bus for missing operands. Source: University of Washington CSEP548 course slides (secondary).
- [unverifiable] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — Primitive-level parameters not published: RS per-entry comparator and cell implementation, dispatch arbitration policy and priority-encoder placement, RAT entry count and port counts, ROB storage-cell form and banking, result-bus count and arbitration, ROB pointer and timestamp field widths, RS/RAT latch counts. Sources: Papworth, IEEE Micro, Apr 1996; Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997; Colwell & Steck, ISSCC 1995.

### Intel Golden Cove

#### Down to the primitives: The 6-wide front end — 32-byte fetch, six decoders and the 4K µop cache

- **CAM (the BTB tag match)** — The branch target buffer is an associative lookup: the fetch address is matched against stored branch tags, and a hit returns the target without touching the instruction cache. Intel puts the total at 12K entries (verified — Intel Architecture Day 2021, recorded in the Level-2 run), and independent measurement found it behaves like three levels, with a 1-cycle penalty for missing one level and hitting the next (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove's BTB seems to have three levels. Missing in one level and hitting in the next incurs a 1 cycle penalty, which is quite fast compared to AMD's 3 cycle penalty for a L2 BTB hit."). The per-level sizes, the tag width and the associativity of each level are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 instruction cache)** — The cold path's store: a 32 KB instruction cache feeding the six decoders at 32 bytes per cycle (verified — Chips and Cheese: "To feed those decoders, Intel has increased L1 instruction cache bandwidth to 32 bytes/cycle."). Its banking, set associativity and port counts are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the µop cache / decoded-stream buffer)** — The hot path's store: an associative SRAM array of 4K entries holding already-decoded µops, delivering up to 8 per cycle (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove gets a bigger 4K entry uop cache, up from 2.25K in Sunny Cove and 1.5K in Skylake. Uop cache bandwidth has improved too. Golden Cove can fetch 8 micro-ops per cycle from that cache, matching Zen in that respect."). Intel's own fact sheet calls it "eight-wide µop cache (up from six)". Its banking, set associativity and the µop format's field layout are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the µop queue)** — Between the two supply paths and rename sits the 144-entry µop queue (72 per thread under SMT), which absorbs the µop cache's 8-per-cycle bursts and meters µops out at 6 per cycle into rename (depth verified — Intel Architecture Day 2021, recorded in the Level-2 run). It is also the loop-unrolling trick: the queue unrolls small loops inside itself, giving two taken branches per cycle (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove can unroll loops within its uop queue, giving it an incredible throughput of two taken branches per cycle. In a sense, Intel's uop queue acts like a tiny trace cache, reducing branch overhead for small loops without needing loop unrolling from the developer or compiler."). The queue's internal organisation — how the unrolled iterations are stored and replayed — is not published.
- **FSM / controller (the six decoders and their clock gating)** — The cold path's translators: six instruction decoders cracking x86 bytes into µops, with the old complex-decoder arrangement removed (verified — Intel Architecture Day 2021, recorded in the Level-2 run). The decoder is a power-gated controller: it sits clock-gated about 80% of the time while the µop cache feeds the core. The decoders' internal structure — length-decoding, prefix handling, µop-cracking tables — is not published.
- **CAM (the L1 instruction TLB tag match)** — Fetch addresses are translated through a 256-entry L1 instruction TLB for 4K pages (32 entries for 2M/4M huge pages) (verified — Intel Architecture Day 2021, recorded in the Level-2 run). A TLB is a fully associative CAM: the virtual page number is matched against stored tags and a hit returns the physical page number in the fetch cycle. The TLB's associativity, replacement policy and miss-handling structure are not published.
- **SRAM bank + counter (the direction predictor's pattern-history tables)** — The predictor's learning memory: SRAM banks indexed by branch history, each entry a saturating counter — the standard construction of a direction predictor. Intel says an on-chip ML model grows or shrinks the BTB to fit the running workload (verified — Intel Architecture Day 2021, recorded in the Level-2 run), and measurement shows the predictor recognises much longer patterns than Skylake's but suffers once a repeating pattern exceeds 48 branches (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove suffers once the pattern length exceeds 48."). Intel publishes no table sizes, history lengths, component count or algorithm for the predictor, so this decomposition names the primitive and its role only.
- **Counter / stack (the return stack)** — Returns are predicted by a return-address stack: a call pushes the return address, a return pops it. Golden Cove's behaviour here is odd — measurement found no clear capacity step even at 128 call/return pairs, and returns get slow once calls nest more than two deep (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove's return prediction behavior is strange. There's no clear jump up, even when we increased the number of call/return pairs to 128. But when calls go more than two deep, Golden Cove is rather slow at handling returns."). The stack's depth is not published, and this decomposition does not infer one from the missing step.
- **Mux (the supply-path select)** — The front end's last primitive is a mux, not a store: each cycle the fetch stream comes either from the µop cache (hit) or from the L1I-plus-decoder refill path (miss), and the queue's loop unrolling lets the predictor cover two taken branches per cycle. The mux radix and the µop cache's way-selection logic are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The sources publish the BTB's 12K-entry total and three-level, 1-cycle-per-level behaviour; the L1I's 32 bytes/cycle fetch bandwidth; the µop cache's 4K entries and 8 µops/cycle; the µop queue's 144-entry depth (72 per thread) and two-taken-branches-per-cycle loop unrolling; the iTLB's 256/32 entries; and the pattern-length-48 predictor ceiling; they publish no BTB per-level sizes, tag widths or associativity; no direction-predictor table sizes, history lengths or algorithm; no return-stack depth; no µop-cache or L1I banking, associativity or port counts; and no decoder internals — and this decomposition invents none of them.

#### Down to the primitives: The 12-port back end — unified scheduler, 512-entry ROB and the 3-load memory subsystem

- **FIFO / queue (the 512-entry reorder buffer)** — The ROB is a 512-entry circular queue: µops are allocated at the tail in program order (up to 6 per cycle) and retired from the head in program order (up to 6 per cycle), with head and tail pointers tracking the window (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove's reorder buffer is a staggering 45% larger than Sunny Cove's. This structure tracks all micro-ops in the backend until they're retired (exceptions checked for and results made final)."). It is the machine's in-order commit mechanism and its precise-interrupt anchor. The ROB's storage-cell form, banking, head/tail pointer widths and the retire-head ready-check circuit are not published.
- **Register file (the integer, FP/vector and flags files)** — Rename draws on three files: a 280-entry integer file (248 usable plus 32), a 332-entry FP/vector file (300 plus 32), and a 248-entry flags file (verified — Chips and Cheese capacity table: "Integer Register File | 280 (~248+32)"; "Floating Point/Vector Register File | 332 (300+32)"; "Flags Register File | 248"). The integer file's read-port floor is measured, not published: five ALUs at two inputs each need ten read ports, and all five ALUs were observed running at once (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Feeding five ALUs with two inputs each would require ten integer register file ports. ... But we were able to use all five integer ALUs at once, so the integer register file has at least ten read ports."). The vector file is a measured estimate too — probably eight read ports and three write ports (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove's vector register file probably has eight read ports and three write ports."). The files' exact port counts, banking and cell form are not published by Intel.
- **Register file (the register alias table and move/zero elimination)** — The rename map: one entry per architectural register, holding the physical register of the most recent writer. Move elimination (6 per cycle) and zeroing-idiom elimination work through this table — a MOV or zeroing idiom is eliminated by remapping architectural registers in the table instead of executing an ALU op (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove carries that forward, but increases rename width to 6 instructions per cycle. Golden Cove's move elimination is thus a match for Zen 3's"; and "Golden Cove can recognize all of the zeroing idioms we tested. And it doesn't just recognize these as special cases of independence – it eliminates them so they don't need an ALU port to execute."). The RAT's entry count, port counts and the exact elimination circuits are not published; the remap-in-the-table mechanism is standard construction.
- **Priority encoder / switch arbiter (the unified scheduler's wakeup and select)** — The back end's select logic: a unified scheduler for integer and FP/vector math (205 entries total, measured) with the address-generation units on a separate scheduling queue (verified — Chips and Cheese capacity table: "Total Scheduler Capacity | 205"; and "Like previous Intel designs, Golden Cove uses a unified scheduler for math operations, and puts address generation units (AGUs) on a separate scheduling queue."). Each cycle the scheduler wakes every queued µop whose operands are ready and picks which ones issue to the 12 ports. The per-class queue depths, the wakeup-compare structure and the exact select policy are not published.
- **Adder tree (the fast FP adders and their bypass)** — Floating-point addition runs on dedicated fast adders on ports 1 and 5: 3-cycle latency with a 2-cycle bypass for back-to-back adds (verified — Intel Architecture Day 2021, recorded in the Level-2 run), and measurement confirms the 2-cycle back-to-back figure at over 5 GHz (verified — Chips and Cheese: "But more impressively, Golden Cove can do floating point additions with two cycle latency. We've seen that before in low frequency designs like VIA's Nano. But Golden Cove is doing this at over 5 GHz."). The adder is an adder tree over the mantissas with exponent alignment and normalisation around it; its depth, carry structure and the bypass mux's exact form are not published.
- **Multiplier array (the FMA multiply stage and the vector integer multiplier)** — Each FP pipe's multiply stage is a multiplier array: mantissa multiplication reduced through a compressor tree into the accumulate stage. The latencies are measured: FP multiply takes 4 cycles, against 3 on Zen 3 (verified — Chips and Cheese: "The situation flips with floating point multiplies, where Golden Cove has 4 cycle latency compared to 3 cycles on Zen 3."), and the vector integer multiplier is slow — 10 cycles for packed 32-bit integers (verified — Chips and Cheese: "However, Golden Cove's vector integer multiplier has 10 cycle latency (with packed 32-bit integers), just like older Intel CPUs."). No multiplier geometry — no Booth encoding, compressor-tree structure or partial-product count — is published.
- **Mux (the bypass/forwarding network)** — Results forward to waiting dependents over a bypass network: a result produced on one port is muxed straight into a dependent µop's operand latch on a later port, which is what makes the 2-cycle FP-add bypass possible. The network's mux radix, the number of forwarding buses and the per-port operand-select logic are not published.
- **Counter (the branch order buffer)** — In-flight branches are tracked in a 128-entry branch order buffer (verified — Chips and Cheese capacity table: "Branch Order Buffer | 128"). It is the checkpoint store: on a mispredict the machine walks it to find the youngest in-flight branch and flush back to it. Its entry format, checkpointing mechanism and port counts are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the 192-entry load queue and 114-entry store queue)** — The memory ordering structures: loads wait in the 192-entry queue until their addresses are known and their data returns; stores wait in the 114-entry queue until retirement, because only known-good data can be committed to cache (verified — Chips and Cheese capacity table: "Load Queue | 192"; "Store Queue | 114"). The queues' associative lookup structure — how a load finds a matching store — is not published.
- **CAM (the store-to-load forwarding match)** — The load queue's correctness check is an associative match: each load's address is compared against the addresses of all in-flight stores, and a match forwards the store's data to the load instead of reading the cache. The comparison is the CAM primitive — one comparator per store-queue entry, matched in parallel — the standard construction of store-to-load forwarding; Intel publishes no match structure, entry width or forwarding latency for Golden Cove, and this decomposition invents none of them.
- **SRAM bank (the 48 KB L1 data cache)** — The load/store unit draws on a 48 KB L1 data cache at 5 cycles of latency, delivering three 256-bit loads per cycle (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Looking at a single thread bandwidth on Golden Cove, we see three 256-bit vector loads per cycle from L1D."), with a 64-byte-per-cycle path to the L2 behind it (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Golden Cove also has a wider 64 byte per cycle path to L2, while AMD uses a narrower 32 byte per cycle path."). The cache's banking, set associativity and port counts are not published.
- **Adder (the three load and two store AGUs)** — Address generation is addition: base + index × scale + displacement, computed by an adder per AGU. Golden Cove has three load AGUs and two store AGUs — a record for x86 (verified — Chips and Cheese: "Intel's P-Core can generate addresses for three loads and two stores per cycle, again setting a record for x86 CPUs."). The AGUs' adder widths, carry structure and the scheduler entries that feed them are not published.
- **FSM / controller (the AGU scheduler, fill buffer and superqueue credit accounting)** — Misses are tracked by credit counters: a 16-entry fill buffer for L1D misses and a 48-entry superqueue for outstanding L2 misses (verified — Chips and Cheese capacity table: "Fill Buffer | 16"; "Superqueue | 48"). Each miss consumes a credit; the credit returns when the line fills. The AGU scheduler — a separate queue from the unified math scheduler — dispatches the address computations. The accounting circuits and the AGU scheduler's queue depth and select policy are not published.
- **CAM + counter (the data TLB and the page walkers)** — Load/store addresses are translated through a 96-entry data TLB — a CAM matching the virtual page number against stored tags — backed by four page walkers that refill it on a miss (verified — Intel Architecture Day 2021, recorded in the Level-2 run). The DTLB's associativity, replacement policy and the walkers' state machines are not published.
- **Multiplier array (AMX — server Golden Cove only)** — The server variant adds AMX, a matrix engine doing 2,048 int8 operations per cycle (verified — Intel Architecture Day 2021, recorded in the Level-2 run). Its core primitive is a multiplier array over tile registers — a grid of multiply-accumulate cells fed by the tile file — but Intel publishes no tile geometry, array dimensions or accumulator structure for AMX on Golden Cove, so this decomposition names the primitive and its rate only.

**Parameter honesty.** The sources publish the ROB's 512 entries; the integer (280), FP/vector (332) and flags (248) register files; the measured integer-RF floor of ten read ports and the estimated vector-RF eight read / three write ports; the 205-entry total scheduler capacity; the 128-entry branch order buffer; the 192-entry load queue and 114-entry store queue; the 16-entry fill buffer and 48-entry superqueue; the L1D's three 256-bit loads per cycle and 64-byte-per-cycle L2 path; the three load and two store AGUs; the FP-add 2-cycle back-to-back latency, the FP-multiply 4-cycle latency and the vector-integer-multiply 10-cycle latency; and AMX's 2,048 int8 ops/cycle (server). They publish no RAT entry counts or port counts; no scheduler per-class queue depths, wakeup structure or select policy; no ROB storage-cell form, banking or pointer widths; no FMA multiplier geometry or adder-tree depth; no bypass-network mux radix or forwarding-bus count; no load/store queue associative-lookup structure; and no AMX tile geometry — and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Five integer execution units, the most in any x86 CPU to date. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
