- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Vector register file probably has eight read ports and three write ports (measured estimate, hedged with "probably" in the source). Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Unified scheduler for math operations, with address generation units on a separate scheduling queue. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Total scheduler capacity 205 entries (measured), up from 160 in Sunny Cove. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021, capacity table.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Branch order buffer holds 128 entries, up from 96 in Sunny Cove. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021, capacity table.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Flags register file holds 248 entries, unchanged from Sunny Cove. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021, capacity table.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Fill buffer holds 16 entries (up from 12); superqueue holds 48 outstanding L2 misses (up from 32). Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021, capacity table.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Three loads and two stores per cycle from the AGUs, a record for x86 CPUs. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — L1D delivers three 256-bit vector loads per cycle, with a 64-byte-per-cycle path to L2. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — FP multiply latency is 4 cycles, against 3 cycles on Zen 3. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Vector integer multiplier latency is 10 cycles for packed 32-bit integers. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Return prediction: no clear capacity step even at 128 call/return pairs, and returns are slow once calls nest more than two deep. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — Branch throughput of about 128 branches at roughly 1 per cycle. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [verified] **Intel Golden Cove** — The µop queue acts like a tiny trace cache, unrolling loops inside itself to give two taken branches per cycle. Source: Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.
- [unverifiable] **Intel Golden Cove** — Primitive-level parameters not published: BTB per-level sizes/tag widths/associativity, direction-predictor table sizes/history lengths/algorithm, return-stack depth, µop-cache/L1I/L1D banking/associativity/port counts, decoder internals, RAT entry counts/port counts, scheduler per-class queue depths/wakeup structure/select policy, ROB storage-cell form/banking/pointer widths, FMA multiplier geometry/adder-tree depth, bypass-network mux radix/forwarding-bus count, load/store queue associative-lookup structure, AMX tile geometry. Sources: Intel Architecture Day 2021 (via AnandTech and WikiChip Fuse), Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.

### Arm Neoverse V2

#### Down to the primitives: The MOP-cache front end — 1,536-entry L0 MOP cache, 6-wide decode and the TAGE predictor

- **SRAM bank (the L0 MOP cache)** — The hot path's store: 1,536 macro-ops in a 4-way skewed-associative array (verified — Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, Table 7-1: "Macro-operation (MOP) cache | 1536 macro-operations | 4-way skewed associative"; and §3.1: "A 1536-entry, 4-way skewed associative L0 Macro-OP (MOP) cache, which contains decoded and optimized instructions for higher performance."). It is a Level 0 instruction cache in the fetch stages (verified — Table 7-1: "Level 0 instruction cache working in the fetch stages of the pipeline to improve throughput and latency"), virtually indexed and virtually tagged, behaving as physically indexed and physically tagged (verified — Table 7-1: "Virtually Indexed, Virtually Tagged (VIVT) behaving as Physically Indexed, Physically Tagged (PIPT)"). Skewed associativity means each way hashes the fetch address differently, so a loop's MOPs spread across ways instead of colliding in one — the standard construction of a skewed cache; the hash functions, the way-selection muxes and the array's banking and port counts are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 instruction cache)** — The cold path's store: 64 KB, 4-way set associative, 64-byte lines (verified — TRM §3.1: "A 64KB, 4-way set associative L1 instruction cache with 64-byte cache lines."), virtually indexed and physically tagged, behaving as physically indexed and physically tagged, and always protected with parity (verified — Table 7-1: "Virtually Indexed, Physically Tagged (VIPT) behaving as Physically Indexed, Physically Tagged (PIPT)" and "Always protected with parity"). Replacement is pseudo-LRU (verified — Table 7-1: "Pseudo-Least Recently Used (LRU) cache replacement policy for L1"). Its banking, port counts and read latency are not published.
- **Counter (the MOP-cache replacement state)** — The MOP cache replaces on a Not Recently Used policy, not LRU (verified — Table 7-1: "Not Recently Used (NRU) replacement policy" for the L0 MOP-cache). NRU is one bit of recency state per entry — set on use, cleared in bulk — the cheapest replacement primitive that still keeps hot loops resident; the bit's exact update rule and the set/way victim selection are not published.
- **CAM (the fully associative L1 instruction TLB)** — Fetch addresses are translated through a fully associative TLB with native support for 4 KB, 16 KB, 64 KB and 2 MB pages (verified — TRM §3.1: "A fully associative L1 instruction Translation Lookaside Buffer (TLB) with native support for 4KB, 16KB, 64KB, and 2MB page sizes."). A fully associative TLB is a CAM: the fetch page number is compared against every stored tag in parallel and a hit returns the physical page in the fetch cycle. Its entry count, replacement policy and the TLB match process internals are not published.
- **SRAM bank + counter (the TAGE direction predictor)** — The predictor's learning memory: "A branch direction predictor that uses the previous branch history" (verified — TRM §7.3), and Arm says the TAGE tables doubled against V1 (verified — Arm community blog, recorded in the Level-2 run). TAGE is a family of tagged tables of saturating counters indexed by branch histories of different lengths, so a short-history table answers easy branches and long-history tables answer correlated ones; the tables are SRAM banks of counters and the tag is the comparator's match key. Arm publishes no table count, history lengths, counter widths or tag widths for V2, so this decomposition names the primitive and its role only.
- **CAM (the branch target buffer)** — The target store: "A Branch Target Buffer (BTB) holding the branch target address of previously observed taken branches" (verified — TRM §7.3), ten times larger than in V1 (verified — Arm community blog, recorded in the Level-2 run). The BTB is an associative lookup: the fetch address is matched against stored branch tags and a hit returns the target without touching the instruction cache. Its entry count, tag width and associativity are not published.
- **Counter / stack (the return stack)** — Returns are predicted by "The return stack, a stack of nested subroutine return addresses" (verified — TRM §7.3), which "stores the address and instruction set state. This address is equal to the link register value stored in X30 in AArch64 state." (verified — TRM §7.3). A BL or BLR pushes, a RET pops; the stack is a last-in-first-out counter with a push/pop pointer. Its depth is not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the fetch queue)** — Between prediction and decode sits the fetch queue, doubled in size against V1 (verified — Arm community blog, recorded in the Level-2 run). It absorbs fetch bursts and meters instructions into the decoders, and it is the buffer a mispredict flush discards — which is why the predictor hardware grew faster than the decoders. Its depth is not published.
- **FSM / controller (the six decoders)** — The cold path's translators: "The instruction decode unit decodes AArch64 instructions into internal format." (verified — TRM §3.1), six decoders wide (verified — Wikipedia, Cortex-X3, recorded in the Level-2 run). Each decoder is a combinational controller turning a fixed-length 4-byte AArch64 instruction into MOPs; because the ISA is fixed-length there is no variable-length pre-decode stage to widen. The decoders' internal structure — field extraction, cracking tables, fused-instruction handling — is not published.
- **Mux (the supply-path select)** — The front end's last primitive is a mux, not a store: each cycle the rename stage is fed either from the MOP cache (hit) or from the L1I-plus-decoder refill path (miss), and the two paths never both drive rename at once. The mux radix and the MOP cache's way-selection logic are not published.
- **Register file (the rename map)** — "The register rename unit performs register renaming to facilitate out-of-order execution and dispatches decoded instructions to various issue queues." (verified — TRM §3.1). The rename map is a register file: one entry per architectural register holding the physical register of the most recent writer, read 8-wide per cycle at dispatch. Its entry count, port counts and the exact elimination circuits are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the per-class issue queues)** — The waiting room: "The instruction issue unit controls when the decoded instructions are dispatched to the execution pipelines. It includes issue queues for storing instructions pending dispatch to execution pipelines." (verified — TRM §3.1). One queue per execution class, each a FIFO with out-of-order exit — any entry can leave next, not just the head, which is what makes the pool a scheduler rather than a pipeline stage. The per-class queue depths and the issue arbitration policy are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the 320-entry reorder buffer)** — The in-order commit mechanism: a 320-entry circular queue (verified — Wikipedia, Cortex-X3, recorded in the Level-2 run), allocated at the tail in program order (up to 8 per cycle) and retired from the head in program order, with head and tail pointers tracking the window. The ROB's storage-cell form, banking, pointer widths and the retire-head ready-check circuit are not published.
- **Comparator (the skewed-associative way match)** — Each MOP-cache way compares its stored tag against the fetch address after its own hash, so a hit is four parallel tag comparisons plus a 4:1 way mux — the comparator is what turns "skewed" from a layout idea into a lookup. The tag width, the hash functions and the comparator circuit are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The sources publish the MOP cache's 1,536 entries, 4-way skewed associativity, VIVT-behaving-as-PIPT indexing and NRU replacement; the L1I's 64 KB, 4-way, 64-byte lines, VIPT-behaving-as-PIPT indexing, parity protection and pseudo-LRU replacement; the I-TLB's fully associative form and four page sizes; the predictor's five components (BTB, history-based direction predictor, return stack, static predictor, indirect predictor); the return stack's X30 push/pop rule; the fetch queue's 2× growth, the BTB's 10× growth and the TAGE tables' 2× growth against V1; the six decoders, 8-wide rename/dispatch and 320-entry ROB of the X3 generation; and the issue queues' existence and role. They publish no MOP-cache or L1I banking, port counts or read latency; no TAGE table count, history lengths, counter widths or tag widths; no BTB entry count, tag width or associativity; no return-stack depth; no fetch-queue depth; no decoder internals; no rename-map entry counts or port counts; no issue-queue depths or arbitration policy; and no ROB storage-cell form, banking or pointer widths — and this decomposition invents none of them.

#### Down to the primitives: The platform fabric — CPU bridge, DSU-110 and the CMN-700 mesh with its distributed system cache

- **FIFO / queue + synchronizer (the asynchronous CPU bridge)** — The clock-domain crossing: "In a cluster, there is one CPU bridge between each Neoverse V2 core and the DSU-110. The CPU bridge controls buffering and synchronization between the core and the DSU-110." (verified — TRM §3.1), and "The CPU bridge is asynchronous to allow different frequency, power, and area implementation points for each core. You can configure the CPU bridge to run synchronously without affecting the other interfaces such as debug and trace which are always asynchronous." (verified — TRM §3.1). The bridge is an asynchronous FIFO with synchronizers: requests are queued on the core side, crossed into the cluster domain, and released when the receiving side is ready — the standard construction of an async bridge; its FIFO depths and synchronizer implementation are not published. The core's clock itself is gated inside the bridge (verified — TRM §4: "Each Neoverse V2 core has a single clock domain and receives a single clock input. This clock input is gated by an architectural clock gate in the CPU bridge.").
- **FSM / controller (the DSU-110 interface manager)** — "The DSU-110 manages all Neoverse V2 core external interfaces to the System on Chip (SoC)." (verified — TRM §3.2). It is the cluster-side controller the bridge talks to: power policy, debug and trace, and the memory-system interface all pass through it, so it is the single choke point between one core and the rest of the SoC. The DSU-110's internal structure is not published in the sources read for this run.
- **Crossbar (the mesh crosspoint, XP)** — The mesh's router: "The Crosspoint (XP) is a switch or router logic module. It is the fundamental component building block of the CMN-700 transport mechanism." (verified — CMN-700 TRM §3.1.1.1), and "The CMN-700 mesh interconnect is built using a set of XP modules. The XP modules are arranged in a two-dimensional rectangular mesh topology." (verified — §3.1.1.1). Each XP carries four CHI channels — Request, Response, Snoop, Data — and up to four mesh ports plus device ports: interior XPs take 4 mesh ports and 2 device ports, edge XPs 3 and 3, corner XPs 2 and 4 (verified — §3.1.1.1). The mesh tops out at 144 XPs in a 12 × 12 grid, each addressed by (X,Y) coordinates (verified — §3.1.1.1: "The maximum size for the CMN-700 mesh is 144 XPs arranged in a 12 × 12 grid. Each XP in the grid is referenced using an (X,Y) coordinate system."). The XP's per-port arbitration policy and buffer depths are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the receive flit buffers)** — Each CHI port buffers incoming flits in 2–4 receive buffers (default 3) (verified — CMN-700 TRM, Table 2-6: "RXBUF_NUM Number of receive flit buffers | 2-4 (3)"), sized against the credit round trip: "The minimum value of 2 corresponds to a credit return latency of one cycle in the interconnect and one cycle in the RN-F or SN-F." (verified — Table 2-6). The buffers are the flow-control shock absorbers — a port with too few cannot sustain full link bandwidth, which is why the count is tied to the credit latency. Per-channel buffer allocation beyond the count is not published.
- **Credit counter (the link-layer credits)** — The mesh's flow control is credited end to end (verified — CMN-700 TRM §2.2: "Credited end-to-end protocol-layer flow-control with a retry once mechanism for flexible bandwidth and resource allocation"), one credit per flit: "A flow-control mechanism by which the transmitting device uses link layer credits to send CHI flits – one credit per flit. In turn, the receiving device sends these credits back to the transmitting device, one at a time, when it is done processing each flit to allow for subsequent flit transfers." (verified — §3.6). Each device is given 4 link credits per channel by default (verified — Table 2-14: "REQLCRD_MAX_COUNT_DEV Max number of Link Credits on REQ channel given to DEV_1-15 (4)", with the same 4 for RSP, DAT and SNP). The round trip sets the buffer floor: "A transmitting device must be able to send flits continuously in a pipelined fashion without stalling because of insufficient link layer credits from the receiving device. This requirement ensures that the system can achieve the full link bandwidth." (verified — §3.6).
- **Pipeline register (the credited slices — MCS / AMCS / DCS / CCS)** — Timing-closure registers with credit accounting: "Credited Slices enable synchronous but higher latency communication at any point in the system." (verified — §3.1.1.14). Mesh Credited Slices sit between XPs — 0 to 4 per XP-XP link in each direction — "optional register slices that can help timing closure in a CMN-700 system" (verified — §3.1.1.14.1); Asynchronous Mesh Credited Slices "perform clock domain crossing between two asynchronous mesh clock domains" (verified — §3.1.1.14.2); Device Credited Slices sit on device-to-XP links, 0 to 4 per link. A credited slice is a pipeline register plus a credit counter: it holds a flit for a cycle and returns the credit only when the flit moves on. The exact slice depths per link in a given system are configuration, not published constants.
- **SRAM bank (the HN-F system-level cache slice)** — Each home node carries a slice of the distributed last-level cache: "The System Level Cache (SLC) is a last-level cache. The SLC allocation policy is exclusive for data lines, except where sharing patterns are detected and pseudo-inclusive for code lines, as indicated by the RN-Fs." (verified — §3.1.1.3). Per-HN-F SLC is configurable from 0K to 4M in 12- or 16-way sets (verified — Table 2-8: "SLC_SIZE Size of system cache per HN-F node", 0K–4M with "SLC_NUM_WAYS Number of ways in the system level cache, set to 12 for 384K, 1.5M, 3M SLC size", otherwise 16). Tag and data RAM latencies are configurable at 1–3 cycles (verified — Table 2-8: "SLC_TAG_RAM_LATENCY Latency of system cache and snoop filter tag RAM" and "SLC_DATA_RAM_LATENCY Latency of system cache data RAM", each 1–3). The slice's banking, port counts and the SLC's replacement policy are not published.
- **CAM (the snoop filter tag RAM)** — The coherence directory: "The Snoop Filter (SF) tracks cachelines that are present in the RN-Fs. It reduces snoop traffic in the system by favoring directed snoops over snoop broadcasts when possible. This approach substantially reduces the snoop response traffic that might otherwise be required." (verified — §3.1.1.3). The filter is an associative tag RAM — each entry records which request nodes hold a line, so a snoop is addressed to exactly those nodes — with up to 1,024 MB of tag RAM across up to 128 partitions, one per HN-F (verified — §2.3: "SF up to 1024MB of tag RAM for increased coherency scalability consisting of up to 128 partitions (one per HN-F)"). Per-HN-F tag RAM runs 512K to 16M (verified — Table 2-8: "SF_SIZE = (32K * (2^SF_SIZE_PARAM) * SF_NUM_WAYS)", with "SF_NUM_WAYS = 16: 512K, 1M, 2M, 4M, 8M" and "SF_NUM_WAYS = 32: 1M, 2M, 4M, 8M, 16M"). The sizing rule is published too: "The optimal total SF size is twice the total exclusive cache size for all RN-Fs." (verified — §2.5.1). The tag RAM's associativity, lookup latency and the RN-F tracking vector's width beyond the published parameters are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the Snoop Filter Eviction Queue)** — When the filter must drop a tracked line it does not drop it on the floor: the "SnoopFilter Eviction Queue, is a Buffer that holds pending Snoop Filter (SF) evictions." (verified — CMN-700 TRM, PMU chapter). The queue drains by forcing back-invalidations, so an eviction is a queued transaction, not a lost record. Its depth is not published.
- **FSM / controller (the combined PoS/PoC)** — The ordering engine: "The combined Point-of-Serialization/Point-of-Coherency (PoS/PoC) is responsible for the ordering of all memory requests sent to the HN-F. Ordering includes serialization of multiple outstanding requests and actions to the same line, and request ordering as required by the RN-F." (verified — §3.1.1.3). The PoS/PoC is a state machine over the Point-of-Coherency Queue, whose tracker holds 16–128 entries (default 32) (verified — Table 2-8: "NUM_ENTRIES_POCQ Number of entries in the POCQ tracker | 16, 24, 32, 48, 64, 80, 96, 128 (32)"). The queue's entry format and the serialisation state machine's encoding are not published.
- **Comparator (the System Address Map decode)** — Routing is address comparison: "In CHI, each byte of address space is assigned to a single Home Node (HN). That HN is responsible for handling all memory transactions that are associated with that address." (verified — §2.5.1), and the map is "A programmable System Address Map (SAM)" (verified — §2.3). Each request's address is compared against the SAM's ranges to find its owning HN-F — the comparator that turns an address into a mesh destination. The SAM's table size, range count and lookup latency are not published.
- **Switch arbiter (the QoS regulator)** — The mesh arbitrates by class, not just by arrival: "QoS regulation for shaping traffic profiles" and "Configurable QoS override to transactions targeting specific memory regions" (verified — §2.3). The regulator is the arbiter that lets latency-sensitive traffic jump the queue and throttles bulk traffic — the primitive that keeps one hot HN-F from starving the mesh. The QoS levels, the regulation algorithm and the per-port arbitration policy are not published.
- **Counter (the CMN-700 PMU)** — The mesh watches itself: "A Performance Monitoring Unit (PMU) to count performance-related events" (verified — §2.3). The counters are the only window into hop latency, credit stalls and snoop-filter hit rate at runtime — the snoop-filter efficiency metric is computed from them. The event list and counter widths are not published in the material read for this run.
- **Comparator + FSM (MESI and the exclusive monitor at the core boundary)** — The core-side half of coherence: "To maintain data coherency between multiple cores, the Neoverse V2 core uses the Modified Exclusive Shared Invalid (MESI) protocol." (verified — V2 TRM §8.1), and "The Neoverse V2 core includes an internal exclusive monitor with a 2-state, open and exclusive state machine that manages Load-Exclusive and Store-Exclusive accesses and Clear-Exclusive (CLREX) instructions." (verified — V2 TRM §8.3). Each L1 line carries a 2-bit MESI state compared on every snoop, and the exclusive monitor is a 2-state FSM guarding load/store-exclusive pairs — the two primitives that let atomics cross the bridge as near atomics in the L1 or far atomics in the L3. The MESI state encoding and the monitor's transition table beyond the two named states are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The sources publish the CPU bridge's one-per-core, asynchronous, buffering-and-synchronising form and its internal clock gate; the DSU-110's role as manager of all core external interfaces; the XP's router role, its 4/3/2 mesh-port and 2/3/4 device-port counts by position, its four CHI channels and the 144-XP 12 × 12 maximum; the receive flit buffers' 2–4 count (default 3) and their credit-latency sizing rule; the credited end-to-end flow control with its retry-once mechanism, one credit per flit, 4 default link credits per channel per device, and the no-stall full-bandwidth condition; the credited slices' kinds and 0–4 counts per link; the HN-F's SLC + PoS/PoC + SF composition, the SLC's exclusive-for-data / pseudo-inclusive-for-code policy, its 0K–4M per-HN-F size in 12 or 16 ways and 1–3-cycle tag/data RAM latencies; the snoop filter's directed-snoop role, its up-to-1,024 MB tag RAM in up to 128 partitions, its 512K–16M per-HN-F sizing and its 2×-exclusive-cache sizing rule; the Snoop Filter Eviction Queue's existence; the PoS/PoC's ordering role and its 16–128-entry POCQ tracker; the SAM's per-byte home-node assignment; the QoS regulation and override; the PMU's event counting; and the core's MESI protocol and 2-state exclusive monitor. They publish no CPU-bridge FIFO depths or synchronizer implementation; no DSU-110 internals; no XP arbitration policy or per-port buffer depths; no per-channel flit-buffer allocation; no credited-slice depths for a given system; no SLC banking, port counts or replacement policy; no snoop-filter associativity, lookup latency or tracking-vector width beyond the published parameters; no SEQ depth; no POCQ entry format or PoS/PoC state encoding; no SAM table size, range count or lookup latency; no QoS levels or regulation algorithm; and no PMU event list or counter widths — and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — L1I is VIPT behaving as PIPT, parity-protected; MOP cache is VIVT behaving as PIPT and works as a Level 0 instruction cache in the fetch stages. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, Table 7-1.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — L1I replacement is pseudo-LRU; MOP-cache replacement is NRU. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, Table 7-1.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Branch prediction hardware comprises a BTB, a history-based direction predictor, a return stack, a static predictor and an indirect predictor. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, §7.3.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Return stack stores the address and instruction-set state, equal to the X30 link-register value; BL/BLR push, RET pops. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, §7.3.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — The instruction issue unit includes issue queues for storing instructions pending dispatch to execution pipelines. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, §3.1.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Each core's clock input is gated by an architectural clock gate in the CPU bridge. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, §4.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — The DSU-110 manages all Neoverse V2 core external interfaces to the SoC. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, §3.2.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Coherency between cores uses the MESI protocol. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, §8.1.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — The core includes an internal exclusive monitor with a 2-state, open and exclusive state machine for load/store-exclusive accesses. Source: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM, §8.3.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — The CMN-700 Crosspoint is a switch/router module, the fundamental building block of the mesh transport; interior XPs have 4 mesh + 2 device ports, edge XPs 3 + 3, corner XPs 2 + 4; the mesh tops out at 144 XPs in a 12 × 12 grid. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, §3.1.1.1.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — CMN-700 uses credited end-to-end protocol-layer flow control with a retry-once mechanism. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, §2.2.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Link-layer credit flow control: one credit per flit, credits returned one at a time as flits are processed; a transmitter must be able to send continuously without stalling on credits to reach full link bandwidth. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, §3.6.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Receive flit buffers number 2–4 per port (default 3); the minimum of 2 matches a credit return latency of one cycle in the interconnect plus one cycle in the RN-F or SN-F. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, Table 2-6.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Each device is given 4 link credits per channel by default (REQ, RSP, DAT, SNP). Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, Table 2-14.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Each HN-F holds an SLC slice, a combined PoS/PoC and a snoop filter; the SLC is exclusive for data lines and pseudo-inclusive for code lines. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, §3.1.1.3.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — The snoop filter tracks cachelines present in the RN-Fs and favours directed snoops over broadcasts. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, §3.1.1.3.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Optimal total snoop-filter size is twice the total exclusive cache size for all RN-Fs. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, §2.5.1.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Per-HN-F SLC is 0K–4M in 12 or 16 ways; per-HN-F snoop-filter tag RAM is 512K–16M; the PoC queue tracker holds 16–128 entries (default 32). Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, Table 2-8.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Credited slices enable synchronous but higher-latency communication anywhere in the system; mesh credited slices run 0–4 per XP-XP link, and asynchronous mesh credited slices perform clock-domain crossing. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, §3.1.1.14.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — In CHI each byte of address space is assigned to a single home node, which handles all memory transactions for that address; the map is a programmable System Address Map. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, §2.5.1 and §2.3.
- [verified] **Arm Neoverse V2** — The Snoop Filter Eviction Queue is a buffer holding pending snoop-filter evictions. Source: Arm CMN-700 TRM, PMU chapter.
- [unverifiable] **Arm Neoverse V2** — Primitive-level parameters not published: MOP-cache/L1I banking/port counts/read latency/hash functions, TAGE table count/history lengths/counter widths/tag widths, BTB entry count/tag width/associativity, return-stack depth, fetch-queue depth, decoder internals, rename-map entry counts/port counts, issue-queue depths/arbitration policy, ROB storage-cell form/banking/pointer widths, CPU-bridge FIFO depths/synchronizer implementation, DSU-110 internals, XP arbitration policy/per-port buffer depths, per-channel flit-buffer allocation, credited-slice depths per system, SLC banking/port counts/replacement policy, snoop-filter associativity/lookup latency/tracking-vector width, SEQ depth, POCQ entry format/PoS-PoC state encoding, SAM table size/range count/lookup latency, QoS levels/regulation algorithm, PMU event list/counter widths. Sources: Arm Neoverse V2 TRM (102375_0001_02_en, Issue 02); Arm CMN-700 TRM (102308_0307_08_en, Issue 08).

### Arm Cortex-X4

#### Down to the primitives: The 10-wide decode front end — eight decoders, no MOP cache

- **SRAM bank (the L1 instruction cache)** — The fetch store: 64 KB, 4-way set associative, 64-byte lines (verified — Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, L1 instruction memory system: "A 64KB, 4-way set associative L1 instruction cache with 64-byte cache lines."), physically indexed and physically tagged (verified — TRM Issue 06, dedicated L1 instruction page). That geometry is 256 sets with an 8-bit set index (arithmetic from the published geometry). Data cache ECC is an optional configurable feature (verified — TRM Issue 06, dedicated L1 instruction page). The replacement policy, banking, port counts and read latency are not published.
- **CAM (the fully associative L1 instruction TLB)** — Fetch addresses are translated through a fully associative TLB with native support for 4 KB, 16 KB, 64 KB and 2 MB page sizes (verified — TRM Issue 06, L1 instruction memory system). A fully associative TLB is a CAM: the fetch page number is compared against every stored tag in parallel and a hit returns the physical page in the fetch cycle. Its entry count, replacement policy and the number of parallel comparators are not published.
- **SRAM bank + counter (the dynamic branch predictor)** — The TRM names only a dynamic branch predictor in the L1 instruction memory system (verified — TRM Issue 06); no table count, history length, counter width, tag width or BTB entry count is published for X4. The predictor's learning memory is SRAM banks of saturating counters and its target store is an associative lookup — the standard construction of a dynamic predictor — but this decomposition names the primitives and their roles only, because Arm publishes no X4 predictor parameters.
- **FSM / controller (the eight decoders)** — The TRM describes the decode unit only as decoding AArch64 instructions into internal format (verified — TRM Issue 06, Instruction decode); the eight-decoder count and the 10-MOP-per-cycle dispatch width come from secondary sources (Wikipedia, Cortex-X4, citing WikiChip Fuse; HWCooling — recorded in the Level-2 run). Each decoder is a combinational controller turning a fixed-length 4-byte AArch64 instruction into MOPs; a complex instruction cracks into two MOPs, which is how eight decoders sustain 10-wide dispatch. The decoders' internal structure — field extraction, cracking tables, fused-instruction handling — is not published.
- **Register file (the rename map)** — The TRM states that the register rename unit performs register renaming to facilitate out-of-order execution and dispatches decoded instructions to various issue queues (verified — TRM Issue 06, Register rename). The rename map is a register file: one entry per architectural register holding the physical register of the most recent writer, read and written up to 10-wide per cycle at dispatch (arithmetic from the published 10-wide dispatch width). Its entry count, port counts and the free-list structure are not published.
- **Counter / FIFO (the physical-register free list)** — Standard construction: a queue of free physical-register numbers, one popped per renamed destination and one pushed per retired instruction, so rename never stalls while free registers exist. Its depth is not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the per-class issue queues)** — The TRM states that the instruction issue unit controls when decoded instructions are dispatched to the execution pipelines and that it includes issue queues for storing instructions pending dispatch to execution pipelines (verified — TRM Issue 06, Instruction issue: "It includes issue queues for storing instructions pending dispatch to execution pipelines."). One queue per execution class, each a FIFO with out-of-order exit — any entry can leave next, not just the head, which is what makes the pool a scheduler rather than a pipeline stage. The per-class queue names beyond the execution classes, the depths and the issue arbitration policy are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the 384-entry reorder buffer)** — The in-order commit mechanism: a 384-entry circular queue holding up to 768 MOPs with fused pairs (verified — Wikipedia, Cortex-X4, recorded in the Level-2 run). The TRM itself never names a reorder buffer — a within-document search of Issue 06 for "reorder buffer" returns no results — so the ROB's existence and size rest on the secondary sources, not on Arm's manual. Allocated at the tail in program order (up to 10 per cycle) and retired from the head in program order, with head and tail pointers tracking the window; 9-bit pointers (arithmetic from 384 entries). The ROB's storage-cell form, banking and the retire-head ready-check circuit are not published.
- **Mux (the dispatch steering network)** — Standard construction: a 10-wide dispatch steers each MOP to its class's issue queue through a mux network, one select per MOP per cycle. The mux radix and the per-cycle steering arbitration are not published.
- **Pipeline register (the 10-stage front end)** — Standard construction: pipeline registers between fetch, decode, rename and dispatch hold the instruction and its MOPs for a cycle each, and a mispredict flush clears them all. The 10-stage depth and the uniform 10-cycle mispredict penalty are published (HWCooling, recorded in the Level-2 run); the register widths and the flush/bypass wiring are not.
- **The missing primitive (the deleted MOP cache)** — The Level-2 decision, restated at the primitive level: X3's 1,536-entry MOP cache — an SRAM bank of decoded MOPs plus its tag comparators, way muxes and hit/miss select — is simply absent in X4. Zero entries means zero tag array, zero way-select mux and zero hit/miss timing split: every instruction takes the same decode path. The deletion is the primitive-level reason the front end is uniform, and it is also why the decoder and rename primitives work harder — they re-decode every iteration of every hot loop.

**Parameter honesty.** The sources publish the L1I's 64 KB, 4-way, 64-byte lines, physically-indexed-physically-tagged indexing and optional ECC; the I-TLB's fully associative form and four page sizes; the predictor's existence as a dynamic branch predictor; the decode unit's role and the eight-decoder / 10-wide dispatch figures (secondary sources); the rename unit's role and dispatch to issue queues; the issue queues' existence and role; the ROB's 384 entries / 768 MOPs (secondary sources); and the 10-stage pipeline with its uniform 10-cycle mispredict penalty. They publish no L1I replacement policy, banking, port counts or read latency; no I-TLB entry count or replacement policy; no predictor table count, history lengths, counter widths, tag widths or BTB entry count; no decoder internals; no rename-map entry counts, port counts or free-list depth; no issue-queue depths, per-class names or arbitration policy; no ROB storage-cell form, banking or retire-head circuit; no dispatch mux radix; and no pipeline-register widths — and this decomposition invents none of them.

#### Down to the primitives: The 21-port back end — eight integer ALUs and the 384-entry window

- **FIFO / queue + CAM (the per-class issue queues and their wakeup)** — The back end's half of the issue queues: each queued entry waits until its operands are ready, and readiness is a parallel tag compare — every entry's source tags are compared against the broadcast result tags each cycle, a CAM-style compare per entry, and the select logic is a priority encoder / arbiter picking the oldest ready entry per port. The queue depths, the number of wakeup comparators per entry and the arbitration policy are not published.
- **Register file (the integer physical register file)** — A multi-ported register file feeding the eight ALUs, three branch units and four AGUs; its entry count and port counts are not published by Arm for X4. The L1 data cache's interface to the integer execute pipeline is published separately (see the L1D bullet): 4x64-bit read paths and 4x64-bit write paths (verified — TRM Issue 06, L1 data memory system, Table 1) — those are cache-interface widths, not register-file ports, and this section does not conflate them.
- **Adder tree (the six simple integer ALUs)** — Each simple ALU is a 64-bit adder (carry-lookahead: an XOR/AND/OR gate tree computing carries in parallel instead of rippling them) plus a logic unit (a mux tree selecting AND/OR/XOR/NOT) plus a comparator for the flag-setting operations. Single-cycle latency is published (HWCooling / Android Authority, recorded in the Level-2 run); the adder's carry-tree depth and the logic unit's mux radix are not.
- **Multiplier array + adder tree (the two MAC units)** — The ALU/MAC and ALU/MAC/DIV units add a multiplier array to the adder: a Wallace/Dadda tree of full adders reducing the partial products, then a final carry-propagate adder — the standard construction of a 64-bit multiplier. The upgrade from X3's single MAC to two MACs is published (Wikipedia / Android Authority, recorded in the Level-2 run); the multiplier's array geometry and pipeline depth are not.
- **FSM / controller + subtractor (the divide unit)** — The ALU/MAC/DIV's divider is an iterative state machine driving a subtractor over multiple cycles — the standard construction of an integer divider. Its iteration count and radix are not published.
- **Comparator + adder (the three branch units)** — The TRM states that the branch execute unit resolves branches, including the branch predictions, and performs branch target calculation (verified — TRM Issue 06, Branch execute). Each branch unit is a comparator (the condition test) plus a target adder (PC + offset). Three units is published (Wikipedia / HWCooling, recorded in the Level-2 run); the comparator width beyond the 64-bit datapath and the target adder's carry structure are not.
- **Adder (the four address-generation units)** — Each AGU is a base-plus-offset adder producing the effective address for a load or store; four AGUs sustain up to four memory operations per cycle (verified — Wikipedia / Android Authority, recorded in the Level-2 run). The adder width beyond the 64-bit address and the AGU-to-cache port mapping are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the load and store queues)** — The TRM states that the load/store unit executes load and store instructions, includes load and store queues and supports multiple outstanding accesses to the L1 cache and L2 memory system (verified — TRM Issue 06, Load/store execute). The queues hold in-flight memory operations and their addresses; their depths, the store-to-load forwarding comparators' count and the outstanding-access limit are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 data cache)** — 64 KB, 4-way set associative, 64-byte lines (verified — TRM Issue 06, L1 data memory system), virtually indexed and physically tagged, behaving as physically indexed and physically tagged, and always protected with ECC (verified — TRM Issue 06, dedicated L1 data page, Table 1). Replacement is Re-Reference Interval Prediction, RRIP (verified — same table). That geometry is 256 sets with an 8-bit set index (arithmetic). Banking, port counts beyond the published interface widths and read latency are not published.
- **CAM (the fully associative L1 data TLB)** — Fully associative, with native support for 4 KB, 16 KB and 64 KB page sizes and 2 MB and 512 MB block sizes (verified — TRM Issue 06, L1 data memory system). Its entry count, replacement policy and the match process internals are not published.
- **Multiplier array + adder tree + barrel shifter (the four 128-bit vector pipes)** — The TRM states that the vector execute unit performs Advanced SIMD and floating-point operations and executes the Scalable Vector Extension and SVE2 instructions (verified — TRM Issue 06, Vector execute). Each of the four 128-bit pipes is a multiplier array plus an adder tree plus a barrel shifter (a mux tree shifting by any amount in one cycle) plus a mux network selecting the operation result; four 128-bit pipes is published (Wikipedia, recorded in the Level-2 run), and floating-point divide and square root are now pipelined (verified — Android Authority, recorded in the Level-2 run). The per-pipe lane split beyond the 128-bit width, the multiplier geometry and the divide/sqrt iteration counts are not published. The vector side's cache interface is published: 3x128-bit read paths and 2x128-bit write paths (verified — TRM Issue 06, L1 data memory system, Table 1).
- **SRAM bank (the private L2 cache)** — Private to the core, 8-way set associative, configurable to 512 KB, 1 MB or 2 MB (verified — TRM Issue 06, L2 memory system), with 64-byte lines and physically indexed, physically tagged indexing (verified — TRM Issue 06, dedicated L2 page), divided into 4 banks (verified — TRM Issue 06, dedicated L2 page: "The L2 cache is divided into 4 banks."), with a dynamic biased replacement policy (verified — TRM Issue 06, dedicated L2 page) and optional ECC (verified — TRM Issue 06, dedicated L2 page). Sets: 1,024 at 512 KB, 2,048 at 1 MB, 4,096 at 2 MB (arithmetic from the published geometry). The CHI interface to the DSU-120 is 256 bits wide (verified — TRM Issue 06, dedicated L2 page: "The CHI interface is 256 bits wide."). Per-bank port counts, tag/data RAM latencies and the replacement policy's bias table are not published.
- **FIFO / queue + synchronizer (the asynchronous CPU bridge)** — The TRM states that the L2 memory system is connected to the DSU-120 through an asynchronous CPU bridge (verified — TRM Issue 06, L2 memory system). Standard construction: an asynchronous FIFO with synchronizers crossing the core/cluster clock domains; its FIFO depths and synchronizer implementation are not published.
- **FSM / controller + counter (the temporal prefetcher)** — The L1 temporal data prefetcher (verified — Android Authority, recorded in the Level-2 run) is a controller watching the load stream for repeating address patterns, with a small table of past addresses and strides — an SRAM bank of counters. Its table size, history depth and prefetch distance are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The sources publish the issue queues' existence and role; the L1D cache-interface widths (4x64-bit read / 4x64-bit write for integer execute, 3x128-bit read / 2x128-bit write for vector); the six single-cycle ALUs and the two MAC units (secondary sources); the three branch units and the branch unit's target-calculation role; the four AGUs and the four-memory-ops-per-cycle rate; the load and store queues' existence and multiple outstanding accesses; the L1D's 64 KB, 4-way, 64-byte lines, VIPT-behaving-as-PIPT indexing, always-on ECC and RRIP replacement; the D-TLB's fully associative form and its page/block sizes; the four 128-bit vector pipes, their SVE/SVE2 role and the pipelined FP divide/square root; the L2's 8-way associativity, 512 KB / 1 MB / 2 MB options, 64-byte lines, PIPT indexing, 4 banks, dynamic biased replacement, optional ECC and 256-bit CHI interface; the asynchronous CPU bridge; and the temporal prefetcher's existence. They publish no issue-queue depths, wakeup-comparator counts or arbitration policy; no integer register-file entry count or port counts; no ALU carry-tree depth, logic-unit mux radix, multiplier geometry, pipeline depth, divider radix or iteration count; no branch comparator width or target-adder carry structure; no AGU adder width or port mapping; no load/store queue depths, forwarding-comparator counts or outstanding-access limit; no L1D banking, port counts beyond the interface widths or read latency; no D-TLB entry count or replacement policy; no vector per-pipe lane split, multiplier geometry or divide/sqrt iteration counts; no L2 per-bank port counts, tag/data RAM latencies or replacement bias table; no CPU-bridge FIFO depths or synchronizer implementation; and no prefetcher table size, history depth or prefetch distance — and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — L1 instruction cache is 64 KB, 4-way set associative, 64-byte lines, physically indexed and physically tagged. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06 (102484_0003_06_en), L1 instruction memory system.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — L1 instruction cache ECC is an optional configurable feature. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, dedicated L1 instruction page.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — L1 data cache is 64 KB, 4-way set associative, 64-byte lines, VIPT behaving as PIPT, always ECC-protected, with RRIP replacement. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, dedicated L1 data page, Table 1.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — L1 data cache interface widths: 4x64-bit read paths and 4x64-bit write paths for the integer execute pipeline; 3x128-bit read paths and 2x128-bit write paths for the vector execute pipeline. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, L1 data memory system, Table 1.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — The L2 cache is divided into 4 banks. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, dedicated L2 page.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — The L2 cache uses a dynamic biased replacement policy. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, dedicated L2 page.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — The L2 cache uses the CHI interface to communicate with the DSU-120; the CHI interface is 256 bits wide. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, dedicated L2 page.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — The L2 cache has 64-byte lines and is physically indexed and physically tagged. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, dedicated L2 page.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — The instruction issue unit includes issue queues for storing instructions pending dispatch to execution pipelines. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, Instruction issue.
- [verified] **Arm Cortex-X4** — The load/store unit includes load and store queues and supports multiple outstanding accesses to the L1 cache and L2 memory system. Source: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM, Issue 06, Load/store execute.
- [unverifiable] **Arm Cortex-X4** — Primitive-level parameters not published: decoder count, rename/dispatch width, ROB size and per-class execution-port counts are not stated in the TRM (a within-document search of Issue 06 for "reorder buffer" returns no results; the figures used in this wiki rest on secondary sources recorded at Level-2); issue-queue depths, per-class queue names and arbitration policy; TLB entry counts and replacement policies; predictor table count, history lengths, counter widths, tag widths and BTB entry count; rename-map entry counts, port counts and free-list depth; ROB storage-cell form, banking and retire-head circuit; dispatch mux radix; pipeline-register widths; integer register-file entry count and port counts; ALU carry-tree depth, logic-unit mux radix, multiplier geometry, pipeline depth, divider radix and iteration count; branch comparator width and target-adder carry structure; AGU adder width and port mapping; load/store queue depths, forwarding-comparator counts and outstanding-access limit; L1I replacement policy, banking, port counts and read latency; L1D banking, port counts beyond the interface widths and read latency; D-TLB entry count and replacement policy; vector per-pipe lane split, multiplier geometry and divide/sqrt iteration counts; L2 per-bank port counts, tag/data RAM latencies and replacement bias table; CPU-bridge FIFO depths and synchronizer implementation; prefetcher table size, history depth and prefetch distance. Sources: Arm Cortex-X4 TRM (102484_0003_06_en, Issue 06, read live this run); Wikipedia, "ARM Cortex-X4"; Android Authority, "Arm Cortex-X4, A720, and A520: 2024 smartphone CPUs deep dive"; HWCooling, "ARM unveils record-breaking Cortex-X4 core with eight ALUs" (secondary figures recorded at Level-2).

### Fujitsu A64FX

#### Down to the primitives: The Core Memory Group — a NUMA node as the unit of design

The CMG decomposes into five primitive families: the crossbar that joins thirteen cores to one L2; the L2's own banks, tags and in-flight queues; the memory access controller (MAC) and its HBM2 stack; the ring interface and on-chip directory that bind the four CMGs; and the per-core leaves — L1 caches, TLBs, reservation stations, register files and prefetch queues — that hang off the crossbar. Fujitsu's own Microarchitecture Manual (v1.4, 2021) publishes the queue depths and bus widths below, which is unusually generous for a vendor manual; where it stops, this section says so.

- **Crossbar (the CMG X-bar)** — A mux/demux fabric plus a switch arbiter joining 13 cores to the shared L2. Published bus widths (verified — Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, Table 9-1, Bus Throughput): L2 to L1D 64 bytes/cycle per core, L1D to L2 32 bytes/cycle per core, L2 to L1D 512 bytes/cycle per CMG, L1D to L2 256 bytes/cycle per CMG. The CMG aggregate (512 bytes/cycle) is below the thirteen cores' combined peak demand (13 × 64 = 832 bytes/cycle, arithmetic), so the X-bar arbitrates — the crossbar, not the L2 banks, is the CMG's shared choke point. The arbiter's policy and the mux radix are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L2 data array)** — 8 MiB per CMG, 16-way set associative, 256-byte lines (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-3, L2 Cache Specifications: "Capacity 8 MiB", "Association method 16-way set associative", "Line size 256 bytes"). Two-bank configuration with physical addresses interleaved at bit[8] (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.2: "The L2 cache has a two-bank configuration, and physical addresses are interleaved at bit[8]."), so each bank is 4 MiB (arithmetic). The index is hashed to spread conflicts (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.2: "The L2 cache hashes the indexes to mitigate index conflicts between processes.") through the published formula index<10:0> = ((PA<36:34> xor PA<32:30> xor PA<31:29> xor PA<27:25> xor PA<23:21>) << 8) xor PA<18:8> — an 11-bit index, hence 2,048 sets (arithmetic). Bank port counts, the tag/data RAM latencies and the bank-internal row/column organisation are not published.
- **SRAM bank + comparator (the L2 tag array)** — Physically indexed, physically tagged (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-3: "Index and tag Physical index and physical tag (PIPT)"), MESI protocol (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-3: "Protocol MESI state"), writeback. Each lookup compares the incoming tag against all 16 ways in parallel — sixteen comparators per lookup (arithmetic from the 16-way geometry) — and hit latency is 37 to 47 cycles depending on the core's position relative to the cache (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.2: "Since the access latency of the L2 cache varies depending on the positional relationship between the processor core and the cache, the latency at cache hit is 37 to 47 cycles."). The tag's stored width is not stated separately from the published index formula, and the tag array's ECC/duplicate protection bits are not itemised.
- **FIFO / queue (the L2 move-in buffer, MIB)** — 256 entries per CMG (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-5, Quantity of Queue Resources at Each Cache Level). The MIB is the L2's miss-status holding register: "Located between the L2 cache and MAC, a buffer called the move in buffer (MIB) manages in-flight requests to the MAC." (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.1). Both cache levels handle multiple misses in flight (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.4: "Both the L1 and L2 caches can handle the processing of multiple cache misses in flight."), and the 256-entry MIB is the cap on how many L2 misses one CMG can have outstanding. The entry width, the address-match comparators used to merge duplicate misses, and the MIB's allocation policy are not published.
- **Register file + comparator (the store lock register)** — 244 entries per CMG (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-5). The L2 has no move-out buffer because "move-out data is sent directly to the MAC" — instead the store lock register marks lines with a move-out in progress (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.4: "The L2 cache has no MOB because move-out data is sent directly to the MAC. However, the L2 cache is equipped with the store lock register, which indicates when a move-out operation is in progress, to guarantee the consistency of write and read."). Its 244 entries cap the number of in-flight writebacks, matching the MAC scheduler queue depth below. The register's cell form and the comparator that checks a line against it are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the MAC scheduler queue)** — 244 entries per MAC (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 10-2, Quantity of Scheduler Resources for HBM2: "Scheduler queue size 244 entries / MAC"). The MAC reorders accesses for maximum throughput under HBM2 timing rules (verified — Manual v1.4, §10.1: "To achieve the maximum throughput while complying with HBM2 standards, the MAC has a scheduler for access order control."). The scheduling policy — row-hit-first, read/write grouping, refresh handling — is not published.
- **SRAM bank + mux (the HBM2 interface)** — One MAC per CMG with a point-to-point connection to one 8 GiB HBM2 stack (verified — Manual v1.4, §10.1: "The memory access controller (MAC) is a unit that writes to and reads from the main memory. The second-generation high bandwidth memory (HBM2) is adopted as the main memory. The MAC is implemented in each CMG. Each MAC has a P2P connection with the HBM2 chip."). The interface is 1,024 bits wide at a 2.0 Gbps data rate (verified — Fujitsu A64FX Datasheet, 2021: "Data Signal Transfer Rate 2.0 Gbps", "Data Width 1,024 bits"), which multiplies out to 256 GB/s per stack (arithmetic, matching the published peak). Read throughput peaks at 256 GB/s per MAC and write throughput at 128 GB/s per MAC (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 10-3) — the write path is half the read path, so the interface is read-biased by construction. Local memory latency is 131 ns from the nearest core and 140 ns from the farthest, at 2 GHz (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 10-3). The HBM2 PHY's internal structure, the bank/channel mapping inside the stack and the refresh scheduler are not published.
- **FIFO / queue + credit counter (the ring interface)** — The four CMGs are connected in a ring at the L2 level (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.1: "As shown in Figure 9-1, the CMGs are connected in a ring structure at the L2 cache level. Coherence between the L2 caches is guaranteed by hardware. The L2 caches are interconnected by two-way ring bus."). Ring traffic runs at 64 bytes/cycle per ring (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-1: "L2 to L2 64 bytes / cycle (per Ring)"), i.e. 128 GB/s per direction at 2 GHz (arithmetic) — an order of magnitude below the CMG-internal 512 bytes/cycle, which is why Level-2 calls the ring the only shared, contended resource. The ring router's buffering, its flow-control/credit structure and the ring arbitration policy are not published.
- **SRAM bank / CAM (the on-chip directory)** — Coherence across the four CMGs is kept by ccNUMA with an on-chip directory (verified — Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 13: "Four CMGs keep cache coherency by ccNUMA with on-chip directory."). The directory is the table that lets a CMG find which other CMG holds a shared line without broadcasting to all four. Its location (at the L2/ring boundary), its entry count, whether it is SRAM-indexed or CAM-based, and its lookup latency are not published in the manual or the deck.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 data cache)** — 64 KiB, 4-way set associative, 256-byte lines, virtual index and physical tag (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-2, L1 Cache Specifications). Two 64-byte/cycle read ports (verified — Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 12: "Read port0, Read port1, Read data0, 64B/cycle, Read data1, 64B/cycle"), which is 128 bytes/cycle — 230.4 GB/s at 1.8 GHz (arithmetic, matching the deck's published ">230 GB/s" read rate). The cache accepts two loads or one store at a time (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.2: "The L1D cache accepts two loads or one store at a time."), and hit latency is 5 cycles for integer loads, 8 cycles for SIMD&FP/SVE loads in short mode and 11 cycles in long mode (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-2). Sets: 64 (arithmetic from the published index formula index_A = (A mod 16,384) / 256). Banking beyond the two read ports, the write-port width and the data-array port style are not published.
- **SRAM bank + comparator (the L1D tag array)** — Four-way tag compare per access (arithmetic from the 4-way geometry), MESI state, writeback. Synonyms from the 4 KiB page size are prevented in hardware (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.2: "Depending on the selection of the page size, a synonym may arise with the L1 cache. Since the L1 cache has a capacity of 64 KiB and is 4-way set associative, its indexes use a 16 KiB space. If the selected page is a 4-KiB page, a synonym may occur at bits[13:12] of the address. The A64FX is designed to prevent synonyms by using hardware."). The tag width and the synonym-prevention circuit's form are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the L1D move-in/move-out buffers, and the L1I move-in buffer)** — L1D MIB 12 entries per core, L1D MOB 4 entries per core, L1I MIB 3 entries per core (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 9-5). The L1D and L2 are connected by a two-tiered bus with separate queues for move-in and move-out, so fills and writebacks proceed asynchronously (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.1: "The L1D cache and the L2 cache are connected by a two-tiered bus. In addition to a MIB, a MOB (Move Out Buffer) is located between the L1D cache and the L2 cache. L1D caches are in a structure that asynchronously manages move-in and move-out requests, and they have separate queues implemented."). Move-in bypass lets a response go to the higher cache level without waiting for the move-in to complete (verified — Manual v1.4, §9.5), which is how the 131 ns local-memory latency stays reachable. Entry widths and the bypass-eligibility logic beyond the published instruction list are not published.
- **Comparator + mux (the combined-gather unit)** — A64FX introduces "Combined Gather" mechanism enabling to return up to two consecutive elements in a "128-byte aligned block" simultaneously (verified — Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 12). At the primitive level this is an address-comparator set — which gathered elements fall in the same 128-byte block — feeding a data mux that packs both elements into the vector register in one pass, doubling gather throughput over the one-element-per-pass baseline. The number of parallel gather lanes, the gather queue depth and the scatter-side counterpart are not published.
- **CAM (the L1 TLBs and the translation table cache)** — L1 instruction and data TLBs are fully associative, 16 entries each, FIFO replacement (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 8-1, TLB Specifications: "Association method Full associative", "Number of entries 16 entries", "Replacement algorithm FIFO"). A fully associative TLB is a CAM: the incoming page number is compared against all 16 stored tags in parallel — sixteen comparators per TLB (arithmetic). The translation table cache is a second 16-entry fully associative CAM with LRU replacement (verified — Manual v1.4, §8.2: "As shown in Table 8-2, the translation table cache is a buffer that has a full associative structure, the number of entries is 16, and each entry holds a table descriptor."). The CAM cell form and the FIFO pointer implementation are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L2 TLBs)** — 4-way set associative, 1,024 entries each for instruction and data, LRU replacement (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 8-1: "Association method 4-way set associative", "Number of entries 1,024 entries", "Replacement algorithm LRU"). That geometry is 256 sets with an 8-bit set index (arithmetic), and four tag comparators per lookup (arithmetic). The LRU state bits per set and the table-walk path are not published beyond the table.
- **FIFO / queue + CAM (the reservation stations)** — Reservation station for EAG (RSA): 10 entries × 2, split; reservation station for EXE (RSE): 20 entries × 2, split, shared by integer, SIMD&FP and SVE; reservation station for branch (RSBR): 19 entries (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 2-1, Out-of-Order Resources). Each is "a scheduler for temporarily storing operation-flows to be executed in the [respective] pipeline and issuing them out of order" (verified — Manual v1.4, §2.4, RSA/RSE definitions). At the primitive level each entry's readiness is a parallel tag compare — its source physical-register tags against the broadcast result tags each cycle, a CAM-style wakeup — and the select logic is a priority encoder picking one ready op per pipe per cycle. The wakeup comparator count per entry, the number of result buses and the issue arbitration policy are not published.
- **Register file (the physical register files)** — General-purpose physical registers: 96 entries (32 architectural + 64 renaming); floating-point physical registers: 128 entries (32 architectural + 96 renaming); predicate physical registers: 48 entries (16 architectural + 32 renaming) (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 2-1). Each FPR entry holds a 512-bit SVE vector, so the vector register file alone is 128 × 512 bits = 8 KiB of register storage per core (arithmetic). The register files are multi-ported SRAM/latch arrays feeding two 512-bit FMA pipes, four ALUs and two address generators; the port counts and the cell form are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the commit stack entry, CSE)** — 128 entries (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 2-1), the in-order commit queue: "This resource is used to reorder instructions executed out of order into program order." (verified — Manual v1.4, §2.4). Up to four µOPs are allocated per Group ID, with 32 GIDs (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 2-1 and §2.4). A 128-entry circular queue needs 7-bit head and tail pointers (arithmetic). The storage-cell form, the commit-head ready-check circuit and the flush/recovery wiring are not published.
- **Counter (the fetch and store ports)** — Virtual fetch port: 160 entries; real fetch port: 40 entries. Virtual store port: 192 entries; real store port: 24 entries. Write buffer: 8 entries (verified — Manual v1.4, Table 2-1). The virtual ports let the core track far more in-flight memory operations than the real ports can execute — the real ports are the throttling resource, and the write buffer holds post-commit store data "until it is written to the L1D cache" (verified — Manual v1.4, §2.4). The counter widths, the virtual-to-real promotion policy and the store-port ordering comparators are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the prefetch queue, PFQ)** — 16 entries per core (verified — Manual v1.4, §11.5: "The hardware has a resource called PFQ (Pre-Fetch Queue) for address prediction and prefetch access. The PFQ is located inside each processor core and has 16 entries per processor core."). Each entry stores a predicted address, a prefetch distance and an address reliableness (Strong or Weak); Strong prefetches wait for resources, Weak ones are dropped when resources are short (verified — Manual v1.4, §11.3). The stream-detection table's geometry beyond the 16-entry PFQ is not published.
- **Register file (the sector-cache partition control)** — The sector cache "is a mechanism that partitions the area of a cache and can select which partition to use in units of instructions or processor cores" (verified — Manual v1.4, §12.1), letting software keep streaming data from evicting reused data. At the primitive level this is a set of partition-control registers (system registers) plus the allocation comparators that steer each fill to its sector. The number of sectors, the control-register widths and the sector-capacity adjustment steps are not published beyond the manual's worked examples.
- **Multiplier array + adder tree (the two 512-bit SVE FMA pipes, FLA/FLB)** — Per core: "2x 512-bit wide SIMD FMA + Predicate Operation + 4x ALU (shared w/ 2x AGEN)" and "2x 512-bit wide SIMD load or 512-bit wide SIMD store" (verified — Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 9). Each FMA pipe is eight double-precision lanes of multiplier array plus adder tree — 512 bits is eight 64-bit lanes — and the two pipes together with the 48 compute cores are what deliver the published >2.7 TFLOPS (verified — Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 7: "Peak Performance (Efficiency) >2.7TFLOPS (>90%@DGEMM)"). The per-lane multiplier geometry, the adder-tree depth and the FMA pipeline latency are not published beyond the manual's per-instruction latency table.

**Parameter honesty.** The manual publishes the CMG's governing numbers in full: the crossbar's per-core and per-CMG bus widths; the L2's 8 MiB, 16-way, 256-byte-line geometry, its two-bank split, its hashed 11-bit index and its 37–47-cycle hit latency; the MIB/MOB queue depths at every level (L1I 3, L1D 12/4, L2 256, store lock register 244); the MAC scheduler's 244-entry queue, the HBM2 1,024-bit / 2.0 Gbps interface and the 131–140 ns local latency; the ring's two-way form and 64 bytes/cycle; the TLBs' 16-entry fully associative / 1,024-entry 4-way geometries; the reservation stations' 10×2 / 20×2 / 19 entries; the register files' 96 / 128 / 48 entries; the CSE's 128 entries; the virtual/real fetch and store port counts; the PFQ's 16 entries; and the SVE pipes' 2×512-bit form. It publishes no crossbar arbitration policy or mux radix; no L2 bank-internal organisation, tag width or tag ECC detail; no MIB entry width or merge comparators; no store-lock-register cell form; no MAC scheduling policy; no HBM2 PHY internals or channel mapping; no ring flow control or router buffering; no directory geometry at all; no L1D/L1I banking or write-port width; no L1D tag width or synonym-circuit form; no gather lane count or queue depth; no TLB CAM cell form; no reservation-station wakeup comparator counts, result-bus counts or issue policy; no register-file port counts or cell form; no CSE storage-cell form; no fetch/store-port counter widths or promotion policy; no PFQ table geometry beyond its 16 entries; no sector-cache sector count or register widths; and no FMA lane multiplier geometry, adder-tree depth or pipeline latency — and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — L1I and L1D caches are 64 KiB, 4-way set associative, 256-byte lines, virtual index and physical tag; the L1D accepts two loads or one store at a time; L1D hit latency is 5 cycles (integer), 8 cycles (SIMD&FP/SVE short), 11 cycles (SIMD&FP/SVE long). Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §9.2, Table 9-2.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — L2 cache is 8 MiB per CMG, 16-way set associative, 256-byte lines, physically indexed and physically tagged, MESI, writeback; hit latency 37–47 cycles depending on core-to-cache position; two-bank configuration with physical addresses interleaved at bit[8]; index is hashed via the published XOR formula. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §9.2, Table 9-3.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Bus throughputs: L2 to L1D 64 bytes/cycle per core; L1D to L2 32 bytes/cycle per core; L2 to L1D 512 bytes/cycle per CMG; L1D to L2 256 bytes/cycle per CMG; L2 to L2 64 bytes/cycle per ring; memory to L2 128 bytes/cycle per CMG; L2 to memory 64 bytes/cycle per CMG. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, Table 9-1.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Queue resources: L1I MIB 3 entries/core; L1D MIB 12 entries/core and MOB 4 entries/core; L2 MIB 256 entries/CMG; store lock register 244 entries/CMG; the L2 has no MOB because move-out data is sent directly to the MAC. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §9.4, Table 9-5.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — The four CMGs are connected in a ring at the L2 cache level, interconnected by a two-way ring bus; coherence between the L2 caches is guaranteed by hardware. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §9.1.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — The MAC is implemented in each CMG, has a point-to-point connection with the HBM2 chip, and carries a scheduler for access-order control; scheduler queue is 244 entries per MAC; HBM2 is 8 GiB per MAC at a 2 Gbps data rate. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §10.1, Tables 10-1 and 10-2.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Memory access performance per CMG: local memory latency (load-to-use) 131 ns shortest core / 140 ns longest core at 2 GHz; read throughput peak 256 GB/s per MAC; write throughput peak 128 GB/s per MAC. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, Table 10-3.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — HBM2 interface is 1,024 bits wide at a 2.0 Gbps data signal transfer rate; one stack per CMG holds 8 GiB and delivers 256 GB/s. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Datasheet (2021).
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — TLB: L1 instruction and data TLBs are fully associative, 16 entries each, FIFO replacement; L2 TLBs are 4-way set associative, 1,024 entries each, LRU replacement. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §8.1, Table 8-1.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Translation table cache is a fully associative buffer of 16 entries, each holding a table descriptor, with LRU replacement. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §8.2, Table 8-2.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Out-of-order resources: CSE 128 entries; GID 32 entries; GPR 96 physical entries (32 architectural + 64 renaming); FPR 128 physical entries (32 + 96); PPR 48 physical entries (16 + 32); RSA 10 entries × 2 split; RSE 20 entries × 2 split; RSBR 19 entries; TOR 3 entries; fetch port virtual 160 / real 40; store port virtual 192 / real 24; write buffer 8 entries. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §2.4, Table 2-1.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Reservation stations are schedulers that temporarily store operation-flows and issue them out of order (RSA for EAGA/EAGB, RSE for EXA/EXB/FLA/FLB/PR, RSBR for branches); the CSE reorders out-of-order execution into program order; the write buffer temporarily stores post-commit store data until written to the L1D cache. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §2.4.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — A CMG consists of 13 cores, an L2 cache and a memory controller; four CMGs keep cache coherency by ccNUMA with an on-chip directory; the X-bar connection in a CMG maximises throughput of the L2 cache. Source: Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 13.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Combined Gather returns up to two consecutive elements in a 128-byte aligned block simultaneously; the L1D has two 64-byte/cycle read ports. Source: Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 12.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Per core: 2× 512-bit wide SIMD FMA plus predicate operation plus 4× ALU shared with 2× address generators; 2× 512-bit wide SIMD load or 512-bit wide SIMD store. Source: Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 9.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — Peak performance over 2.7 TFLOPS at over 90% DGEMM efficiency; memory bandwidth 1,024 GB/s at over 80% Stream Triad. Source: Fujitsu, Hot Chips 30 (2018) deck, slide 7.
- [verified] **Fujitsu A64FX** — The PFQ (Pre-Fetch Queue) is located inside each processor core and has 16 entries per processor core, for address prediction and prefetch access. Source: Fujitsu A64FX Microarchitecture Manual v1.4, §11.5.
