### IBM POWER10

#### Down to the primitives: The eight-slice engine and the Matrix Math Engine — slices, queues and a 4×2 grid of multiply-add tiles

The two signature blocks decompose into two primitive families: the slice engine's queues, register files and issue select, and the Matrix Math Engine's accumulator register file, its 4×2 grid of processing units, and the buses that feed and drain it. The MMA paper (Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142, §III) publishes the MME's physical structure in unusual detail — the grid, the per-half port counts, the transfer cycle counts — which is why the MME half of this section is mostly verified. For the slice engine, IBM publishes the queue depths only through the Hot Chips 32 deck, which was not directly readable this run, so those figures stay unverifiable and the wakeup, arbitration and port-count internals are stated as not published.

- **FIFO / queue (the 512-entry shared instruction table)** — The in-flight window for the whole SMT8 core: dispatched instructions wait here until their operands are ready, then issue to one of the eight slices. 512 entries (unverifiable — Wikipedia, "Power10", tracing to the Hot Chips 32 deck); a 512-entry table needs a 9-bit entry index (arithmetic). The table is shared by all eight threads, so at full SMT8 the 512 entries are the per-thread ceiling Level-2 calls the shared-table ceiling. The wakeup mechanism (how a result broadcast finds its waiting entries) and the issue-select logic are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the per-slice in-flight window)** — Each slice holds up to 20 in-flight instructions (unverifiable — Wikipedia, tracing to the Hot Chips 32 deck); eight slices × 20 = 160 in-flight instructions per SMT8 core (arithmetic). The per-slice window is the queue a slice issues from, one instruction per cycle; its storage form (register array vs SRAM) and its ready-check circuit are not published.
- **Register file (the per-slice vector-scalar register file, VS RF)** — Each execution slice contains a register file for the 128-bit vector-scalar registers (verified — MMA paper §III: "An execution slice contains a register file (VS RF) for the 128-bit wide vector-scalar registers and an execution unit (VU) for performing operations on those registers."). 64 VSRs × 128 bits = 8,192 bits (1 KiB) of register storage per slice's VS RF (arithmetic). The read/write port counts, the bypass network feeding it, and the cell form are not published.
- **Priority encoder / mux (per-slice issue select)** — Each slice issues one instruction per cycle (verified — MMA paper §III: "On a given cycle, each slice can issue one instruction for execution."), so the SMT8 core issues up to eight instructions per cycle across its eight slices (arithmetic). The select picks one ready instruction from the slice's 20-entry window each cycle — a 20-to-1 priority choice, hence a ~5-bit select (arithmetic from log2(20)). The issue arbitration policy between threads and the operand-bypass mux radix are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the load queue and the store queue)** — 128-entry load queue and 80-entry store queue, halved to 64 and 40 when a single thread runs (unverifiable — Wikipedia, tracing to the Hot Chips 32 deck). At the primitive level these are FIFOs with an address-match function: each new access must be checked against queued entries for ordering and for store-to-load forwarding. The entry widths, the number of address comparators, the forwarding policy and the queue's drain order are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 instruction and data caches)** — 48 KB L1 instruction cache and 32 KB L1 data cache per SMT4 half (verified — IT Jungle, "Drilling Down Into The Power10 Chip Architecture", recorded at Level-2), i.e. 2×48 KB L1I and 2×32 KB L1D per SMT8 core. Line size, associativity, port counts and hit latencies are not published in the sources read this run.
- **SRAM bank (the private L2 cache)** — 2 MB private L2 per core (unverifiable — Wikipedia and RCS Wiki, "POWER10", tracing to the Hot Chips 32 deck). Associativity, line size, bank count and hit latency are not published in the sources read this run.
- **Lookup table (the 4,096-entry TLB)** — 4,096 entries (unverifiable — Wikipedia, "Power10", tracing to the Hot Chips 32 deck). Associativity, page-size coverage, lookup latency and the cell form are not published in the sources read this run.
- **FSM / controller (the Instruction Fetch Unit, IFU)** — The front-end controller: MMA instructions "are fetched, decoded and dispatched, like any other instruction, by the front-end component of the core (called the Instruction Fetch Unit, or IFU)" (verified — MMA paper §II), and the IFU also handles the 64-bit prefixed instructions. Decode width, the rename-table structure and the dispatch bandwidth into the 512-entry table are not published.
- **SRAM bank + comparator (the branch prediction tables)** — Branch prediction accuracy roughly doubled over POWER9 (unverifiable — Wikipedia, recorded at Level-2). The predictor's table sizes, organisation and the geometry behind the doubled accuracy are not published in the sources read this run.
- **Register file (the accumulator register file, ACC RF)** — The eight 512-bit accumulators are the architectural state (verified, recorded at Level-2). Physically, the MME's accumulator register file is shared by its two pipelines: "The matrix math engine logically consists of two (2) execution pipelines (MU2 and MU3 for instructions issued from slices 2 and 3, respectively) sharing a common accumulator register file (ACC RF)." (verified — MMA paper §III). Each processing-unit tile holds a 64-bit slice of the ACC RF, and each half-slice carries two read and one write ports (verified — MMA paper §III, see the PU bullet below). The total physical ACC RF storage per MME and the slice-to-accumulator mapping beyond the 64-bit slice are not stated in the paper.
- **Multiplier array + adder tree (the processing-unit arithmetic units)** — The MME's physical implementation "consists of a 4 × 2 grid of processing units (PU)" (verified — MMA paper §III), i.e. eight PUs per MME (arithmetic). "Each processing unit has two identical halves, one for each of the issuing slices (2 and 3). Each half includes a 64-bit slice of the accumulator register file (ACC2 and ACC3) with two read and one write ports. The arithmetic and logic unit in each half (ALU2 and ALU3) can perform one double-precision or two single-precision floating-point multiply-add(s). It can also perform 4, 8, or 16 multiply-adds of 16-, 8-, or 4-bit data, respectively." (verified — MMA paper §III). Per MME that is 8 PUs × 2 halves × 1 DP FMA = 16 DP FMAs = 32 FLOPs per cycle (arithmetic); with two MMEs per SMT8 core (one per SMT4 half) that is 64 FP64 FLOPs per cycle (arithmetic, matching Thompto's verified 64 FLOPs/cycle figure). For INT4: 8 PUs × 2 halves × 16 MACs = 256 MACs = 512 ops per cycle per MME, × 2 MMEs = 1,024 ops per cycle per core (arithmetic, matching the verified ~1,024 figure). The FMA pipeline depth and the adder-tree structure inside each half-ALU are not published.
- **Mux / demux + bus (the fetch buses and the result buses)** — "The fetch buses from the vector-scalar register files bring X and Y operands for the outer product instructions and transfer data from the vector-scalar registers to the accumulators." (verified — MMA paper §III). "The result buses transfer data from accumulators to the vector-scalar registers. It takes two (2) cycles to transfer four (4) vector-scalar registers to an accumulator and four (4) cycles to transfer one accumulator to 4 vector-scalar registers. Up to two transfers can be performed simultaneously." (verified — MMA paper §III). Effective transfer rates: 512 bits in 2 cycles = 256 bits/cycle into the accumulator, 512 bits in 4 cycles = 128 bits/cycle out (arithmetic). The bus widths and the two-transfer arbitration are not published.
- **FSM / controller (the two rank-k pipelines, MU2 and MU3)** — Two pipelines per MME executing "two rank-k update instructions per cycle" (verified — MMA paper §III: "This organization supports the execution of two rank-k update instructions per cycle."), fed only by slices 2 and 3: "Slices 2 and 3 can issue either a vector instruction or an MMA instruction. Slices 0 and 1 can only issue vector instructions." (verified — MMA paper §III). IBM's own guidance counts the result as "Four parallel units per SMT8 core" (verified — IBM Developer, "Matrix-Multiply Assist (MMA) exploitation in OpenBLAS on IBM AIX"), i.e. the four rank-k pipelines (two per MME, one MME per SMT4 half); the wiki's Level-2 "four MME units per core" counts those same four pipelines, so no value changes. Per-pipeline issue arbitration and ACC RF port-conflict handling are not published.
- **Power gate (the MME power domain)** — The MME unit can be power gated: "When the MME unit is power gated, thus eliminating any MME draw when running the VSX code, that difference increases to 12%." (verified — MMA paper §VI, power results). The gating granularity (per MME vs per pipeline) and the wake latency are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The MMA paper publishes the MME's structure in full: one MME per four-slice backend with two pipelines (MU2, MU3) sharing a common ACC RF; a 4×2 grid of eight processing units per MME; two halves per PU, one per issuing slice; a 64-bit ACC RF slice with two read and one write ports per half; per-half ALU capability of 1 DP / 2 SP FMA or 4/8/16 multiply-adds of 16/8/4-bit data; two rank-k updates per cycle; fetch and result bus transfer counts (2 cycles in, 4 cycles out, up to two simultaneous); accumulator locality during computation; and MME power gating. IBM's own guidance publishes the four-parallel-units-per-SMT8-core count. The slice engine's published numbers are the secondary-sourced ones from Level-2 (512-entry table, 20 per slice, 128/80 queues, 4,096-entry TLB, 2 MB L2) — the Hot Chips 32 deck behind them was not directly readable this run, so they stay unverifiable. It publishes no instruction-table wakeup or issue-select implementation; no per-slice queue storage form; no VS RF port counts or bypass mux radix; no load/store queue entry widths, comparator counts or forwarding policy; no L1/L2 line size, associativity or latency; no TLB associativity; no IFU decode width or rename structure; no branch-predictor table geometry; no FMA pipeline depth or adder-tree structure inside the PU halves; no ACC RF total physical storage; no fetch/result bus widths; no MU arbitration policy; and no MME power-gating granularity or wake latency — and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — The backend per SMT4 half consists of four execution slices ES[0:3] plus one attached Matrix Math Engine; each slice contains a vector-scalar register file (VS RF) and a vector execution unit (VU). Source: Moreira et al., "A matrix math facility for Power ISA(TM) processors", arXiv:2104.03142, §III.
- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — Each slice issues one instruction per cycle; slices 2 and 3 can issue either a vector instruction or an MMA instruction, while slices 0 and 1 can only issue vector instructions. Source: Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142, §III.
- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — The MME logically consists of two execution pipelines (MU2 and MU3, for instructions issued from slices 2 and 3) sharing a common accumulator register file (ACC RF); this organisation supports two rank-k update instructions per cycle. Source: Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142, §III.
- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — The MME's physical implementation is a 4×2 grid of processing units (PU); each PU has two identical halves, one per issuing slice, and each half includes a 64-bit slice of the accumulator register file with two read and one write ports. Source: Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142, §III.
- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — Each PU-half ALU can perform one double-precision or two single-precision floating-point multiply-adds, or 4, 8, or 16 multiply-adds of 16-, 8-, or 4-bit data; each ALU's result is always written to the corresponding accumulator register file while its input can come from either half (hence the two read ports). Source: Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142, §III.
- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — Fetch buses bring the X and Y operands from the vector-scalar register files to the MME; result buses transfer accumulator data back to the VSRs; it takes two cycles to transfer four VSRs to an accumulator and four cycles to transfer one accumulator to four VSRs, with up to two transfers simultaneously. Source: Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142, §III.
- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — During the computation phase of a math kernel the accumulator data stays local to the Matrix Math Engine and no output is placed on the result buses, making execution more power efficient. Source: Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142, §III.
- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — Four parallel MMA units per SMT8 core. Source: IBM Developer, "Matrix-Multiply Assist (MMA) exploitation in OpenBLAS on IBM AIX" (Figure 1: Power10 SMT8 Core and MMA Facility).
- [verified] **IBM POWER10** — The MME unit can be power gated, eliminating MME power draw when running VSX code. Source: Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142, §VI (power results).
- [unverifiable] **IBM POWER10** — Private L2 cache is 2 MB per core; the per-core L3 region is 8 MB (120 MB per die); the TLB has 4,096 entries. Source: Wikipedia, "Power10"; RCS Wiki, "POWER10" (both secondary; tracing to the Hot Chips 32 deck).
- [unverifiable] **IBM POWER10** — Primitive-level parameters not published: instruction-table wakeup/select implementation; per-slice queue storage form; VS register-file port counts and bypass mux radix; load/store queue entry widths, address-comparator counts and forwarding policy; L1/L2 line size, associativity and latency; TLB associativity and lookup latency; IFU decode width and rename-table structure; branch-predictor table sizes and organisation; FMA pipeline depth and adder-tree structure inside the PU halves; total physical ACC RF storage per MME; fetch/result bus widths and two-transfer arbitration; MU issue arbitration and ACC RF port-conflict handling; MME power-gating granularity and wake latency. Sources: Moreira et al., arXiv:2104.03142 (read live this run); IBM Developer MMA tutorial (read live this run); Wikipedia, "Power10" (Hot Chips 32 deck not directly readable this run).

### SiFive Performance P670

#### Down to the primitives: The 4-wide rename/dispatch fabric — 160-entry ROB, four dispatch buffers, nine issue queues

The fabric decomposes into four primitive families: the reorder buffer and rename pools that hold the in-flight window; the dispatch buffers and issue queues that stage and schedule it; the execution clusters' adders and register files; and the memory-side SRAM banks, predictor tables and 32-byte links that feed it. SiFive's launch pipeline-overview diagram publishes the queue depths and pool sizes below — a vendor diagram that actually numbers its queues — which is why this section can state depths; where the diagram stops, this section says so.

- **FIFO / queue (the 160-entry reorder buffer)** — The in-flight window for the whole core: renamed instructions take a slot at dispatch in program order and retire from the head in program order, up to 4 per cycle. A 160-entry circular queue needs 8-bit head and tail pointers (arithmetic: 2^7 = 128 < 160 ≤ 256 = 2^8). The ROB's storage-cell form, its banking and the retire-head ready-check circuit are not published.
- **Register file (the rename map and the rename pools)** — Rename maps each architectural register to a physical register: the pools hold 208 integer, 208 floating-point and 2×64 vector physical registers (verified — launch diagram), so physical-register indices are 8 bits wide (arithmetic: 2^8 = 256 ≥ 208). The map itself is a register file indexed by architectural register number, read and written up to 4-wide per cycle at dispatch; its entry count beyond the pool sizes, its port counts and the free-list structure that hands out fresh physical registers are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the four dispatch buffers)** — One staging FIFO per cluster — Integer, Load/Store, Floating Point, Vector (verified — launch diagram) — sitting between the 4-wide rename/dispatch stage and the issue queues, absorbing the cycles when rename runs ahead of issue. Their depths, entry widths and the backpressure signalling into dispatch are not published.
- **FIFO / queue + CAM (the nine issue queues and their wakeup)** — Four 16-entry integer queues, one 24-entry load/store queue, two 16-entry floating-point queues and two 16-entry vector queues — 152 entries in total (verified — launch diagram). Each entry waits until its operands are ready, and readiness is a parallel tag compare: every entry's source tags are compared against the broadcast result tags each cycle, a CAM-style compare per entry, with a priority encoder picking the oldest ready entry per port. The queue depths are published; the number of wakeup comparators per entry, the result-bus count and the arbitration policy among ready entries within a queue are not published.
- **Priority encoder / mux (issue select)** — Each cluster selects ready entries for its execution ports each cycle: the integer cluster's four queues feed two ALUs and two branch units, the load/store cluster's single 24-entry queue feeds two AGUs over two load/store pipes (verified — launch diagram). The select is a priority choice among the ready entries — the encoder's input width is the queue depth — but the arbitration policy (oldest-first vs round-robin vs port-priority) is not published.
- **Adder tree (the two integer ALUs)** — Each ALU is a 64-bit adder (a carry-lookahead tree computing carries in parallel) plus a logic unit (a mux tree selecting AND/OR/XOR/NOT) plus a comparator for flag-setting operations. MUL, DIV and int-to-float conversion sit under the first ALU (verified — launch diagram). The adder's carry-tree depth, the logic unit's mux radix and the MUL/DIV pipeline depths are not published.
- **Adder (the two address-generation units)** — Each AGU in the load/store cluster is a base-plus-offset adder producing the effective address for a load or store; two AGUs over two load/store pipes (verified — launch diagram). The adder width beyond the 64-bit address and the AGU-to-cache port mapping are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 instruction and data caches)** — 32 KB instruction cache and 32 KB data cache (verified — launch diagram). Each 32 KB array is 262,144 bits, i.e. 1,572,864 6T SRAM cell transistors per cache (arithmetic). Line size, associativity, port counts and hit latencies are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the private L2 cache)** — Up to 512 KB per core (verified — launch diagram), i.e. up to 4,194,304 bits, or 25,165,824 6T cell transistors at the maximum configuration (arithmetic). Associativity, line size, bank count and hit latency are not published.
- **SRAM bank + comparator (the branch predictor's four pieces)** — The Next Line Predictor, Branch Direction Predictor, Return Address Stack and Indirect Jump Target Predictor (verified — launch diagram). The predictors' learning memory is SRAM banks of counters and target entries indexed by PC and history, compared against the fetch address; the Return Address Stack is a LIFO — a small register array plus a stack-pointer counter. Table sizes, history widths, counter widths and the RAS depth are not published.
- **Mux / demux + bus (the 32-bytes-per-cycle links)** — The instruction cache, the data cache and the L2 all connect at 32 bytes per cycle (verified — launch diagram) — 256-bit buses (arithmetic) — with a credit counter per link tracking how many transfers the receiver can still accept, so the sender never overruns it. The bus widths are arithmetic from the published rate; the credit-counter depths and the arbitration between the three links are not published.
- **FSM / controller + counter (the streaming prefetcher and cache stashing)** — The streaming prefetcher watches the load stream for strides and issues prefetches ahead of demand; cache stashing lets a tightly coupled accelerator push data into the L3 (verified — SiFive P600 Series page). Both are a control FSM driving address counters and a small table of past addresses. The prefetcher's table size, stride-detection width and prefetch distance, and the stashing interface's queue depth, are not published.
- **Pipeline register (the 13-stage pipeline)** — Thirteen stages of pipeline registers separate fetch, decode, rename, dispatch, issue, execute, writeback and retire (verified — SiFive P600 Series page: "a thirteen-stage, four-issue, out-of-order pipeline"). Each register holds the instruction, its renamed operands and its ROB slot for a cycle; a mispredict flush clears them. The stage boundaries beyond the 13-stage count and the register widths are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The launch diagram publishes the front end's 32 KB instruction cache and fetch-up-to-8 rate; the four named predictor pieces; the 4-wide decode/rename/dispatch; the 160-entry ROB; the 208 / 208 / 2×64 rename pools; the four dispatch buffers by name; the nine issue queues with their depths (4×16 integer, 1×24 load/store, 2×16 FP, 2×16 vector); the integer cluster's two ALUs, two branch units and the MUL/DIV/I2F placement; the load/store cluster's two AGUs, two pipes and 32 KB data cache; the floating-point cluster's two FMAC, two FADD and two FMUL pipes; the vector cluster's two 128-bit ALUs, DIV and V2S; the private L2 up to 512 KB and shared L3 up to 16 MB; and the 32-bytes-per-cycle links. The P600 page publishes the 13-stage pipeline, the streaming prefetcher and cache stashing. They publish no ROB storage-cell form, banking or retire-head circuit; no rename-map entry counts, port counts or free-list form; no dispatch-buffer depths or backpressure signalling; no wakeup-comparator counts, result-bus counts or issue-arbitration policy; no ALU carry-tree depth, logic-unit mux radix or MUL/DIV pipeline depths; no AGU adder width or port mapping; no L1/L2 line size, associativity, port counts or latencies; no predictor table sizes, history widths, counter widths or RAS depth; no credit-counter depths or link arbitration; no prefetcher table size, stride width, prefetch distance or stashing queue depth; and no pipeline stage boundaries beyond the 13-stage count — and this decomposition invents none of them.

#### Down to the primitives: The dual 128-bit vector cluster — DLEN = VLEN, two ALUs, DIV and V2S

The vector cluster decomposes into three primitive families: the two 128-bit ALUs and their lane arithmetic; the vector register file and the queues that feed it; and the small units around the ALUs — the divider, the vector-to-scalar path, the shifters, the comparators and the length-agnostic control. The product brief publishes the datapath width and the datatype list; the launch diagram publishes the queue depths and the unit list. Everything else is named by role, not sized.

- **Multiplier array + adder tree (the two 128-bit vector ALUs)** — Each ALU is a set of parallel lanes: 16 INT8, 8 INT16, 4 INT32 or FP32, or 2 INT64 or FP64 lanes per cycle per ALU (arithmetic from the published 128-bit width and datatype list — brief: "2x128-bit ALU, DLEN = VLEN"; datatypes INT8/16/32/64, FP16/32/64). Two ALUs let two independent vector instructions execute in the same cycle. The multiplier array's geometry, the adder-tree depth and the FP16/FP32/FP64 pipeline latencies are not published.
- **Register file (the vector physical register file)** — The dedicated 2×64-entry vector rename pool (verified — launch diagram) is 128 physical vector registers of 128 bits each: 16,384 bits, 2 KiB, of register storage (arithmetic). Its read/write port counts, the bypass network feeding the two ALUs and the cell form are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the Vector Dispatch Buffer and the two 16-entry issue queues)** — The cluster's own dispatch buffer stages vector instructions after rename, and each ALU has its own 16-entry issue queue (verified — launch diagram). Each queue entry waits on operand-ready, like the scalar queues. The dispatch buffer's depth and the queues' wakeup-comparator counts and arbitration policy are not published.
- **Mux / demux (the V2S unit)** — The vector-to-scalar unit moves one element out of a vector register into the scalar side: a lane-select mux choosing among 16 INT8, 8 INT16, 4 INT32/FP32 or 2 INT64/FP64 lanes (arithmetic from the published width and datatypes). Which lane it selects and the unit's latency are not published.
- **FSM / controller + subtractor (the vector DIV unit)** — The divider beside the two ALUs is an iterative state machine driving a subtractor over multiple cycles — the standard construction of a divider. Its radix, iteration count and latency are not published.
- **Barrel shifter (vector shifts)** — Vector shifts run through a barrel shifter in each ALU lane: a mux tree shifting by any amount in one cycle, with a per-element shift amount. The shifter's mux radix and whether shifts share the ALU's adder tree or have a dedicated tree are not published.
- **Comparator (vector compares and the mask)** — Vector compare instructions run a comparator per lane and write the results into a mask register: a 16-bit mask for INT8, 8-bit for INT16, 4-bit for INT32/FP32, 2-bit for INT64/FP64 (arithmetic from the lane counts). The mask register's storage form and the compare-to-mask write path are not published.
- **FSM / controller (vector-length-agnostic control)** — Vector length agnostic operation with dynamic runtime setting of vector lengths (verified — product brief) is a small control FSM holding the VL and VTYPE state and masking lanes off per instruction, so the same 128-bit datapath serves any vector length up to VLEN. The state registers' widths beyond the ISA-defined VLEN of 128 bits and the LMUL register-grouping control are not published.
- **Multiplier array (vector cryptography)** — The vector crypto extensions (verified — press release: "First to market with the RISC-V Vector Cryptography extensions") execute in this cluster, on crypto datapaths — AES and SHA lanes — beside the general vector ALUs. Their lane counts, pipeline structure and whether they share the ALU multipliers or have dedicated arrays are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The brief publishes the 2×128-bit ALU width, DLEN = VLEN, the vector-length-agnostic operation with dynamic runtime length setting, and the INT8/16/32/64 and FP16/32/64 datatype list. The launch diagram publishes the two 128-bit ALUs, the DIV unit and the V2S unit, the Vector Dispatch Buffer and the two 16-entry issue queues, and the 2×64 vector rename pool. The press release publishes the first-to-market vector crypto extensions. They publish no multiplier-array geometry, adder-tree depth or FP pipeline latencies; no vector register-file port counts, bypass network or cell form; no dispatch-buffer depth or queue wakeup/arbitration detail; no V2S lane selection or latency; no DIV radix, iteration count or latency; no barrel-shifter mux radix or sharing; no mask-register storage form; no VL/VTYPE state widths beyond the ISA-defined VLEN; and no vector-crypto datapath structure — and this decomposition invents none of them.

**New fact records added in this run** (appended to `files/fact-records.json`; statuses per the fact standard):

- [verified] **SiFive Performance P670** — The 160-entry reorder buffer is a circular queue; its head and tail pointers are 8 bits wide (arithmetic: 2^7 = 128 < 160 ≤ 256 = 2^8). Source: SiFive, "SiFive Performance P670 detailed pipeline overview" diagram (November 2022, via CNX Software).
- [verified] **SiFive Performance P670** — The rename pools (208 integer / 208 floating-point / 2×64 vector physical registers) imply 8-bit physical-register indices (arithmetic: 2^8 = 256 ≥ 208). Source: SiFive, "SiFive Performance P670 detailed pipeline overview" diagram (November 2022, via CNX Software).
- [verified] **SiFive Performance P670** — Each 128-bit vector ALU processes 16 INT8, 8 INT16, 4 INT32/FP32 or 2 INT64/FP64 lanes per cycle (arithmetic from the published 128-bit width and datatype list). Source: SiFive Performance P670 Product Brief, rev 1.0 (2025).
- [verified] **SiFive Performance P670** — The vector physical register file is 128 physical vector registers of 128 bits each — 16,384 bits (2 KiB) of register storage (arithmetic from the published 2×64 vector rename pool). Source: SiFive, "SiFive Performance P670 detailed pipeline overview" diagram (November 2022, via CNX Software).

Source: SiFive, "SiFive Performance P670 detailed pipeline overview" diagram (November 2022, via CNX Software).

### NVIDIA Ampere A100

#### Down to the primitives: MIG — the partition enforced in the memory system

- **Register file — the partition table.** MIG is a table before it is anything else: for each of the seven possible instances, the hardware holds which SM slice, which memory slice, which L2 slice, which memory controllers and which engines (copy engines, NVDECs) belong to it. Published: the table's contents — 7 SM slices, 8 memory slices of 5 GB on the 40 GB part, 1/8 of L2 per memory slice, 1 copy engine per 1g.5gb instance. Not published: how the table is stored — its depth beyond the 7-instance maximum, its cell form, or whether it lives in fuses, flops or SRAM.
- **FSM / controller — the partition manager.** Creating and destroying an instance is a configuration sequence driven by the driver (NVML / nvidia-smi): the partition controller fences off the slices, then hands the instance its own command streams and fault domain; teardown returns all three resource slices to the pool together. The controller is a state machine that walks the profile table — only the fixed profiles may be created, never arbitrary slices. Its internal states and sequencing are not published.
- **Crossbar — the partitioned interconnect.** The on-chip crossbar is where the isolation is physical: each instance's SMs get crossbar ports assigned uniquely to that instance, so a request from one tenant never shares a port with another's. Published: the A100 L2 has a new partitioned crossbar structure delivering 2.3× the L2 read bandwidth of V100. Per-instance port counts and crossbar radix are not published.
- **Switch arbiter + priority encoder — grant selection inside the partition.** Within an instance, the crossbar arbiter and the memory-controller schedulers pick among that instance's requests only — the priority encoder's grant can never cross an instance boundary, because the request pool it sees is already partitioned. Arbiter policy and encoder depth are not published.
- **SRAM bank — the L2 slices.** The 40 MB L2 is not one cache with a sharing policy; it is banks assigned to instances — each memory slice carries 1/8 of the L2 (5 MB of the 40 MB total, by arithmetic), so a neighbour streaming gigabytes cannot evict this instance's working set. Published: 40 MB total, 1/8 per slice. Bank count, associativity and line size per slice are not published.
- **Comparator — the address-range check.** Every memory request carries its instance's identity, and the L2 and memory-controller front ends admit only addresses inside that instance's own DRAM range — the isolation is an address-range / instance-ID comparator on every request, backed by the published guarantee that DRAM address buses are assigned uniquely per instance. Tag width and check placement are not published.
- **Credit counter — per-instance bandwidth accounting.** A memory slice is 1/8 of the total memory resources "including both capacity and bandwidth", so the memory controllers must account bandwidth per instance — credit counters that release an instance's requests only within its share, throttling a tenant that would otherwise saturate the HBM interfaces. Counter widths, refill rates and throttle policy are not published.
- **Mux / demux — instance-ID steering.** Work dispatch and memory routing are both demuxes keyed on the instance ID: the front end steers a kernel only to its instance's SMs, and the L2/memory demux steers its requests only to its instance's banks and controllers. Mux radix and steering-table structure are not published.
- **FIFO / queue — per-instance command and copy queues.** Each instance has its own command streams and its own copy engines (one per 1g.5gb instance), so scheduling queues are per-instance by construction — a queued kernel in one instance cannot be delayed behind another instance's queue. Queue depths are not published.
- **Pipeline register — the instance ID travels with the request.** The instance identity is latched into pipeline registers at each stage of the crossbar → L2 → memory-controller path, so the partition check made at the front end still holds at the DRAM bus. Stage count and ID width are not published.
- **Counter — fault attribution.** The A100 attributes errors to the application causing them, and that attribution is what lets a fault in one instance be charged to that instance alone rather than to the whole GPU. Published as a capability; counter counts, register widths and the attribution table are not published.
- **Synchronizer / reset domain — fault containment.** Faults are isolated and contained so an error in one instance does not leak into another's — architecturally, per-instance error/reset domains with synchronizers at the boundaries, so one tenant's crash cannot take the neighbouring instances down with it. Domain boundaries and synchronizer counts are not published.
- **FSM / controller — the compute-instance split.** Inside a GPU instance, the SM slices can be split again into compute instances, a second, smaller partition FSM that subdivides the already-partitioned SM pool without touching the memory slices. Its states are not published.

**Parameter honesty.** NVIDIA publishes the partition geometry — 8 memory slices of 5 GB, 7 SM slices, 14 SMs per slice, 98 SMs in the full 7g.40gb profile, 1/8 of L2 per slice, one copy engine per smallest instance — and the isolation guarantees that define MIG. It publishes nothing about the hardware that enforces the partition: no partition-table storage form, no tag or ID widths, no arbiter policy, no credit-counter sizes, no queue depths. This decomposition names those enforcement primitives by their role and says so, rather than estimating them.

### NVIDIA Volta V100

#### Down to the primitives: The first Tensor Core — a 4×4×4 matrix engine

The Tensor Core block decomposes into thirteen primitives: the register file that holds the warp's fragments; the FP16 multiplier array and the FP32 adder tree that do the actual 4×4×4 work; the muxes that route fragments in and results out; the warp scheduler's issue queue and the operand-collection path that feed the array; the WMMA/HMMA sequencer that turns a warp-level 16×16×16 operation into 4×4×4 steps; the pipeline registers, the scoreboard comparators, the per-thread program counters and the warp-synchronisation that keep the warp coherent; and the L1/shared staging store and the SM crossbar that bring tiles in from memory. NVIDIA's whitepaper publishes the block-level geometry generously — the core count, the per-clock FMA rate, the 4×4×4 operation, the SM partition, the register-file and L1 sizes — and stops there: no multiplier geometry, no collector width, no sequencer state count is published, and this section says so at each primitive.

- **Register file (the fragment store)** — The warp's operands live in registers, not in a dedicated Tensor Core memory: each of the 32 threads holds the specific A, B and C elements the fragment layout assigns it, and the warp-level 16×16 operation spans all 32 threads (verified — NVIDIA, Volta whitepaper WP-08608-001_v1.1: "At the CUDA level, the warp-level interface assumes 16x16 size matrices spanning all 32 threads of the warp."). The file is 64 KB per processing block (verified — whitepaper: "The GV100 SM is partitioned into four processing blocks, each with 16 FP32 Cores, 8 FP64 Cores, 16 INT32 Cores, two of the new mixed-precision Tensor Cores for deep learning matrix arithmetic, a new L0 instruction cache, one warp scheduler, one dispatch unit, and a 64 KB Register File."), i.e. 256 KB per SM by arithmetic, matching the published 65,536 32-bit registers per SM (verified — whitepaper, Table 2: "Max 32-bit Registers / SM 65536" for GV100). The file's banking, read-port count and the fragment layout's per-thread register allocation are not published.
- **Multiplier array (the FP16 product engine)** — The heart of the block: each Tensor Core is a fixed array that multiplies two 4×4 FP16 matrices, 64 multiplies per core per clock (verified — whitepaper: "In Volta GV100, each Tensor Core performs 64 floating point FMA operations per clock, and eight Tensor Cores in an SM perform a total of 512 FMA operations (or 1024 individual floating point operations) per clock."). Each of the 16 output elements is a 4-term dot product, and the products are formed in full precision, not rounded to FP16 (verified — whitepaper: "The FP16 multiply results in a full precision product that is then accumulated using FP32 addition with the other intermediate products for a 4x4x4 matrix multiply."). The multiplier encoding, the partial-product reduction structure and the array's physical layout are not published.
- **Adder tree (the FP32 accumulation)** — The four full-precision products behind each output element are summed and accumulated in FP32 with the C matrix (verified — whitepaper: "Tensor Cores operate on FP16 input data with FP32 accumulation."). The accumulation matrices C and D may be FP16 or FP32 (verified — whitepaper: "The matrix multiply inputs A and B are FP16 matrices, while the accumulation matrices C and D may be FP16 or FP32 matrices."). The adder-tree depth, the carry structure and whether accumulation is a tree or a serial chain are not published.
- **Mux / demux (fragment routing)** — Operands are muxed out of the register file's read ports into the multiplier array, and the D fragment is demuxed back to the owning threads' registers — the fragment's home is a set of registers scattered across the warp, so every HMMA is a scatter-gather through muxes on both sides. The mux radix, the read-port count and the routing fabric between the file and the array are not published.
- **FIFO / queue (the warp scheduler's issue queue and the operand-collection path)** — One warp scheduler and one dispatch unit per processing block (verified — whitepaper, SM partition quote above), so each block issues at most one warp instruction per clock, and the HMMA waits in the scheduler's queue until the warp is selected. Before it fires, the operand-collection path gathers the A, B and C fragments out of the register file — the fragments are not contiguous in the file, so collection is a queued, multi-cycle gather, not a single wide read. The queue depth, the collector's width and its bank-conflict behaviour are not published.
- **FSM / controller (the WMMA/HMMA sequencer)** — The warp-level operation is 16×16×16, but the hardware step is 4×4×4, so a sequencer decomposes each WMMA into 4×4×4 steps across the SM's eight Tensor Cores (verified — NVIDIA, 'Programming Tensor Cores in CUDA 9': "During program execution, multiple Tensor Cores are used concurrently by a full warp of execution. The threads within a warp provide a larger 16x16x16 matrix operation to be processed by the Tensor Cores."). The exposed shapes are m16n16k16, m8n32k16 and m32n8k16, with the inner dot-product dimension always 16 (verified — NVIDIA, 'Tensor Core Programming Using CUDA Fortran': "The Tensor Core unit itself supports 3 combinations of sizes for m, n, and k. ... we will refer to them here as m16n16k16, m8n32k16, and m32n8k16. Note that k, the inner dot-product dimension, is always 16."). By arithmetic, one 16×16×16 warp MMA is 4,096 FMAs — 64 core-steps of 64 FMAs each — so at full rate with all eight cores busy it takes 8 clocks. The sequencer's state count, its step ordering across the four processing blocks and the HMMA pipeline latency are not published.
- **Pipeline register (the fragment latches)** — Fragments are latched between the register-file read, the multiply, the accumulate and the writeback stages, so one HMMA occupies the Tensor Core pipeline for several cycles while the scheduler moves on. The dependent-issue latency the scheduler must respect is published for the FMA datapath: four clock cycles on Volta, down from six on Pascal (verified — whitepaper: "Dependent instruction issue latency is also reduced for core FMA (Fused Multiply-Add) math operations, requiring only four clock cycles on Volta, compared to six cycles on Pascal."). The HMMA stage count, the latch widths and the Tensor Core's own pipeline depth are not published.
- **Comparator (the scoreboard / readiness check)** — A warp's HMMA issues only when its operand fragments are ready, and Volta's independent thread scheduling makes that check per-thread: each thread carries its own program counter (verified — whitepaper, Table 2 footnote: "The per-thread program counter (PC) that forms part of the improved SIMT model typically requires two of the register slots per thread."), so readiness is tracked thread by thread rather than warp by warp. The scoreboard's implementation, the ready-check circuit and the stall-signalling path are not published.
- **Crossbar (the SM's internal interconnect)** — The path from the combined L1/shared store that stages tiles before fragment loads, and the dispatch path from each block's scheduler to its two Tensor Cores. The crossbar is what lets a fragment load from the L1 reach any thread's registers and lets a scheduler's HMMA reach either of its block's cores. Its radix, port counts and arbitration policy are not published.
- **SRAM bank (the L1/shared staging store)** — Tiles are staged in the combined L1 data cache and shared memory before fragments are loaded into registers: 128 KB per SM, all of it usable as cache by programs that do not use shared memory (verified — whitepaper: "The combined capacity is 128 KB/SM, more than seven times larger than the GP100 data cache, and all of it is usable as a cache by programs that do not use shared memory."), with shared memory configurable up to 96 KB per SM (verified — whitepaper: "The merger of shared memory and L1 resources enables an increase in shared memory capacity to 96 KB per Volta SM, compared to 64 KB in GP100."). The bank count, line size and associativity of the combined store are not published.
- **Counter (the per-thread program counters and the warp census)** — Up to 64 warps and 2,048 threads per SM (verified — whitepaper, Table 2: "Max Warps / SM 64", "Max Threads / SM 2048"), each thread with its own program counter, so the scheduler's warp census is 64 program counters plus the active mask per warp. The counter widths beyond the published thread counts, and the active-mask storage form, are not published.
- **Synchronizer (warp convergence for WMMA)** — A WMMA is a warp-synchronising operation: all 32 threads must participate or the result is wrong (verified — NVIDIA, 'Tensor Core Programming Using CUDA Fortran': "Every thread in the warp must take part in the operation for the results to be correct."). The reconvergence mechanism that gathers the warp's threads — diverged by Volta's per-thread scheduling — back together before the MMA fires is the synchroniser at the heart of the block. Its implementation and the convergence-barrier latency are not published.
- **Credit counter (the one-issue-slot-per-clock throttle)** — Each processing block's dispatch unit grants at most one warp instruction per clock (verified — whitepaper: one warp scheduler and one dispatch unit per processing block), so a Tensor Core block can start at most one HMMA per clock and the eight cores can between them retire at most eight 4×4×4 steps per clock — the issue slot is the credit that paces the array, and a block whose scheduler is busy with FP32/INT32 work issues no HMMA at all that cycle. Anything beyond the one-per-clock grant — credit widths, issue priority between HMMA and scalar work — is not published.

**Parameter honesty.** The whitepaper publishes the block geometry in full: 640 Tensor Cores, eight per SM, two per processing block; 64 FMA per core per clock, 512 FMA (1,024 FLOPs) per SM per clock; 125 Tensor TFLOPS; the 4×4 D = A×B + C operation with FP16 inputs and FP16 or FP32 accumulation; the warp-level 16×16 interface spanning all 32 threads; the SM partition (16 FP32, 8 FP64, 16 INT32, two Tensor Cores, L0 instruction cache, one warp scheduler, one dispatch unit, 64 KB register file per block); 65,536 32-bit registers per SM; the 128 KB combined L1/shared store per SM with up to 96 KB shared; the four-cycle FMA dependent-issue latency; and 64 warps / 2,048 threads per SM with per-thread program counters. The CUDA 9 and CUDA Fortran blogs add the warp-concurrent 16×16×16 operation, the three HMMA shapes (m16n16k16, m8n32k16, m32n8k16, k always 16) and the all-threads-must-participate rule. NVIDIA publishes no multiplier or adder geometry inside the Tensor Core, no operand-collector depth or width, no fragment-routing mux radix, no scoreboard implementation, no HMMA sequencer state count or pipeline depth, no L1 bank count, line size or associativity, no SM crossbar radix, and no warp-synchronisation circuit — and this decomposition invents none of them.
