- [unverifiable] **Intel Pentium Pro (P6)** — Gate-level structure not published: no Intel source discloses the P6's per-primitive gate/transistor implementation (ROB/RS storage-cell form and banking, RS tag-comparator circuit form, dispatch arbitration policy and priority-encoder placement, RAT entry count and port counts, result-bus count and arbitration, ROB pointer and timestamp field widths, RS/RAT latch counts). All gate-level detail in the Level-4 decomposition is textbook-standard construction, labelled as such. Sources: Papworth, IEEE Micro, Apr 1996; Bhandarkar & Ding, HPCA 1997; Colwell & Steck, ISSCC 1995.

### Intel Golden Cove

#### Down to the gates: The 6-wide front end — 32-byte fetch, six decoders and the 4K µop cache

- **CAM (the BTB tag match)** — At gate level a CAM is the primer's CAM cell per stored bit: a 6T SRAM cell holding the tag bit plus an XNOR/XOR compare gate against the search bit, every cell on a row wired-NOR onto one precharged matchline — a single mismatching bit pulls the matchline down, so the whole tag compares in one evaluation. The BTB's three-level behaviour is three such arrays in series, a miss in one level falling through to the next with a 1-cycle penalty per level, and a hit in any level returning its target through a priority mux that lets the highest level's match win. The per-level sizes, tag widths and associativity are not published, so no per-level transistor count is claimed. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 instruction cache)** — 6T cells in rows: a row decoder — an AND/NAND tree from the set-index bits — raises one wordline per set; the 32 bytes per cycle read out through bitlines into sense amplifiers, with a tag SRAM beside the data array and one comparator per way selecting the hit way through a mux. The cache's banking, set associativity and port counts are not published. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank (the µop cache / decoded-stream buffer)** — The same construction at 4K entries: a tag array with a comparator per way, a data array of decoded µops, and a way-select mux delivering up to 8 µops per cycle. The 4K-entry total is a 12-bit index by arithmetic (2^12 = 4,096); the µop format's field layout, the banking and the way count are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the µop queue)** — A circular queue in flops: entry storage in D flip-flop arrays (master–slave, ≈20–28 transistors per bit in a standard-cell library), write and read pointer counters (D flip-flops plus a full-adder incrementer), and full/empty comparators — XNOR gates per pointer bit feeding an AND tree. The 144-entry depth is an 8-bit pointer by arithmetic (2^8 = 256 ≥ 144); the queue's loop-unrolling replay — re-reading stored µops instead of re-fetching them — is a read-pointer rewind, the standard construction of a trace-cache-like replay. The queue's internal organisation is not published. Standard construction.
- **FSM / controller (the six decoders and their clock gating)** — Each decoder is combinational logic: length decoding and prefix handling as AND/OR planes (a PLA-style decode) or random logic, cracking x86 bytes into µops. The clock gating is the primer's integrated clock-gating cell: a latch plus an AND gate per decoder block, holding the clock low while the µop cache feeds the core — the ~80% gated figure is the measured duty cycle, the gate cell itself standard construction. The decoders' internal structure is not published. Standard construction.
- **CAM (the L1 instruction TLB tag match)** — A fully associative CAM: one CAM cell per stored tag bit, one matchline per entry — 256 matchlines for the 4K-page entries, 32 for the huge-page entries — each matchline feeding a priority encoder that selects the hit entry and drives its physical page number out. The TLB's replacement policy and miss-handling structure are not published. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank + counter (the direction predictor's pattern-history tables)** — Each pattern-history table is an SRAM bank of 2-bit saturating counters: two D flip-flops per counter, an increment/decrement unit (a tiny adder of full-adder cells), and saturation comparators (a few AND/OR gates) clamping the count at 00 and 11. The prediction itself is a wire — the counter's most-significant bit, read straight off the storage bit. Intel's on-chip ML model that grows or shrinks the BTB to fit the workload is a sizing control on top of these tables; its gate-level form is not published, and this decomposition does not invent it. Table sizes, history lengths, component count and algorithm are not published. Standard construction.
- **Counter / stack (the return stack)** — A LIFO in flops: return addresses held in D flip-flop registers, a stack-pointer counter (flip-flops plus a full-adder incrementer/decrementer) selecting the top entry, and push/pop muxes steering the address in on a call and out on a return. The stack's depth is not published, and the odd measured behaviour — no clear capacity step even at 128 call/return pairs, slow returns past two-deep nesting — is a behaviour, not a circuit, so no depth is inferred from it. Standard construction.
- **Mux (the supply-path select)** — The front end's last gate-level primitive is a 2:1 mux tree: transmission-gate muxes (2 transistors per leg) choosing, per µop bit, between the µop-cache hit path and the L1I-plus-decoder refill path, steered by the µop-cache hit signal. The two-taken-branches-per-cycle throughput comes from the queue's loop unrolling, not from this mux. Mux radix and the µop cache's way-selection logic are not published. Standard construction.

#### Down to the gates: The 12-port back end — unified scheduler, 512-entry ROB and the 3-load memory subsystem

- **FIFO / queue (the 512-entry reorder buffer)** — A circular queue in flops: 512 storage slots, each a D flip-flop/latch array holding one µop's control bits, destination, result value once produced and status bits (the standard construction; the ROB's actual storage-cell form and banking are not published). Head and tail are 9-bit pointer counters by arithmetic (2^9 = 512), each a D-flip-flop bank with a full-adder incrementer that advances by 0–6 per cycle as up to six µops are allocated or retired, and a wrap comparator — XNOR gates per pointer bit feeding an AND tree — that detects the count reaching 512 and wraps the pointer to zero. Full/empty is the same equality compare between head and tail. Standard construction.
- **Register file (the integer, FP/vector and flags files)** — Flop/latch storage behind read-port mux trees: every read port costs a full N:1 mux per bit — transmission-gate (2 transistors per leg) or NAND trees — which is why port count, not capacity, prices a register file. The integer file's measured floor of ten read ports is ten such mux trees per bit across 280 entries; the vector file's estimated eight read / three write ports are the same construction at 332 entries of 512-bit ZMM width. The physical-register index is 9 bits by arithmetic for both files (2^9 = 512 ≥ 280 and ≥ 332); the flags file's 248 entries need 8 bits (2^8 = 256 ≥ 248). The files' exact port counts, banking and cell form are not published by Intel. Standard construction.
- **Register file (the register alias table and move/zero elimination)** — The rename map is a small register file of the same form: one entry per architectural register, each holding the physical-register index of the most recent writer (9 bits by arithmetic for the integer file) plus a valid bit, read out through a mux on a source lookup and written through a decoder on a destination rename. Move elimination is a RAT write only — the destination architectural register is remapped to the source's physical register, no ALU op issued, no result written. Zeroing-idiom elimination is one zero-bit latch per architectural register: set the bit and the register reads as zero without allocating a physical register at all. The RAT's entry count, port counts and the exact elimination circuits are not published. Standard construction.
- **Priority encoder / switch arbiter (the unified scheduler's wakeup and select)** — Wakeup is a CAM: each of the 205 scheduler entries stores the tags of the physical registers it waits on, one XNOR compare gate per tag bit, and a result broadcast discharges the matchline of every entry waiting on that tag. Select is a priority encoder — an AND/OR prefix tree computing the highest-priority (oldest-first) ready request — producing one grant per execution port, twelve grants per cycle at most, latched into a grant register of D flip-flops for the dispatch cycle. The per-class queue depths, the wakeup-compare structure and the exact select policy are not published. Standard construction.
- **Adder tree (the fast FP adders and their bypass)** — A floating-point add is exponent compare and subtract (a small adder), a mantissa alignment shifter (a barrel shifter — a mux tree), a mantissa adder tree of full-adder cells grouped by a carry-lookahead prefix network (AND/OR trees computing generate/propagate across bit groups) so the addition does not wait on a bit-by-bit ripple, then a normalisation shifter and rounding. The 2-cycle back-to-back bypass is a result mux: the adder's output feeds straight back into the next add's operand latch, skipping the register file. Tree depth, carry structure and the bypass mux's exact form are not published. Standard construction.
- **Multiplier array (the FMA multiply stage and the vector integer multiplier)** — A mantissa multiply is partial-product generation (one AND gate per bit pair, or Booth-encoded muxes selecting 0/±1/±2 multiples) feeding a reduction tree of 3:2 and 4:2 compressors — full-adder cells (≈28 transistors each in the mirror-adder form) — that collapses the partial products in logarithmic depth before one final carry-propagate adder finishes the product. The 4-cycle FP-multiply latency and the 10-cycle vector-integer-multiply latency are pipeline depths — more D-flip-flop pipeline-register stages between the compressor levels — not wider trees. Booth encoding and compressor-tree geometry are not published. Standard construction.
- **Mux (the bypass/forwarding network)** — Each operand latch sits behind a mux that selects among the result buses: a transmission-gate or NAND-tree mux per operand bit, steered by the tag-match state, so a result produced on one port feeds a dependent µop's operand latch on a later port without a register-file round trip — the 2-cycle FP-add bypass is this mux doing its work. On the writeback side each completing result is demuxed to its ROB entry by a write decoder and broadcast to all waiting scheduler entries at once — a fan-out net with a per-entry enable (an AND gate) driven by the tag match. The network's mux radix, the number of forwarding buses and the per-port operand-select logic are not published. Standard construction.
- **Counter (the branch order buffer)** — A 128-entry checkpoint store: flop-array storage for one checkpoint per in-flight branch, a 7-bit index counter by arithmetic (2^7 = 128) tracking the youngest branch, and a restore path — a mux per checkpoint bit that reloads the saved rename state when a mispredict is detected. Its entry format, checkpointing mechanism and port counts are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the 192-entry load queue and 114-entry store queue)** — Circular queues in flops with pointer counters and full/empty comparators, as in the ROB: the load queue's pointer is 8 bits by arithmetic (2^8 = 256 ≥ 192), the store queue's 7 bits (2^7 = 128 ≥ 114). Loads wait for their addresses and data; stores wait for retirement, because only known-good data can be committed to cache. The associative part — the load finding a matching store — is the CAM below. The queues' lookup structure is not published. Standard construction.
- **CAM (the store-to-load forwarding match)** — The load queue's correctness check at gate level is one comparator per store-queue entry: each in-flight store's address held in flops, compared bit by bit against the load's address — one XNOR gate per address bit, the bits ANDed into a match signal, 114 such comparators in parallel — and a match steers the store's data to the load through a mux instead of reading the cache. The comparison is the CAM primitive in flop form; Intel publishes no match structure, entry width or forwarding latency for Golden Cove. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank (the 48 KB L1 data cache)** — 6T cell arrays: a row decoder (an AND/NAND tree from the set-index bits) raises one wordline per set; the data reads out through bitlines into sense amplifiers, with a tag SRAM beside the data array and one comparator per way selecting the hit way through a mux. Three 256-bit loads per cycle means three read ports — duplicated arrays or multi-ported cells — each with its own decoder and sense amplifiers, and the 64-byte-per-cycle path to L2 is a second, narrower read port. The cache's banking, set associativity and port counts are not published. Standard construction.
- **Adder (the three load and two store AGUs)** — Address generation is addition: base + index × scale + displacement. The index × scale term is a barrel shifter — a mux tree shifting the index by 0–3 bits — and the sum is a full-adder chain with a carry-lookahead prefix network, one such adder per AGU, five adders in total for the three load and two store AGUs. The AGUs' adder widths, carry structure and the scheduler entries that feed them are not published. Standard construction.
- **FSM / controller (the AGU scheduler, fill buffer and superqueue credit accounting)** — Misses are tracked by credit counters: a small up/down counter per pool — a flip-flop register with a full-adder increment/decrement path, a zero-detect NOR tree (all-zero = full) and a full-detect compare — decremented when a miss is issued, incremented when the line fills. The 16-entry fill buffer is 16 slots of flop storage behind a 4-bit counter; the 48-entry superqueue a 6-bit counter. The AGU scheduler — a separate queue from the unified math scheduler — dispatches the address computations; its queue depth and select policy are not published. Standard construction.
- **CAM + counter (the data TLB and the page walkers)** — The 96-entry data TLB is a CAM: one CAM cell per stored tag bit, one matchline per entry (96 matchlines), a priority encoder selecting the hit entry and driving its physical page number out. Behind it, four page walkers are FSMs — state bits in D flip-flops, next-state and output logic as NAND/NOR networks — each sequencing a multi-level page-table walk, one walk per walker at a time. The DTLB's associativity, replacement policy and the walkers' state machines are not published. Standard construction.
- **Multiplier array (AMX — server Golden Cove only)** — The server variant's matrix engine is a multiplier array over tile registers: a grid of multiply-accumulate cells, each a small multiplier array feeding an adder tree that accumulates into a tile register — a flop array holding the partial sums — at 2,048 int8 operations per cycle. Intel publishes no tile geometry, array dimensions or accumulator structure for AMX on Golden Cove, so this decomposition names the primitive and its rate only. Standard construction.

Honesty: Intel publishes no gate-level structure for Golden Cove: Intel Architecture Day 2021 (via AnandTech and WikiChip Fuse) and Chips and Cheese's "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove" (December 2021) describe blocks, capacities and measured behaviour, and nothing below that. Every gate and transistor structure above is standard construction from the primer's bottom layer (NAND/NOR gates, D flip-flops, 6T SRAM cells, CAM cells, full adders) — the generic textbook form of each primitive, never a claim about Golden Cove's actual implementation. The only Golden Cove-specific facts in this section are the block and primitive parameters already recorded at Levels 2–3. Pointer-width and comparison-count figures are arithmetic from the published geometries (9-bit ROB pointers from 512 entries; 9-bit physical-register indices from the 280/332-entry files; 7-bit branch-order-buffer index from 128 entries; 8-bit load-queue and 7-bit store-queue pointers from 192/114 entries; 114 store-to-load comparators from the 114-entry store queue; 12-bit µop-cache index from 4K entries); no per-primitive gate or transistor counts are published, and this decomposition invents none. (New Level-4 fact record: unverifiable.)

- [unverifiable] **Intel Golden Cove** — Gate-level structure not published: no Intel source discloses Golden Cove's per-primitive gate/transistor implementation (BTB CAM cell form and per-level organisation, µop-cache/L1I/L1D array organisation below set/way geometry, decoder and clock-gating circuitry, direction-predictor table and ML sizing circuits, return-stack cell form, ROB storage-cell form and banking, scheduler wakeup/select circuitry, FMA multiplier-array geometry and adder-tree depth, bypass-network mux radix and forwarding-bus count, load/store queue lookup structure, AGU adder widths, AMX tile geometry). All gate-level detail in the Level-4 decomposition is textbook-standard construction, labelled as such. Sources: Intel Architecture Day 2021 (via AnandTech and WikiChip Fuse); Chips and Cheese, "Popping the Hood on Golden Cove", December 2021.

### Arm Neoverse V2

#### Down to the gates: The MOP-cache front end — 1,536-entry L0 MOP cache, 6-wide decode and the TAGE predictor

- **SRAM bank (the L0 MOP cache)** — 6T cells in rows: a row decoder — an AND/NAND tree from the index bits — raises one wordline per set; the 1,536 MOPs read out through bitlines into sense amplifiers, with a tag SRAM beside the data array and one comparator per way selecting the hit way through a 4:1 mux (2-bit way select by arithmetic). Skewed associativity means each of the four ways hashes the fetch address differently, so each way needs its own hash network — a small XOR tree over the address bits — feeding its own decoder and comparator; the hash functions themselves are not published. The 1,536-entry total is an 11-bit index by arithmetic (2^11 = 2,048 ≥ 1,536). The array's banking, port counts and read latency are not published. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 instruction cache)** — The same construction: 64 KB / 64-byte lines = 1,024 lines, 4-way → 256 sets → an 8-bit set index by arithmetic; a row decoder raises one wordline per set, bitlines feed sense amplifiers, a tag SRAM and one comparator per way feed a 4:1 way mux. Parity protection is one extra bit per stored word — an XOR parity tree on write, a parity check (XOR tree) on read, a mismatch raising a fault. The cache's banking, port counts and read latency are not published. Standard construction.
- **Counter (the MOP-cache NRU replacement state)** — One recency bit per MOP-cache entry, held in a D flip-flop: set on use, cleared in bulk when every bit in a set is set — the clearing is a NOR tree over the set's bits feeding the reset of all four way-bits at once. Victim selection is a priority encoder over the cleared bits, picking the first entry whose bit is clear. The bit's exact update rule and the victim-selection circuit are not published. Standard construction.
- **CAM (the fully associative L1 instruction TLB)** — One CAM cell per stored tag bit: a 6T SRAM cell holding the tag bit plus an XNOR/XOR compare gate against the search bit, every cell on a row wired-NOR onto one precharged matchline — a single mismatching bit pulls the matchline down, so the whole page number compares in one evaluation. A hit drives its physical page number out through a priority encoder (or, with one hit guaranteed, a plain OR of the matchlines' data). The TLB's entry count, replacement policy and match-process internals are not published. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank + counter (the TAGE direction predictor)** — Each tagged table is an SRAM bank of saturating counters: D flip-flops per counter bit, an increment/decrement unit (a tiny adder of full-adder cells), and saturation comparators (a few AND/OR gates) clamping the count at its rails. The prediction is a wire — the counter's most-significant bit, read straight off the storage bit. The tag is the comparator's match key: the stored tag is XNOR-compared against the history-derived tag, one gate per tag bit, ANDed into a hit signal; the longest-history table with a hit wins, selected by a priority mux. Table count, history lengths, counter widths and tag widths are not published, so no per-table transistor count is claimed. Standard construction.
- **CAM (the branch target buffer)** — A CAM in the same form as the TLB: one CAM cell per stored tag bit, one matchline per entry, a hit returning the target address through a priority encoder. The 10× growth over V1 is more entries — more matchlines — not a different cell. Entry count, tag width and associativity are not published. Standard construction.
- **Counter / stack (the return stack)** — A LIFO in flops: return addresses held in D flip-flop registers, a stack-pointer counter (flip-flops plus a full-adder incrementer/decrementer) selecting the top entry, and push/pop muxes steering the address in on a call and out on a return. The pushed value is the X30 link-register value plus its instruction-set state bit — one extra stored bit per entry. The stack's depth is not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the fetch queue)** — A circular queue in flops: entry storage in D flip-flop arrays (master–slave, ≈20–28 transistors per bit in a standard-cell library), write and read pointer counters (D flip-flops plus a full-adder incrementer), and full/empty comparators — XNOR gates per pointer bit feeding an AND tree. A mispredict flush is a pointer reset: the write pointer is set back to the read pointer, discarding the queued instructions in one cycle. Its depth is not published. Standard construction.
- **FSM / controller (the six decoders)** — Each decoder is combinational logic: field extraction as AND/OR planes (a PLA-style decode) or random logic, turning a fixed-length 4-byte AArch64 instruction into MOPs. Because the ISA is fixed-length, there is no variable-length length-decoding stage — the six decoders sit side by side, each fed one instruction word per cycle. The decoders' internal structure — field extraction, cracking tables, fused-instruction handling — is not published. Standard construction.
- **Mux (the supply-path select)** — A 2:1 mux tree: transmission-gate muxes (2 transistors per leg) choosing, per MOP bit, between the MOP-cache hit path and the L1I-plus-decoder refill path, steered by the MOP-cache hit signal. The mux radix and the MOP cache's way-selection logic are not published. Standard construction.
- **Register file (the rename map)** — Flop/latch storage behind read-port mux trees: one entry per architectural register holding the physical-register index of the most recent writer plus a valid bit, read out through a mux on a source lookup and written through a decoder on a destination rename. Eight-wide dispatch means eight source lookups per cycle — eight read-port mux trees per bit. The map's entry count, port counts and the exact elimination circuits are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the per-class issue queues)** — Circular queues in flops with pointer counters and full/empty comparators, as in the fetch queue — but with out-of-order exit: any entry can leave next, so each entry carries a ready bit and the select is a priority encoder over the ready bits (an AND/OR prefix tree computing the highest-priority ready request), one grant per execution class per cycle, latched into a grant register of D flip-flops for the dispatch cycle. The per-class queue depths and the issue arbitration policy are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the 320-entry reorder buffer)** — A circular queue in flops: 320 storage slots, each a D flip-flop/latch array holding one instruction's control bits, destination, result value once produced and status bits (the standard construction; the ROB's actual storage-cell form and banking are not published). Head and tail are 9-bit pointer counters by arithmetic (2^9 = 512 ≥ 320), each a D-flip-flop bank with a full-adder incrementer that advances by 0–8 per cycle as up to eight instructions are allocated or retired, and a wrap comparator — XNOR gates per pointer bit feeding an AND tree — that detects the count reaching 320 and wraps the pointer to zero. Full/empty is the same equality compare between head and tail. Standard construction.
- **Comparator (the skewed-associative way match)** — Four tag comparators in parallel, one per way: each stored tag XNOR-compared against the fetch address after that way's hash, one gate per tag bit, the bits ANDed into a per-way hit signal, and the four hit signals feeding a 4:1 way mux (a 2-bit select) that picks the matching way's MOPs. A miss — no way hits — steers the fetch to the L1I path. The tag width, the hash functions and the comparator circuit are not published. Standard construction.

Honesty: Arm publishes no gate-level structure for Neoverse V2: the Core Technical Reference Manual (doc 102375_0001_02_en, Issue 02) and the CMN-700 Technical Reference Manual (doc 102308_0307_08_en, Issue 08) describe blocks, capacities and behaviours, and nothing below that. Every gate and transistor structure above is standard construction from the primer's bottom layer (NAND/NOR gates, D flip-flops, 6T SRAM cells, CAM cells, full adders) — the generic textbook form of each primitive, never a claim about Neoverse V2's actual implementation. The only Neoverse V2-specific facts in this section are the block and primitive parameters already recorded at Levels 2–3. Index- and pointer-width figures are arithmetic from the published geometries (11-bit MOP-cache index from 1,536 entries; 8-bit L1I set index from 64 KB / 64-byte lines / 4 ways; 9-bit ROB pointers from 320 entries); no per-primitive gate or transistor counts are published, and this decomposition invents none. (New Level-4 fact record: unverifiable.)

#### Down to the gates: The platform fabric — CPU bridge, DSU-110 and the CMN-700 mesh with its distributed system cache

- **FIFO / queue + synchronizer (the asynchronous CPU bridge)** — The bridge is an asynchronous FIFO: entry storage in D flip-flop arrays, write and read pointer counters in their own clock domains, and the pointers crossed between domains through two-flop synchronizers — a pair of D flip-flops in series per pointer bit, the standard construction that lets a metastable first flop settle before the second samples it. Full/empty is computed on Gray-coded pointers (one bit changes per increment, so a synchronizer can never sample a half-updated pointer) compared with XNOR gates and an AND tree. The core's clock gate is the primer's integrated clock-gating cell: a latch plus an AND gate holding the core clock low while the bridge idles. FIFO depths and the synchronizer implementation are not published. Standard construction.
- **FSM / controller (the DSU-110 interface manager)** — A controller is a finite state machine: state bits in D flip-flops, next-state logic as a NAND/NOR network over the state bits and the incoming request signals, and output logic driving the power, debug, trace and memory-system interfaces. The DSU-110's internal structure — its state count, its interface queues, its power-policy logic — is not published, so this decomposition names only the generic FSM form; it does not invent a state diagram. Standard construction.
- **Crossbar (the mesh crosspoint, XP)** — A crossbar is a mux tree per output port: each of the XP's output ports (up to 4 mesh + up to 4 device, depending on position — 6 ports on an interior XP) carries one N:1 mux per flit bit, the muxes selecting among the input ports' flits. Each input port's flits arrive through the receive buffers below; the per-output arbitration is a priority encoder / round-robin FSM granting one input per output per cycle. The mesh coordinates (X,Y) are 4 bits each by arithmetic (12 ≤ 16). The XP's per-port arbitration policy and buffer depths are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the receive flit buffers)** — 2–4 flit buffers per port (default 3), each a small FIFO in flops: a flit register (D flip-flops, one per flit bit), a valid bit, and a write/read pointer pair. The credit round trip sets the floor — 2 buffers cover the one-cycle interconnect plus one-cycle RN-F/SN-F credit return latency — so the buffer count is the flow-control geometry made concrete. Per-channel buffer allocation beyond the count is not published. Standard construction.
- **Credit counter (the link-layer credits)** — A credit counter is an up/down counter: a flip-flop register holding the credit count, a full-adder increment/decrement path, a zero-detect NOR tree (all-zero = no credits left, stop sending) and a full-detect compare (count back at its maximum, 4 per channel by default). Sending a flit decrements; a returned credit increments. The no-stall condition — a transmitter able to send continuously — is the counter never hitting zero while flits are waiting, which is why the buffer count and the credit count are sized together. Standard construction.
- **Pipeline register (the credited slices — MCS / AMCS / DCS / CCS)** — A credited slice is a pipeline register plus a credit counter: the register is a D-flip-flop bank holding one flit for a cycle, and the counter returns the credit only when the flit moves on — the same up/down counter as the link-layer credits, one per slice. An asynchronous mesh credited slice adds the bridge's two-flop synchronizer on the pointer crossing, so a flit crosses clock domains inside the slice. Slice counts per link (0–4) are configuration, not published constants. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank (the HN-F system-level cache slice)** — 6T cells in rows: a row decoder — an AND/NAND tree from the set-index bits — raises one wordline per set; the data reads out through bitlines into sense amplifiers, with a tag SRAM beside the data array and one comparator per way selecting the hit way through a mux (12 or 16 ways by configuration, a 4-bit way select either way). The 1–3-cycle tag and data RAM latencies are pipeline-register stages between the decoder, the sense amplifiers and the output mux. The slice's banking, port counts and the SLC's replacement policy are not published. Standard construction.
- **CAM (the snoop filter tag RAM)** — A CAM in the primer's form: one CAM cell per stored tag bit — a 6T SRAM cell plus an XNOR/XOR compare gate — every cell on a row wired-NOR onto one precharged matchline, so a line's presence in an RN-F is decided in one evaluation. The matchline feeds a small encoder that returns the list of holding request nodes, so the snoop goes to exactly those nodes instead of a broadcast. The tag RAM's associativity, lookup latency and the RN-F tracking vector's width beyond the published parameters are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the Snoop Filter Eviction Queue)** — A FIFO in flops with pointer counters and full/empty comparators: each entry holds the address of a line being evicted from the filter, and the queue drains by forcing back-invalidations — a state machine that issues one invalidation per entry and pops the entry when the invalidation is acknowledged. Its depth is not published. Standard construction.
- **FSM / controller (the combined PoS/PoC)** — The ordering engine is a state machine over the Point-of-Coherency Queue: the POCQ is a FIFO in flops (16–128 entries, default 32 — a 5-bit pointer by arithmetic at the default, 7-bit at the 128-entry maximum), each entry holding one outstanding request's address and state; the PoS/PoC FSM is state bits in D flip-flops with next-state logic that serialises requests to the same line — a later request to a line with an outstanding request waits until the earlier one completes. The queue's entry format and the serialisation state machine's encoding are not published. Standard construction.
- **Comparator (the System Address Map decode)** — Address decode is range comparison: each SAM range is a pair of bound registers (D flip-flops) and the request address is compared against both bounds — a subtractor (full-adder chain) or a magnitude comparator (XOR per bit feeding an AND/OR tree) per bound — producing a hit when the address falls inside the range; a priority encoder over the range hits selects the owning home node. The SAM's table size, range count and lookup latency are not published. Standard construction.
- **Switch arbiter (the QoS regulator)** — The regulator is an arbiter: a priority encoder over the waiting requests, with the QoS class as the priority key — latency-sensitive classes win over bulk classes — plus a token-bucket or credit counter per class (an up/down counter refilled at a configured rate) that throttles a class once its budget is spent. The QoS levels, the regulation algorithm and the per-port arbitration policy are not published. Standard construction.
- **Counter (the CMN-700 PMU)** — A performance counter is a wide up-counter: D flip-flops per bit with a full-adder incrementer, an enable AND gate driven by the event signal, and an overflow flag — a comparator against all-ones. The event list and counter widths are not published. Standard construction.
- **Comparator + FSM (MESI and the exclusive monitor at the core boundary)** — Each L1 line carries a 2-bit MESI state in flops; on every snoop the stored state is compared against the snoop's requirements — a few AND/OR gates per line — and the state machine transitions the line (Modified → Invalid on a remote write, and so on). The exclusive monitor is a 2-state FSM — a single state bit in a D flip-flop, open or exclusive — set by a Load-Exclusive, cleared by a Store-Exclusive or CLREX, with the state bit gating whether the store is allowed to commit. The MESI state encoding and the monitor's transition table beyond the two named states are not published. Standard construction.

Honesty: Arm publishes no gate-level structure for the CMN-700 mesh or the DSU-110 either: the CMN-700 TRM describes XP port counts, credit rules, slice kinds, SLC and snoop-filter sizing and the PoS/PoC's role, and nothing below that. Every gate and transistor structure above is standard construction from the primer's bottom layer — the generic textbook form of each primitive, never a claim about Neoverse V2's or the CMN-700's actual implementation. The only fabric-specific facts in this section are the block and primitive parameters already recorded at Levels 2–3. Coordinate and pointer widths are arithmetic from the published geometries (4-bit (X,Y) coordinates from the 12 × 12 grid; 5-bit POCQ pointer from the 32-entry default, 7-bit at the 128-entry maximum); no per-primitive gate or transistor counts are published, and this decomposition invents none. (New Level-4 fact record: unverifiable.)

### Arm Cortex-X4

#### Down to the gates: The 10-wide decode front end — eight decoders, no MOP cache

- **SRAM bank (the L1 instruction cache)** — 6T cells in rows: 64 KB is 524,288 bits — 524,288 6T cells, ≈3.1 million transistors in the data array alone (arithmetic from the published geometry) — arranged as 256 sets of 4 ways × 64-byte lines, so an 8-bit set index by arithmetic drives a row decoder — an AND/NAND tree from the index bits — that raises one wordline per set; the selected line reads out through bitlines into sense amplifiers (a cross-coupled inverter pair with a precharge and an enable), with a tag SRAM beside the data array and one comparator per way (XNOR per tag bit, ANDed into a hit signal) selecting the hit way through a 4:1 mux (a 2-bit way select). Optional ECC is one extra bit per stored word — an XOR parity/ECC tree on write, a syndrome check on read. The replacement policy, banking, port counts and read latency are not published. Standard construction.
- **CAM (the fully associative L1 instruction TLB)** — One CAM cell per stored tag bit: a 6T SRAM cell holding the tag bit plus an XNOR/XOR compare gate against the search bit, every cell on a row wired-NOR onto one precharged matchline — a single mismatching bit pulls the matchline down, so the whole page number compares in one evaluation. A hit drives its physical page number out through a priority encoder (or, with one hit guaranteed, a plain OR of the matchlines' data); a valid bit per entry (a D flip-flop) gates stale entries out of the match. The TLB's entry count, replacement policy and match-process internals are not published. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank + counter (the dynamic branch predictor)** — Each prediction table is an SRAM bank of saturating counters: D flip-flops per counter bit, an increment/decrement unit (a tiny adder of full-adder cells), and saturation comparators (a few AND/OR gates) clamping the count at its rails; the prediction is a wire — the counter's most-significant bit, read straight off the storage bit. The target store is an associative lookup — a CAM in the TLB's form, one CAM cell per tag bit with a matchline per entry, returning the stored target through a priority encoder. Table count, history lengths, counter widths, tag widths and BTB entry count are not published, so no per-table transistor count is claimed. Standard construction.
- **FSM / controller (the eight decoders)** — Each decoder is combinational logic: field extraction as AND/OR planes (a PLA-style decode) or random logic, turning a fixed-length 4-byte AArch64 instruction into MOPs, with cracking handled by small lookup tables — a ROM/PLA AND-OR plane mapping an opcode pattern to a two-MOP sequence — and fused-instruction detection as a comparator over the opcode bits. Because the ISA is fixed-length, there is no variable-length length-decoding stage — the eight decoders sit side by side, each fed one instruction word per cycle, their outputs latched into pipeline registers. The decoders' internal structure — field extraction, cracking tables, fused-instruction handling — is not published. Standard construction.
- **Register file (the rename map)** — Flop/latch storage behind read-port mux trees: one entry per architectural register holding the physical-register index of the most recent writer plus a valid bit, read out through a mux on a source lookup and written through a decoder on a destination rename. Ten-wide dispatch means ten source lookups per cycle — ten read-port mux trees per bit, each a transmission-gate mux (2 transistors per leg) or a NAND/NOR select tree. The map's entry count, port counts and the free-list structure are not published. Standard construction.
- **Counter / FIFO (the physical-register free list)** — A FIFO in flops: free physical-register numbers held in D-flip-flop registers, a write pointer and a read pointer (each a D-flip-flop bank with a full-adder incrementer), and full/empty comparators — XNOR gates per pointer bit feeding an AND tree. A rename pops one number per destination; a retire pushes one back. Its depth is not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the per-class issue queues)** — Circular queues in flops with pointer counters and full/empty comparators — but with out-of-order exit: any entry can leave next, so each entry carries a ready bit and the select is a priority encoder over the ready bits (an AND/OR prefix tree computing the highest-priority ready request), one grant per execution class per cycle, latched into a grant register of D flip-flops for the dispatch cycle. The per-class queue names beyond the execution classes, the depths and the issue arbitration policy are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the 384-entry reorder buffer)** — A circular queue in flops: 384 storage slots, each a D-flip-flop/latch array holding one instruction's control bits, destination, result value once produced and status bits (the standard construction; the ROB's actual storage-cell form and banking are not published). Head and tail are 9-bit pointer counters by arithmetic (2^9 = 512 ≥ 384), each a D-flip-flop bank with a full-adder incrementer that advances by 0–10 per cycle as up to ten MOPs are allocated or retired, and a wrap comparator — XNOR gates per pointer bit feeding an AND tree — that detects the count reaching 384 and wraps the pointer to zero. Full/empty is the same equality compare between head and tail. The retire-head ready check is an AND over the head entry's ready bit and its exception flags. Standard construction.
- **Mux (the dispatch steering network)** — A 10-wide steering network: per MOP, a mux tree selecting its class's issue queue — transmission-gate muxes (2 transistors per input leg) or NAND/NOR select trees, one select per MOP per cycle, driven by the class bits decoded from the MOP. Ten parallel muxes, one per dispatch slot, each fanning out to the per-class queues. The mux radix and the per-cycle steering arbitration are not published. Standard construction.
- **Pipeline register (the 10-stage front end)** — Each stage boundary is a bank of master–slave D flip-flops (≈20–28 transistors per bit in a standard-cell library) holding the instruction and its MOPs for a cycle; a mispredict flush is a mux in front of each flip-flop (a 2:1 transmission-gate mux, ≈ +6–8 transistors per bit) selecting a bubble instead of the incoming instruction, or a synchronous clear of the register. The register widths and the flush/bypass wiring are not published. Standard construction.
- **The missing primitive (the deleted MOP cache)** — Zero entries means zero gates: no tag array, no way-select mux, no hit/miss select, no data array — nothing to decompose. The deletion is the primitive-level reason the front end is uniform, and it is also why the decoder and rename primitives work harder — they re-decode every iteration of every hot loop.

Honesty: Arm publishes no gate-level structure for Cortex-X4: the Core Technical Reference Manual (doc 102484_0003_06_en, Issue 06) describes blocks, capacities and behaviours, and nothing below that. Every gate and transistor structure above is standard construction from the primer's bottom layer (NAND/NOR gates, D flip-flops, 6T SRAM cells, CAM cells, full adders) — the generic textbook form of each primitive, never a claim about Cortex-X4's actual implementation. The only X4-specific facts in this section are the block and primitive parameters already recorded at Levels 2–3. Index- and pointer-width figures are arithmetic from the published geometries (8-bit L1I set index from 64 KB / 64-byte lines / 4 ways; 9-bit ROB pointers from 384 entries; ≈3.1 million data-array transistors from 64 KB × 8 bits × 6 transistors); no per-primitive gate or transistor counts are published, and this decomposition invents none. (New Level-4 fact record: unverifiable.)

#### Down to the gates: The 21-port back end — eight integer ALUs and the 384-entry window

- **FIFO / queue + CAM (the per-class issue queues and their wakeup)** — The same construction as the front-end issue queues, with the wakeup made explicit: every queued entry carries its source operand tags in D flip-flops, and each cycle every entry's tags are compared against the broadcast result tags — a CAM-style compare, one XNOR gate per tag bit per source, ANDed into a per-source ready signal — so a result wakes all its consumers in one cycle. The select is a priority encoder over the ready bits (an AND/OR prefix tree), one grant per port per cycle. The queue depths, the number of wakeup comparators per entry and the arbitration policy are not published. Standard construction.
- **Register file (the integer physical register file)** — Multi-ported storage: each bit stored in a D flip-flop (or, in denser implementations, an 8T/10T SRAM cell with extra access transistors per port), read out through one mux tree per read port per bit and written through one decoder per write port — a multi-ported file's transistor count grows with ports, which is why the port count is the file's governing cost. Its entry count and port counts are not published by Arm for X4. Standard construction.
- **Adder tree (the six simple integer ALUs)** — A 64-bit carry-lookahead adder: per-bit generate (AND) and propagate (XOR) gates, a carry tree — an AND/OR prefix network computing all 64 carries in parallel instead of rippling them — and per-bit sum XORs; each full adder is two half adders, an XOR/AND/OR gate set (≈28 transistors in static CMOS). The logic unit is a mux tree selecting AND/OR/XOR/NOT per bit, the NOT from an inverter, the selection from the opcode bits through NAND/NOR decode. The comparator for flag-setting operations is an XOR tree over the result bits feeding an OR reduction (zero detect) plus the sign bit straight off the top sum bit. Single-cycle latency is published; the adder's carry-tree depth and the logic unit's mux radix are not. Standard construction.
- **Multiplier array + adder tree (the two MAC units)** — A 64-bit multiplier: a partial-product AND plane (one AND gate per multiplicand/multiplier bit pair), a Wallace/Dadda tree of full adders — carry-save adders reducing the partial-product rows three-to-two at each level — then a final carry-propagate adder (the carry-lookahead form above), and the MAC's accumulator register (a bank of D flip-flops) feeding the adder's second input so the product accumulates in place. The upgrade from X3's single MAC to two MACs is published; the multiplier's array geometry and pipeline depth are not. Standard construction.
- **FSM / controller + subtractor (the divide unit)** — An iterative divider: a state machine — state bits in D flip-flops, next-state logic as a NAND/NOR network — driving a subtractor (an adder with the subtrahend inverted and the carry-in set, XOR gates for the inversion plus the carry-lookahead adder) and a quotient register (D flip-flops) that shifts one result bit per iteration. The divider's iteration count and radix are not published. Standard construction.
- **Comparator + adder (the three branch units)** — Each branch unit is a condition comparator — an XOR per bit of the two operands feeding an OR reduction, or a subtractor-based compare reusing the adder form — plus a target adder (PC + offset), a 64-bit carry-lookahead adder in the ALU's form. The branch prediction resolution is a mux selecting the predicted target or the computed one. Three units is published; the comparator width beyond the 64-bit datapath and the target adder's carry structure are not. Standard construction.
- **Adder (the four address-generation units)** — Each AGU is a base-plus-offset adder: a 64-bit carry-lookahead adder (generate/propagate AND/XOR gates, carry prefix tree, sum XORs), with a 3:2 compressor (a row of full adders) ahead of it when base, index and offset must be summed together. Four AGUs sustain up to four memory operations per cycle. The adder width beyond the 64-bit address and the AGU-to-cache port mapping are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue (the load and store queues)** — Queues in flops: each entry holds the address, data and state bits in D flip-flops; store-to-load forwarding is a set of address comparators — one XOR-per-bit compare plus an AND tree per store entry against the load address, with an age comparator (a magnitude comparator over the entry sequence numbers) picking the youngest matching store — and the outstanding-access tracking is an up/down counter (flip-flops plus a full-adder incrementer/decrementer) with a full-detect compare. The queue depths, the forwarding-comparator counts and the outstanding-access limit are not published. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank (the L1 data cache)** — The same construction as the L1I: 64 KB is 524,288 bits — 524,288 6T cells, ≈3.1 million transistors in the data array (arithmetic) — 256 sets of 4 ways × 64-byte lines, an 8-bit set index, a row decoder, bitlines into sense amplifiers, a tag SRAM with one comparator per way and a 4:1 way mux. Always-on ECC is a SECDED code: an XOR tree computing check bits on write, a syndrome XOR tree on read, a single-bit error corrected by an XOR of the syndrome into the data bit, a double-bit error flagged. RRIP replacement is a 2-bit re-reference prediction value per line — two D flip-flops per line, incremented on a hit, reset on insertion — with a victim-selection priority encoder over the lines whose value is at its maximum. Banking, port counts beyond the published interface widths and read latency are not published. Standard construction.
- **CAM (the fully associative L1 data TLB)** — One CAM cell per stored tag bit in the TLB's form: a 6T SRAM cell plus an XNOR/XOR compare gate, every cell on a row wired-NOR onto one precharged matchline, a hit driving the physical page out through a priority encoder. The 2 MB and 512 MB block sizes mean fewer tag bits are compared for those entries — the matchline is shorter, not a different cell. Its entry count, replacement policy and the match process internals are not published. Standard construction.
- **Multiplier array + adder tree + barrel shifter (the four 128-bit vector pipes)** — Each 128-bit pipe is a multiplier array (a partial-product AND plane plus a Wallace/Dadda full-adder tree, in 128-bit form) plus an adder tree (a 128-bit carry-lookahead adder) plus a barrel shifter — log₂(128) = 7 stages of 2:1 transmission-gate muxes per bit, each stage shifting by 1, 2, 4, … 64 positions, so any shift amount completes in one cycle — plus a result-select mux network (one mux tree per output bit) choosing among the multiplier, adder, shifter and logic results. Floating-point divide and square root, now pipelined, are iterative FSMs in the divider's form — a state machine driving a subtractor and a quotient register — unrolled into pipeline stages. The per-pipe lane split beyond the 128-bit width, the multiplier geometry and the divide/sqrt iteration counts are not published. Standard construction.
- **SRAM bank (the private L2 cache)** — 6T cells in rows, in four banks: 512 KB / 1 MB / 2 MB is 4,194,304 / 8,388,608 / 16,777,216 bits — that many 6T cells (arithmetic), split into four bank sub-arrays, each with its own row decoder, bitlines, sense amplifiers and tag SRAM; 8-way associativity means eight tag comparators per bank and an 8:1 way mux (a 3-bit select); 64-byte lines give 1,024 / 2,048 / 4,096 sets — a 10/11/12-bit set index by arithmetic. The CHI interface to the DSU-120 is 256 bits wide — 256 data wires latched through a bank of D flip-flops per transfer. Dynamic biased replacement is per-set counters (D flip-flops with a full-adder incrementer) plus a bias table (a small SRAM bank) steering the victim choice. Per-bank port counts, tag/data RAM latencies and the replacement policy's bias table are not published. Standard construction.
- **FIFO / queue + synchronizer (the asynchronous CPU bridge)** — An asynchronous FIFO: entry storage in D-flip-flop arrays, write and read pointer counters in their own clock domains, and the pointers crossed between domains through two-flop synchronizers — a pair of D flip-flops in series per pointer bit, the standard construction that lets a metastable first flop settle before the second samples it. Full/empty is computed on Gray-coded pointers (one bit changes per increment, so a synchronizer can never sample a half-updated pointer) compared with XNOR gates and an AND tree; Gray conversion is an XOR per pointer bit. Its FIFO depths and synchronizer implementation are not published. Standard construction.
- **FSM / controller + counter (the temporal prefetcher)** — A controller watching the load stream: an FSM (state bits in D flip-flops, next-state logic as a NAND/NOR network) plus a small table of past addresses and strides — an SRAM bank of counters, each entry a D-flip-flop register holding an address and a stride adder (a full-adder chain) computing the next prefetch address, with an address comparator (XNOR per bit, ANDed) detecting a repeating pattern and triggering the prefetch issue. Its table size, history depth and prefetch distance are not published. Standard construction.

Honesty: Arm publishes no gate-level structure for Cortex-X4: the Core Technical Reference Manual (doc 102484_0003_06_en, Issue 06) describes blocks, capacities and behaviours, and nothing below that. Every gate and transistor structure above is standard construction from the primer's bottom layer — the generic textbook form of each primitive, never a claim about Cortex-X4's actual implementation. The only X4-specific facts in this section are the block and primitive parameters already recorded at Levels 2–3. Index- and pointer-width figures are arithmetic from the published geometries (8-bit L1D set index from 64 KB / 64-byte lines / 4 ways; 10/11/12-bit L2 set indices from 512 KB / 1 MB / 2 MB at 8 ways; 7-stage barrel shifter from the 128-bit width; ≈3.1 million L1D data-array transistors and 4/8/16 million L2 bits from the published capacities); no per-primitive gate or transistor counts are published, and this decomposition invents none.
